Reference

Troubleshooting

Known traps and how to avoid them.

Hard-won lessons. Every entry cost a rebuild, a wrong number on a screen, or a shipped defect.

Read the reading traps (§1–§5) before binding an app to any service you did not publish yourself, and the publishing traps (§6) before running /publish.

How to read an entry

Marker Meaning
[core: fixed] Was a defect in strata/packages. Fixed — do not apply the old workaround. Kept because the symptom looks identical when it comes from elsewhere.
[core: open] A current limitation. The workaround is live.
[data] External service behaviour. Will never be "fixed" — guard against it.
[app] A mistake an app made. Structural: easy to repeat.
[browser] Platform, CSS or runtime behaviour.

Where two entries disagree, the newer wins. Three rules that govern the rest

A trap is a property of a service, not a law of nature. Test, never inherit. The pipeline build was burned by zero-padded identifiers (PLINEID as String(4)); the watershed build checked the same trap against its HUC codes and found all 8,194 rows full-width strings. Inheriting the guard is harmless; inheriting the conclusion would have been wrong.

One probe is not enough to declare an endpoint dead. A basins layer returned three consecutive 500s during research and three consecutive {count:21} an hour later. Intermittent is not broken — sample more than once, and write suites that pass on either outcome.

Assert the rule, not the victims. When one cascade bug hit a splash overlay and then a radius control, the fix was not a second per-component guard — it was one global rule plus an assertion on that rule. A guard that enumerates the victims found so far keeps finding the next one by hand.

1 · Identity — which row is this?

A field named "Unique Identifier" is NOT unique — and it IS unique one scope down, which is the half that rescues the join. [data] (2026-09-07, facilities & space utilisation) The federal real-property inventory publishes Real_Property_Unique_Identifier on every row, with zero nulls and zero blanks, which is exactly what a trustworthy key looks like. Across the 13,001 California buildings it carries 12,806 distinct values: 193 identifiers repeat over 388 rows, and the three rows sharing 90027 are a warehouse in Alameda reported by Energy, a restroom in Riverside reported by Interior, and an "All Other" in Alameda reported by Transportation. De-duplicating on it merges unrelated buildings; joining on it across services attaches the wrong vacancy figure to the wrong building — silently, because the field is fully populated. But scoped to a single Reporting_Agency it has zero duplicates (707 GSA buildings, 707 distinct ids), and that is what makes a cross-register join safe here at all. Guard: probe distinctness before treating any field as a key, whatever it is called, and when it fails, look for the scope in which it holds rather than abandoning it — a published key is often unique inside the publishing unit and repeats across units. Key the application itself on the OID; use the natural key only inside its proven scope. Belongs beside the OID entries above: same failure, different field, and a fuller name is not more trustworthy.

A layer document can report objectIdFieldName: null while carrying an OID-typed field — and the ids endpoint will name it for you. [data] @@STAMP@@ California HCD's TableA2 publishes objectIdFieldName: null on its layer document while its field list plainly contains OBJECTID of type esriFieldTypeOID. The catalogued advice for a null OID is bind read-only, and that would have been the wrong call here: a returnIdsOnly=true query answers objectIdFieldName: 'OBJECTID' alongside its 78,381 ids, which is an authoritative read rather than an inference from the field list. The same call also reveals the true unbounded population, because maxRecordCount governs feature paging and not the ids endpoint. Guard: when the document reports null, ask the ids endpoint before concluding there is no stable id — and take the name from that response, never from the field list, because a service can carry a column literally named OBJECTID that is not the object id.

objectIdField can be ABSENT from the response, not merely null — and a === null guard passes for the wrong reason. [data] (2026-09-02, site selection - the swing slate) Census TIGERweb's tract layer is recorded in more than one recipe as reporting objectIdField: null. It does not: the key is not present in the layer document at all, and neither is objectIdFieldName. Python's d.get('objectIdField') returns None and reads as null, which is how the wrong shape got written down; JavaScript's meta.objectIdField === null is then false for an undefined, so a build gate written from that record fails on a layer that is behaving exactly as documented. The conclusion is unchanged — no declared object id, bind read-only, key on a natural identifier such as GEOID — but the test is == null, covering absent and null together. Guard: assert "there is no usable object id" (objectIdField == null && objectIdFieldName == null), never === null; and when transcribing a probe from one language into a guard in another, re-run the guard against the live service rather than against the note.

A service's objectIdField can differ between two reads of the same URL — and between releases of the same dataset. [data] (2026-08-30, resilience disclosure pack) The FEMA NRI census-tract layer document reported "objectIdField": null on 2026-08-18 and 'objectIdField': 'OBJECTID' on 2026-08-30 — same URL, twelve days apart, no reissue, serverGen unchanged at 187760764 — while returnIdsOnly reported objectIdFieldName='OBJECTID' on both reads. Meanwhile a sibling release from the same publisher (NRI_CensusTracts_v117, the October 2020 edition) reports objectIdField: 'FID' and carries no OBJECTID column at all (has FID: True | has OBJECTID: False), so count(OBJECTID) returns 'Invalid field: OBJECTID' parameter is invalid on that one release and succeeds on the other three. Guard: the OID is a per-read, per-release fact, not a dataset fact. Read it from returnIdsOnly on every build — that channel was right on both dates while the layer document disagreed with itself — and re-read it whenever the bound release changes. This strengthens rather than replaces the house rule: never assume the OID on a service you did not publish; here the layer document was not merely wrong once, it was inconsistent with itself over time.

A join across two census-tract vintages SUCCEEDS, and the partial case is far worse than the empty one. [data] (2026-08-24, health equity overlay) This file already says a join that silently returns nothing looks exactly like a data gap. Sharpen it: the join that returns most of it does not look like anything at all. A GEOID join from a 2010-vintage outcome file (7,516 California tracts) to a 2020-vintage vulnerability index (9,109 tracts) returns 6,434 rows — 74 % of the state's population — with no error, no warning, and a plausible statewide map. 06001402800 became 06001402801 and 06001402802 in a boundary split; a quarter of California silently drops out and reads as no data. The empty join announces itself; the 74 % join ships. Guard: two tract layers that disagree on vintage must not be joined at all — pin the whole application to one vintage, and assert the chain (subset, count and the named exceptions) in a suite. That app asserts usaleep 7,516 ⊆ ces4 8,035 ⊆ tracts 8,057 ∪ {06037930401} and aborts the build on any other orphan. Generalises to every US tract, block-group and ZCTA binding, not just health.

The OID is whatever the service says it is. [data] objectIdField is guaranteed to be OBJECTID only on a layer you published to Strata Serve (§6). On a pipeline centerline the OID is FID, and a different column is literally named OBJECTID — FID 4 is OBJECTID 7. On three SafeGraph slices it is OBJECTID_1, and both columns exist. Selecting, highlighting or joining on the assumed field addresses the wrong rows with no error. Guard: read objectIdFieldName from the layer metadata; assert it in the suite.

Some layers report no OID although one exists. [data] Three geohazard layers returned objectIdField: null while carrying a usable OID column. Guard: bind read-only; never build an edit or selection key on it.

A whole ESTATE can omit objectIdFieldName, and the real OID differs per layer. [data] (2026-08-23, access to care) Across the seven layers one app bound — HCAI, HRSA, CDC PLACES and Cal OES — not one published objectIdFieldName. The real OID, read from the esriFieldTypeOID field, was ObjectId (camelCase) on the facility and health-centre layers, FID on MSSA2024_Tract, on the PCSA shortage-area layer and on HRSA's HPSA primary-care boundaries, and OBJECTID on the CDC and Cal OES tract layers. The PCSA layer carries a different column literally named OBJECTID beside its FID OID — the §1 trap above, alive in a health estate. A recipe that opens "OBJECTID is the OID throughout" is stating a hope. Guard: derive the OID from the field list by type, per layer, and assert each one; never inherit a per-estate claim.

The OID name can differ between SIBLING LAYERS INSIDE ONE SERVICE. [data] (2026-08-24, shortage-area explorer) On one federal MapServer publishing three disciplines of the same register, layers /10 and /2 report OBJECTID from esriFieldTypeOID and layer /6 reports lower-case objectid. A guard that string-matches the name — "OBJECTID", case-sensitively, or a per-service constant read once — binds correctly on two of the three siblings and silently mis-selects on the third. Guard: derive the OID from the field type, per layer, never per service, and never by name. Related: the estate-wide entry above; this one is narrower and easier to miss, because two of three layers agree.

Identifiers are strings; only magnitudes are numbers. [app] A zero-padded String(4) line id coerced to a number makes every row of an operator's upload miss its line — and report a cheerful "0 matched". Guard: refuse to coerce any cell carrying a leading zero.

Unidentified rows need unique keys. [app] Collapsing every unbranded outlet onto one empty brand key rolls them up as a single phantom chain, inventing a major competitor out of missing data.

A layer can OMIT objectIdFieldName rather than report it null — and a .get() probe cannot tell. [data] (2026-08-20, branch & ATM network operations) TIGERweb's tract layer does not carry the key at all, while returnIdsOnly names OBJECTID and an OBJECTID column exists on the layer. A probe written as j.get("objectIdFieldName") — the obvious idiom — returns None for an absent key exactly as it does for a null value, so a recipe recorded "objectIdFieldName": null and a suite asserting === null then went red against a service that had not changed. Guard: assert the finding (== null, which covers both) and, where the distinction matters, test key presence separately. Record which one you measured, because the two are different statements about the service.

Confirmed a second time, on a different layer of the same service, and it moved in between: (2026-08-20, fraud / AML geo-dashboard) TIGERweb's 2020 ZCTA layer reported objectIdField: null on 18 August and omits the key entirely on 20 August, while returnIdsOnly answered OBJECTID throughout and an esriFieldTypeOID column was in the field list the whole time. So this is not a stable property of a layer to be recorded once — the shape of the absence changes under you. Assert == null (which covers both) and test key presence separately, so the next shape-change is visible rather than silently absorbed by a ?? "OBJECTID".

objectIdFieldName can be null in the LAYER DOCUMENT and present in the /query response. [data] (2026-08-23, property peril report) Across all nine services one app bound — two AGOL hosted layers, three ArcGIS Server MapServers and four more — objectIdFieldName was null in every layer document, while the /query response named it correctly on all of them (and named it OBJECTID_1, beside a Double decoy called OBJECTID, on one). Reading the layer document alone concludes there is no OID and binds the wrong column or none at all. Guard: read objectIdFieldName from a /query response, per layer, every time; fall back to the field whose type is esriFieldTypeOID in the field list, and never to the name OBJECTID.

outFields is matched case-insensitively; the response keys are not. [data] (2026-08-24, shortage-area explorer) One HRSA service carries OBJECTID on two sibling layers and lower-case objectid on the third — and the third has no column named OBJECTID at all. Requesting outFields=OBJECTID against it nonetheless succeeds, because the server matches field names without regard to case. The divergence surfaces one step later: the returned attribute keys carry the layer's casing, so row.OBJECTID is undefined on exactly one layer of three while every request looks healthy. A field-name guard that is stricter than the server also mis-fires here, rejecting a request that works. Guard: validate requested names case-insensitively (match the server), but read every value back through the OID resolved from esriFieldTypeOID on that layer. Assert both: that the layer has no column literally named OBJECTID, and that the response keys carry the casing you resolved.

Two publishers name the same region differently, on the same key. [data] (2026-08-24, housing & homelessness services) Continuum CA-505 is Contra Costa County CoC in HUD's boundary layer and Richmond/Contra Costa County CoC in HUD's own Point-in-Time workbook and in a sibling boundary service. Nothing is wrong with either; they are different registers of the same publisher. An app that keys on the name loses rows, and an app that prints whichever name the last-refreshed lane supplied changes its own labels between builds. Guard: join on the code, always; nominate one source for the label and say in the UI which one it is; never key on a name. Where one population has no row in the nominated source — here, the two Continuums with a boundary and no count — name the fallback explicitly rather than letting it vary.

Two id systems side by side on the SAME row, and the familiar one returns a clean zero. [data] (2026-08-26, hospital network planning) HCAI's licence register carries both OSHPD_ID (a 9-digit licence id) and PERM_ID (a 5-digit building-permit id) on every row, and they are equal on 0 of 458 hospitals. Every other lane in that substrate keys on OSHPD_ID, so it is the id a builder reaches for first -- and joining the seismic layer on it resolves 0 of 43, which reads as "the seismic layer doesn't cover us" rather than "you used the wrong key". The right key, PERM_ID, resolves 40 of 43. This is the sibling of the cross-type PERM_ID entry above: that one is about casting, this one is about choosing. Guard: when a join returns zero, prove the key before you doubt the source -- join both ways on a sample and print both counts. Assert that the wrong key keeps failing, so a later refactor cannot quietly switch back to the familiar one; this build's suite asserts PERM_ID -> 40 and OSHPD_ID -> 0 side by side.

Second occurrence — a layer can declare objectIdField: null and still publish a working OBJECTID, and the neighbouring county is why you may still not infer it. [data] (2026-09-07, valuation & taxation base map; first recorded 2026-09-07, development pipeline tracker) Los Angeles County's parcel service — 2,432,918 rows — reports no object-id field on its layer document while carrying an esriFieldTypeOID column literally named OBJECTID, and where OBJECTID=1 answers {"count":1}. So far this is the catalogued shape. What this build adds is the reason the obvious shortcut is still wrong: a neighbouring California county's parcel layer keys on FID and carries a decoy column also called OBJECTID beside it. So "the document is silent, therefore use the column named OBJECTID" is a rule that works on one service and selects the wrong feature on the next — and it fails silently, because a wrong id highlights a real parcel. Guard: bind the id explicitly and read-only, per layer, from a probe of that layer — never infer it from the field list, and never inherit it from a sibling service in the same vertical. Where the ids endpoint will name it, take the name from there (the entry at the head of this section); where it will not, record the chosen column in the app's own map spec so a reviewer can see which id was bound and why.

Two feeds of one state spell its counties in different cases — and a baseline taken before the reference layer loaded keeps the other spelling. [data] [app] (2026-10-21, watch wall build) Cal OES's power-outage layers name counties in UPPER CASE (ORANGE, in both the incident points and the by-county totals), while the state's own county boundary layer and the national wildfire feed use Orange. The app joined the outage totals to the boundary names case-folded — but its shift baseline was captured on the first outage read, which landed before the 58 county polygons had loaded, so the baseline kept ORANGE and every later read said Orange. One minute into a live shift the by-county reading said "17 of 58 counties changed — outages changed" while statewide customers out read ±0. Only a screenshot caught it: every suite was green, because each fixture loaded counties first. Guard: compare join keys case-folded at the comparison, not only when the reference happens to be loaded; and never let the order in which feeds answer decide what a stored baseline contains — assert the mixed-case baseline against a later proper-case read in the suite. (2026-10-21, lifeline board build) Confirmed on a second pair. Joining the same Cal OES outage incidents to the California Geoportal county layer by name gave 0 of 58 exact matches and 58 of 58 once case-folded. The by-county layer also spells a field Impacted_Cutomers_Not_Planned (sic). Bind it literally, and never "correct" a field name in a query.

One evacuation zone, two id forms, two row sets. [data] (2026-10-21, incident situation map build) Cal OES's statewide evacuation aggregation and CAL FIRE's statewide evacuation-status view describe the same zones, but key them differently: the aggregation carries PVL-E042, the status view US-CA-XTU-PVL-E042. Neither id is a prefix-free match for the other, and the join that works is on the last segment of the status view's url field, not on any id column. And the two layers are not the same population: each carries zones the other lacks, so a join in either direction silently drops rows. Guard: normalise both sides to the shared zone code before joining, assert the normalisation on a known pair in the suite, and report the unmatched rows on both sides as their own count ("in the status view only", "in the aggregation only") rather than letting the join discard them.

One water system, several polygons: counting polygons double-counts systems. [data] (2026-10-21, lifeline board build) - Symptom: the State Water Board's drinking-water System Area Boundary Layer, queried for the service areas that intersect today's outage areas, returned 16 polygons for 14 water systems. The same WATER_SYSTEM_NUMBER appeared under two OBJECTID_1 values (one system, two disjoint service areas). A count of features said 16 systems were exposed. Every hospital, population figure or cascade item chained from those rows was duplicated. - Cause: the layer is keyed by polygon, not by system. - Guard: group by the publisher's system identifier (WATER_SYSTEM_NUMBER) before counting or chaining. Print "n systems (m service-area polygons)" when the two differ, and assert the grouping in the live suite on a known multi-polygon system.

The same layer's OID is OBJECTID_1, beside an ordinary Integer column named OBJECTID (8890801 vs 133 on one row; see the head of this section). Its statistics aliases come back upper-cased ("n" → "N"). It also carries contact fields (AC_PHONE_NUMBER, AC_EMAIL) that an app should never request.

2 · Counting and paging — is this all of it?

A row is not a record of the thing you are counting, and a longitudinal register will not tell you so. [data] @@STAMP@@ California's statutory housing return publishes 464,059 rows that are 313,397 distinct projects: a project entitled in 2019 is reported again in the 2020 and 2021 filings, so 32.5 % of all rows are a re-filing of something already counted. Nothing in the response says this. The composite key UniqueID + YEAR is unique on every row and UniqueID alone is not, and the only way to see it is to ask for both distinct counts and compare them to the row count. De-duplicating to each project's first reporting year moved the headline ninetieth percentile by 191 days (1,425 → 1,234), moved the over-two-years share from 17.1 % to 15.0 %, and cut the jurisdictions carrying a hundred or more measurable projects from 83 to 62. Guard: before reporting any count from a periodic return, ask for the distinct count of its natural key and of that key plus the period; if the second equals the row count and the first does not, the row is a filing rather than a thing. Print the grain beside every figure. A second sting here: the only reliable project key in this record is an identity field (its values are the jurisdiction name concatenated with a street address), so the de-duplication has to happen inside the build script and the key must never reach an artifact.

"Has data" and "no data" are not enough states, and the biggest publishers are often the ones that need the third. [data] @@STAMP@@ Of 521 filing jurisdictions in the California return, 165 filed and support no timing read at all — San Jose filed 3,248 rows with 88 carrying an entitlement date and not one carrying both, and Sacramento filed 11,358 rows and reached zero the same way. Filed and cannot be measured, did not file, and outside the frame are three different facts with three different remedies, and collapsing them into one blank makes the largest cities look like the smallest. This is the same shape as the already-catalogued absence-in-a-partial-coverage-layer-is-a-jurisdiction entry, arriving from the other direction: there the gap was coverage, here it is measurability. Guard: where an app reports a derived measure across a published register, count the entities that filed and cannot support the measure, render that as its own map class, and refuse to print a figure for them. Four states plus a thin-denominator state, never two.

Absence in a PARTIAL-COVERAGE layer is a jurisdiction, not a zero. [data] (2026-09-07, portfolio & asset register) CAL FIRE's State Responsibility Area hazard layer covers only State Responsibility Area, so most of urban Los Angeles County has no polygon at all — and that absence means "this is Local Responsibility Area, read the other map," not "no hazard." Binding one half and reading a blank as safe is the worst error available in a hazard lane, and it is silent: the join succeeds, the column fills with a plausible value, and nothing is null. The two halves also carry different class vocabularies — the local one adds NonWildland, which the service codes FHSZ = -3, a sentinel rather than a severity — so they cannot be unioned into one legend without saying so. The same shape recurs wherever coverage is split by administrative responsibility rather than by geography. Guard: bind every half, render what neither covers as not mapped rather than as a class or a zero, name the responsibility area beside the class in the legend, and assert that an uncovered record reads not mapped and never None.

returnDistinctValues caps at maxRecordCount and reports it only in exceededTransferLimit. [data] (2026-09-03, parcel record coverage) A recipe recorded this parameter as returning HTTP 400 on a county assessment-roll service. Re-probed, it does not fail at all: it returned 76 distinct land-use descriptions and 58 distinct base years without complaint. Asking the same service for distinct parcel keys returned exactly 1,000 values — the layer's maxRecordCount — with exceededTransferLimit: true and nothing else to mark the truncation. So the trap is real and the recorded symptom was the wrong one: the call succeeds, and a chip list built from it silently becomes a chip list of the first thousand. Guard: read exceededTransferLimit on every distinct-value response, not only on feature pages, and treat a returned count equal to maxRecordCount as presumed truncated until proved otherwise. And when a recipe records a capability as failing, re-run it before building the workaround — a symptom recorded once is a claim about one day.

Duplication is not uniform, so never divide a row count to get an entity count. [data] (2026-09-02, site selection - the swing slate) Caltrans' statewide AADT layer serves 13,919 rows, and the duplication is real and well known: probe any station and it comes back exactly twice. Two recipes therefore record "13,919 rows for roughly 6,960 stations" — 13,919 divided by 2. Measured across the whole layer, the rows-per-(RTE, PM) histogram runs 1x to 28x: 6,444 keys appear twice, 156 once, 95 four times, 29 six times, and a tail beyond. The real count is 6,754, and the halving is wrong by 205. Guard: derive an entity count by de-duplicating on the key and taking the size of the set, never by dividing. Probing two or three rows establishes that duplication exists; it establishes nothing about its multiplicity. Print the histogram once during research — it costs one query, and it is the difference between a measured number and an assumed one.

A join between two layers of the same publisher can cover a MINORITY of rows — and a licensed fallback makes that invisible. [data] (2026-09-02, site selection - the swing slate) Caltrans' AADT layer carries no vintage field, and the documented workaround is to take the count year from its Truck Volumes sibling, joining on route and postmile: "where the join fails the exposure figure renders count year unknown, never a guess." Measured: only 2,362 of 6,754 stations have any match — 35 %. "Unknown" is the majority state, not the exception, and where the join does resolve, the year is frequently ancient: the spread across matched stations runs 1972 to 2024, with two stations at 2024 and 120 at 1996. Guard: measure the join rate before designing around the join, and design the miss as an ordinary value with its own rendering rather than as an error state. The danger compounds whenever a recipe already licenses a graceful fallback: a coverage of 35 %, of 5 % and of 0 % all render identically as "unknown", so the fallback conceals both a sparse join and a completely broken one. Assert the ratio, and print it on screen.

A {"count":0} from a spatial query is three different answers, and only one of them is "no." [app] (2026-09-02, land bank & acquisition screening) It may be a real absence, a gap in a surface whose own coverage is unknown, or a geography the layer does not cover at all. Measured: one 225-acre footprint in San Bernardino County returned {"count":0} from six layers, and three of those zeros meant three different things — the wildfire zero was a true absence, the liquefaction zero meant never evaluated (its companion "unevaluated areas" layer returned 1 for the same geometry), and the parcel zero meant this county is not bound at all. Rendering all six identically is the worst defect available to a constraint screen, because it converts a coverage gap into an all-clear on the ground a buyer is about to option.

Guard: resolve the containing administrative unit BEFORE reading any count, and render NOT COVERED rather than zero wherever the layer's own frame excludes the query geometry. Where a register publishes a companion "unevaluated" or "not mapped" surface, query it in the same breath and let it separate clear from never looked.

A published layer whose title claims a whole jurisdiction may cover unincorporated territory only. [data] (2026-09-02, land bank & acquisition screening) Santa Clara County's Zoning service holds 1,087 polygons for a county of 504,648 parcels; its published title is "Santa Clara County Unincorporated Zoning Districts", and the service name is not. Sacramento County's County Zoning (PLANNING/16) is the same shape and gives itself away only in the data: JURISDICTION has exactly one distinct value, UNINCORPORATED, across all 3,296 polygons. So two of the three counties in one bound set publish county zoning that answers nothing inside a city — and an empty result there is a gap, not an absence of constraint.

Guard: check the polygon count against the parcel count, and read the distinct values of any jurisdiction column. A ratio of 1,087 to 504,648 is not a zoning atlas. Where the layer is territory-limited, an empty answer must render as unknowable / not covered, naming the reason, never as no constraint.

Whether a service SETS exceededTransferLimit is not stable between reads — so no guard may depend on its presence or its absence. [data] (2026-08-30, resilience disclosure pack) On the FEMA NRI census-tract service, resultRecordCount=20000 truncated to 2,000 rows with no exceededTransferLimit flag on 2026-08-18. Twelve days later the same query, the same query with no resultRecordCount, and three returnDistinctValues queries all returned 2,000 rows with exceededTransferLimit=True:

2026-08-18  asked 50000, features : returned=9106  exceededTransferLimit=None
2026-08-30  asked 20000, features : returned=2000  exceededTransferLimit=True
2026-08-30  no resultRecordCount  : returned=2000  exceededTransferLimit=True
2026-08-30  distinct TRACTFIPS    : returned=2000  exceededTransferLimit=True
2026-08-30  returnIdsOnly=true    : objectIdFieldName='OBJECTID'  len=9106

The cap never moved; only the service's honesty about it did. A guard written against the flag being present would have shipped truncated numbers on the first date; a guard written against it being absent is dead code on the second. Guard: neither. Treat any count that lands exactly on maxRecordCount as "at least N" and page — and note that returnIdsOnly returned all 9,106 ids in one response on both dates, which is the escape here. Joins the existing paging silently stops short and ids-vs-features are different channels entries: the flag is a courtesy, not a contract.

Paging silently stops short when the server caps below your page size. [data] [app] A paging helper compared the returned page against the requested size. The flowline layer caps at 1,000; asking for 2,000 returned exactly 1,000 — no error, no exceededTransferLimit — so the loop halted at 1,000 of 1,619 reaches. That understated the stream network and inflated the measured share from 11.2 % to 18.0 %, in the flattering direction. Guard: the effective page size is whatever the server actually returns at its widest, not what you asked for. The most expensive entry in this file.

returnIdsOnly and a feature query are DIFFERENT channels, and only one of them is capped. [data] (2026-08-20, asset-level exposure scoring) The house probe measures "the real page size" with returnIdsOnly=true&resultRecordCount=50000. On Cal OES's FEMA_Flood_Hazard_Areas/3 that returns 50,000 ids — the id channel ignores maxRecordCount entirely — while the same request asking for features returns 2,000 with exceededTransferLimit: true. Believing the id probe, you would page 70,856 polygons in two requests instead of thirty-six and silently ingest 3 % of the layer. Guard: measure the page size on the channel you will actually page — ask for outFields and read features.length, not objectIds.length.

A capped denominator inflates every share computed from it. [data] A 2,985-row register returned exactly 2,000 without complaint. Guard: compare against returnCountOnly and fail loudly on equality with maxRecordCount.

returnCountOnly itself can lie. [data] One boundaries layer returns {"count":0} with HTTP 200 against 36 real object ids. Guard: on any layer whose count reaches a KPI, assert the count and a fetch of ids.

A host can answer HTTP 200 with a challenge page instead of data. [data] A WAF (Imperva/Incapsula and friends) returns an HTML interstitial with a 200 status, so curl -w '%{http_code}' reports success and a naive fetch stores markup as if it were JSON — and it does this intermittently, so one clean probe proves nothing. Guard: assert the response parses and carries the field you expect, never just that the status was 200.

Some services publish hundreds of layers — never enumerate them. [data] One drilldown MapServer exposes 855 layers (one per provider × technology). Walking the catalogue is a self-inflicted outage. Guard: use the service's identify/summary endpoint instead, and cap any catalogue crawl.

returnIdsOnly ignores the feature page size, so pacing a fetch off it truncates. [data] (2026-08-13, catchment & market-share) One layer advertises maxRecordCount: 2000. Asking for 5,000 ids returns exactly 5,000; asking for 5,000 features returns 2,000 with exceededTransferLimit: true. A pager that establishes its stride from an ids-only probe therefore believes it has a 5,000-row page and drops 60 % of every subsequent one. Guard: the stride is what the server returns for the request you actually make — measure it with features, not ids.

A capped fetch does not SAMPLE the viewport — it slices off a REGION of it. [app] [data] (2026-08-16, collateral & portfolio risk) ArcGIS returns features in object-id order, and object-id order correlates with geography. A viewport query capped at 1,500 of 2,806 published polygons therefore paints one part of the view and leaves the rest bare: the first statewide screenshot showed unevaluated-quadrangle hazard across the north-east and south-east and none along the dense coastal corridor, which is exactly backwards — that corridor is where the portfolio was. Every assertion was green, and the layer panel honestly said capped. A caption cannot undo a map that makes a false spatial claim. Guard: page to completeness; ask returnCountOnly first and, where a layer cannot be completed within the page budget, draw nothing and say too dense to draw here, zoom in. Assert that nothing is drawn from an incomplete fetch, and that what is drawn spans the view rather than a corner of it.

A stride probe can return HTTP 200 with an {error} body instead of a short page. [data] (2026-08-16, collateral & portfolio risk) Asking TIGERweb's tract layer — maxRecordCount 100,000 — for 50,000 features with geometry returns 200 carrying {"code":500,"message":"Error performing query operation"}. Not a short page, and not a transport error. A pager that reads it as "this layer is dead" abandons a healthy layer; one that reads it as transient retries a request that fails identically forever. Guard: step a ladder of page sizes down and take the first the server will actually serve for the request you are going to make — the widest request is not always answerable, and the failure arrives dressed as a permanent server fault.

The stride is what the server RETURNS, and writing that down does not make you do it. [app] (2026-08-16, collateral & portfolio risk) A paging helper carried a comment explaining this exact trap and then compared each page against the requested size anyway: a 1,000-row page against a 2,000-row request ended the loop, declaring a 1,041-row layer complete at 1,000 and a 2,806-row layer complete at 1,000. Guard: measure the stride from the first page, and reconcile the total against the server's own returnCountOnly before calling a fetch complete. A comment is not a guard; an assertion is.

A computational halo must never reach a number on screen. [app] (2026-08-13, catchment & market-share) Outlets were deliberately fetched on a box ~9 km wider than the study area, so a nearest-neighbour distance at the edge would not be wrong for want of a feature just outside the frame. That halo then leaked into the legend (362 against the register chip's 113 for the same layer) and, worse, into a decision: the default subject brand was chosen as the largest on the halo rather than the largest in the market, naming a different company. Guard: clip to the study area for every displayed count and every derived choice; let the halo feed geometry only, and say on screen that the map draws context the counts exclude.

Never truncate silently. [app] Every cap says so on screen: +N more, Capped, not complete, 250 of 1,975 rows.

ArcGIS answers errors with HTTP 200 and an {error} body. [core: fixed] [data] Rate limits and bad queries return 200 plus {"error": …} and no features. Code doing j.features || [] reads that as zero features and overwrites good data on the next refresh tick. Fixed in engine/arcgisSource.ts (assertNotArcgisError throws). Still applies to any fetch you write yourself.

returnIdsOnly and returnGeometry=false features are DIFFERENT page-size channels. [data] (2026-08-23, access to care) On HCAI's facility layer (maxRecordCount: 1000), asking returnIdsOnly=true&resultRecordCount=5000 returns 5,000 ids with exceededTransferLimit: true, while the same request for features returns exactly 1,000. Sizing a feature pager from the ids probe therefore reads one page and stops, losing 90 % of the layer with no error and no flag. Guard: measure the page size on the channel you will actually page — features, with the outFields you will actually request — and page until a short page comes back, not until a count you computed elsewhere is reached.

A layer with one row per GEOMETRY is not a layer with one row per RECORD, and the ratio can be 110:1. [data] (2026-08-24, shortage-area explorer) A "component boundaries" publication of a federal register returned 1,131 rows for one state on the dental layer and 2,363 on the mental-health layer — which read as 1,131 and 2,363 designations. They are 93 and 135: one designation is sliced into one row per census tract it touches, and the largest single record occupied 110 rows. Quoting the row count overstates the record count by an order of magnitude, and the error is invisible because every row is individually correct. The publisher's own perimeter publication of the same register carries exactly one row per record — and its designated count matched COUNT(DISTINCT <sourceId>) on the component layer exactly. Guard: before any count reaches a screen, compare returnCountOnly against COUNT(DISTINCT <the record id>); where they differ, the layer is a geometry table and the headline number must come from the distinct count or from a different publication.

A Total row inside the data — and the sibling sheet keys the same row BLANK. [data] (2026-08-24, housing & homelessness services) The 2025 Housing Inventory Count sheet's last data row is keyed 'Total' and carries the national roll-up: 488,985 crisis beds. Read naively it becomes a 388th "region" whose inventory is the whole country's, sitting at the top of every class break and doubling every statewide sum. The Point-in-Time sheet carries the same roll-up with an empty key column and the word Total in the name column, followed by two blank rows. A build that dropped "the last row", or dropped "the row keyed Total", is wrong on one of the two sheets. Guard: validate the key against the identifier's own pattern (^[A-Z]{2}-\d{3}[a-z]?$ here) and assert the surviving row count — 386, not 387, on both sheets. Never drop by position, and never drop by one publisher's sentinel word.

A footnote paragraph in the key column, and a footnote marker ON a key. [data] (2026-08-24, housing & homelessness services) The same count sheet ends with a paragraph of prose in the key column ("a MO-604 covers territory in both Missouri and Kansas…"), and keys Kansas City MO-604a where the inventory workbook and both boundary services say MO-604. A straight key join drops that region from the comparison without a word — and the prose row, if the regex is loose, becomes a region too. Guard: a key regex and normalisation of a trailing footnote marker, and a symmetric-difference report printed on screen rather than logged. Without the normalisation the difference carries both MO-604a and MO-604, and one more region falls out silently.

A layer where 96 % of rows carry no attributes and every one of them shares a single coordinate. [data] (2026-08-25, public health preparedness) A state alternate-care-site register returned {"count":955} and looked healthy. 919 of those rows carried no attributes at all — FacilityName, FacilityType, County and TotalBeds all null — and all 919 sat on the same point (-122.429655, 37.728171), which would have rendered as a 919-deep stack of markers on one San Francisco block. Of the 36 rows that did carry attributes, 13 were Demobilized and exactly one had OperationalBeds > 0; the layer was last edited five years earlier. A count check, an ids-vs-features check and a geometry-not-null check all pass on this layer. Guard: two cheap tests disqualify it — count rows where the layer's primary attribute is null, and count distinct geometries against the row count. Where they disagree by an order of magnitude the source has disqualified itself, and that is a finding to render (the lane stays empty with its citation), not a rendering problem to solve. Belongs beside the existing count that disagrees with its ids test.

A hard page cap that 422s instead of clamping - and the tail is one whole province. [data] (2026-08-26, humanitarian response map) limit=20000 returns HTTP 422 {"detail":[{"type":"less_than_equal", ... "ctx":{"le":10000}}]} - a schema validation error, not a truncated page. Cause: the cap is declared on the query parameter, so asking above it is a malformed request rather than a large one. Guard: this is good news dressed as an error - prefer it to a silent clamp - but still page, and still assert the total. The lane here returned 10,000 + 97, and those 97 rows were one entire province (Nimroz). A build that requests one page and stops loses a province without a single error.

A server-side equality filter that is really a case-insensitive substring match. [data] (2026-08-26, humanitarian response map) &category=total on a people-in-need lane returns rows whose category is Total - Iran (District of Return), Total - Pakistan (District of Return), Total - Border / EC and Total - Temporary Sites alongside the total row actually wanted. Summing the response double-counts every returnee and displacement sub-population into the denominator. Cause: the filter is implemented as a LIKE, and nothing in the parameter name says so. The same API does it again on sector_code=PRO, which returns PRO, PRO-CPN, PRO-GBV, PRO-HLP and PRO-MIN. Guard: never trust a filter parameter to mean equality - assert the returned distinct values against what you asked for, and re-filter client-side. This is the most dangerous entry of its build: it is silent, it inflates rather than empties, and the inflated result still looks plausible.

Two releases of the same annual figure served in one response. [data] (2026-08-26, humanitarian response map) A needs lane returned both the 2024 and the 2025 humanitarian needs overview for the same districts and sectors, distinguished only by a resource_hdx_id and a reference period - 107,971 rows against 100,591. Summing produces roughly double, and every cell looks duplicated for no visible reason. Cause: the API is a union over published resources, not a current view. Guard: pin the release id, fail the build if more than one survives the pin, and print the pinned id on screen. With the release pinned the cells were unique (cells with >1 row: 0), which is the assertion that proves the pin worked rather than merely ran.

A lane exploded by a free-text dimension: 208,562 rows describing 4,693 cells. [data] (2026-08-26, humanitarian response map) A per-sector, per-district population lane returned forty times more rows than it has cells, because each cell repeats once per category - a free-text column mixing age band, gender, disability status and population group, carrying two competing spellings of the same idea (Adult/Adults, Children/Boys+Girls). A build that sums population over the response inflates its denominator roughly forty-fold. Guard: count the distinct keys before trusting a row count as a cell count; select the one aggregate row exactly rather than by filter; and treat a free-text dimension as a reason not to offer slicing by it - thin cells sliced by an uncontrolled demographic vocabulary are demographically identifiable, not analysis.

A JSON API can honour an over-large page size PARTIALLY, with no error and no flag. [data] (2026-08-26, facility capacity & catchment) data.cms.gov/data-api/v1/dataset/<id>/data?size=10000 returns 6,500 rows. Not an error, not a 400, not a documented maxRecordCount — just fewer rows than asked for, silently. A pager that stops when rows < size ends at 6,500 of 1,175,708 and reports success. Guard: the house rule ("the effective page size is whatever the server returns at its widest") applies to plain JSON APIs and not only to ArcGIS — probe the ceiling once, page at or below it, and assert the total against the endpoint's own /stats found_rows when it publishes one.

A row count is not an event count, and the register usually counts rows. [data] (2026-09-03, transaction evidence) A county assessor's recorded-transfer file published 57,357 rows; those rows are 48,119 distinct recorded instruments, because one deed can convey many parcels and the file lists each parcel it touched. The overstatement is 19.2 % across the file and 239.2 % on one class — 3,975 vacant-land rows are 1,172 deals — and a single deed recorded on one day conveys 614 parcels. The duplicate check ran first and came back clean: zero identical (key, date, amount, document) signatures, so this is a grain error, not a duplication error, and no de-duplication pass would have caught it. The same shape appears wherever one document, permit, claim, order or invoice touches many features.

Guard: establish the event key and count distinct on it before reporting any count as a count of events — then say on screen which of the two numbers is showing. A count labelled "sales" that is really "parcels conveyed" turns one transaction into six hundred pieces of evidence, which is the opposite of what the reader is trying to establish.

A distinct count may be impossible server-side, and that is an architecture decision rather than a detail. [data] (2026-09-03, transaction evidence) On an ArcGIS MapServer, both routes to a distinct count were closed: outStatistics with statisticType: "count-distinct" returned {"error":{"code":400,"extendedCode":-2147024809,"message":"Invalid or missing input parameters."}}, and returnDistinctValues=true together with returnCountOnly=true returned "When returnCountOnly and returnDistinctValues are true, more than one field specified in outFields is not supported." So a count that the whole application reported could only be produced by paging every row and counting in the client — 29 pages and 44 s for 57,357 rows, which changed the load budget, the load order and the widget inventory of the app that needed it.

Guard: probe both routes before designing a screen around a distinct count. If neither exists, decide deliberately between paging at load, a build-time bake with a visible staleness state, and dropping the figure — and make that choice in the design rather than discovering it in the build.

A min/max range is not a series, and reading one without its distribution is how a three-year file becomes a fifty-five-year history. [data] (2026-09-03, transaction evidence) MIN/MAX on a date field returned 1971-02-22 → 2026-08-24 and was recorded as a 55-year series. The cumulative counts say otherwise: >= 1990 57,308, >= 2010 57,259, >= 2023-01-01 57,243 — 99.80 % of the file, with only 114 rows carrying the whole remaining span and 49 of them predating 1990. It is a rolling window of about three years, which is what the publisher's file is for and is not a history. The consequence is a design, not a number: a time slider across it would have implied a past that does not exist, and every trend, pace and period-on-period comparison had to come out.

Guard: ask for cumulative counts at several cutoffs before putting any time control on screen. This is the same class of error as the lexicographic maximum — a range statistic read without its distribution — but it kills a silhouette rather than a figure.

A universe derived from the rows loaded SO FAR makes a measured zero disappear instead of rendering as one. [app] (2026-09-03, transaction evidence) The app bands 55 submarket cells on how many recorded instruments each holds for the property class in force, and a cell holding none is the finding the whole product exists to show. The cell list was built from the rows already paged, so a submarket with no transfer of that class was not banded NONE — it was absent from the surface entirely, and the band counts silently summed to fewer than 55. It never showed against the live file, where every situs ZIP happens to carry something in some class; it showed against a synthetic fixture built to contain such a cell, and it would have shown in production during the first of two load phases, when only five of six classes had arrived. Cause: the denominator was taken from the data that had loaded rather than from the data that exists. Guard: get the universe from the register — a groupByFieldsForStatistics over the whole file, once — and iterate that, looking each cell's value up rather than reading the keys back out of the rows. Then assert the sum: every band count together must equal the universe, on every class, including during a partial load. A zero you cannot see is indistinguishable from a geography you never covered, and this file renders those two states differently on purpose.

resultOffset paging is quadratic on a large ArcGIS layer; keyset paging is flat. [data] (2026-09-07, valuation & taxation base map) The same 1,000-row page — twelve fields, no geometry — measured twice four days apart against a 2.37 M-row roll:

offset 0            2.08 s   and 0.80 s on the re-probe
offset 500,000     22.35 s
offset 1,200,000   46.61 s   and 44.29 s on the re-probe

A full walk by offset is about 15.6 hours, because the server counts past every skipped row. The same page taken by key — where AIN > '<last AIN seen>' with orderByFields=AIN and no resultOffset — is flat at 0.5–1.9 s wherever it starts and 25 % smaller on the wire. Partitioned into seven disjoint leading-digit key ranges and run in parallel, the whole roll walked in 310 s. Guard: page by key on anything above a few tens of thousands of rows, and assert the flatness rather than the total — an offset walk that is merely slow today is a walk that trips a timeout tomorrow, and the failure arrives as a short walk rather than as an error. The key must be unique for the cursor not to skip or repeat a row; prove that with a count on one value before trusting it.

A group-by statistics query caps at maxRecordCount groups, and resultOffset does not page it. [data] (2026-09-07, valuation & taxation base map) A count per UseCode over a 2,372,107-row roll returned exactly 1,000 groups with exceededTransferLimit: true against 1,075 distinct values, and the returned group counts summed to 2,372,032 — 75 rows missing from a total that looks complete. resultOffset=1000 on the same query returns the same first page again, starting at 0100, so the tail is unreachable by paging. This is the group-by sibling of the returnDistinctValues-caps-at-maxRecordCount entry above: same cap, same flag, and no offset that will get past it. Guard: never consume a group-by without reading exceededTransferLimit, and never let a group-by total stand as a population — cross-check it against a plain count of the same predicate and print both if they differ. To reach a capped tail, slice by an explicit predicate until each slice fits under the cap.

returnCountOnly and an outStatistics count disagree on the same spatial query, and neither path is reliably the larger. [data] (2026-09-07, valuation & taxation base map) One envelope over Los Angeles, esriSpatialRelIntersects, same where: returnCountOnly answers 61,429 and count(AIN) through outStatistics answers 61,506. Reproducible, and not a null-field artefact — count(OBJECTID) on a non-null object id returns the same 61,506, and AIN IS NULL on the roll returns zero. The two code paths apply the spatial predicate differently. The sting is in the direction: an earlier probe of a different envelope on the same layer had returnCountOnly higher (39,590 against 39,587), so neither path can even be characterised as the generous one and no constant offset reconciles them. Guard: pick one counting path and use it for every number on the screen — this build uses the statistics path everywhere, the same one that produces its headline share — because a readout saying "61,429 parcels in view" beside a table listing 61,506 is a contradiction the reader finds before you do.

returnDistinctValues paired with returnCountOnly can be refused INTERMITTENTLY, for tens of minutes, while the layer's advertised capabilities never change. [data] (2026-09-07, valuation & taxation base map) This route answered on 2026-09-03 and was written into a recipe as the honesty cross-check its distinct-page count depended on. On the morning of 2026-09-07 it returned HTTP 400 "Failed to execute query." with an empty details array on every field tried — Assr_Map, UseCode, UseType, Roll_Year, Roll_LandBaseYear — on five consecutive attempts across two separate processes over twenty minutes. Throughout, advancedQueryCapabilities went on reporting supportsDistinct: true and supportsCountDistinct: true, and returnDistinctValues=true without returnCountOnly still answered normally, capped at 1,000 rows. A LIKE predicate breaks the distinct query even while it is up. An hour later it answered twelve times out of twelve, with the same 87,640 it had given four days earlier. It was never dead; every symptom pointed at a permanent removal.

Guard, in two halves. First, the house rule about declaring an endpoint dead applies to a parameter combination as much as to a host: probe more than once, spaced, before recording that a query shape is gone — and write the suite to pass on either outcome and say which it saw, so a reader learns the shape is unreliable rather than learning to ignore a red line. Second, and the more important half: an honesty cross-check may not depend on a route that can vanish. The replacement built here is a group-by over a key prefix, sliced narrow enough to stay under the 1,000-group cap and recursing into a longer prefix wherever exceededTransferLimit says a slice was capped — every slice checked, nothing summed that was truncated. It reproduced 87,640 exactly, in 517 requests and 67 s. Keep the cheap route as the fast path, fall back to the resilient one, and cross-check the cheap answer against one slice of the resilient one, so a route that answers with a wrong number is caught as well as one that refuses.

A sentinel's size depends on the filter you have not applied yet. [data] (2026-09-07, valuation & taxation base map) A '0000' base-year sentinel appears on 53,283 rows of a parcel layer — and on 124 rows of the current roll. The other 53,159 sit in a roll-year-null population that also carries zero value on every row, so the sentinel is not a defect inside the register at all: it is the marker of a parcel that is not on it. Reported unsliced it is a 53,283-row problem nobody would ever check; sliced by the predicate the product actually applies it is a 129-row list a person can work through, which is a smaller headline and a better product. Guard: slice every sentinel by the filter the application itself applies before reporting its size, and then check what the rows outside that filter have in common — here, all of them valueless. That shared property is usually the real finding.

A GET over ~2,048 characters answers HTTP 404 with an HTML page, and a join reads it as "no match". [data] (2026-10-21, watch wall research) An IrwinID IN (…) query against a national ArcGIS Online wildfire layer, sent as GET in chunks of 40 GUIDs, joined 12 of 92 records that were all there. The chunks whose URL ran to 2,213 characters came back HTTP 404 with an XHTML error page — not 414, not an ArcGIS {error} — and a client that treats any unparseable body as "no rows" counted them as misses; 1,703 characters answered 200, and the same 40 ids by POST answered {"count":40}. Guard: send IN-lists and long where clauses by POST (application/x-www-form-urlencoded), treat a non-JSON body as a failed request that stops the join, and assert in the suite that the joined count equals the ids you sent. (2026-10-21, impact zone build) The same failure on a different host answers HTTP 400, still with an HTML body: a polygon query to a federal-data ArcGIS Online host whose zone ring ran past 2,048 characters returned HTTP 400 <!DOCTYPE HTML PUBLIC …, and the identical query by form-encoded POST answered. The status code is not the signal — a non-JSON body is. Send every geometry query by POST (one real perimeter's rings reached 409 KB). (2026-10-22, data compact build) The same ~2,150-character county-polygon count was JSON on services.arcgis.com, services5 and services7 and a 404 text/html page on services1 and services3 — so a GET that works against one ArcGIS Online host is no evidence for the next. Re-measured 2026-10-22: unchanged. Every geometry query is now a form-encoded POST (a CORS simple request; * came back on every host).

A feed that blanks during its own reload is indistinguishable from "nothing here". [data] (2026-10-21, watch wall research) The NWS watch / warning / advisory MapServer rebuilds about every two minutes, and for 5–10 seconds of each rebuild it answers HTTP 200, CORS intact, count 0 and features: [] — on 4 of 44 reads taken 4 s apart, and on both layers at once in a separate probe. A live display that renders each poll literally goes blank every couple of minutes, and an alerting rule on "the count fell to zero" fires on the service's housekeeping. A statewide evacuation aggregation documents the same shape in its own item description ("a brief period (10 sec) where the service is rebuilding and not all features are visible"). Guard: keep the last good set; accept a zero that follows a non-zero only when a second read ≥ 15 s later agrees; never alert on a disappearance. (2026-10-21, incident situation map build) Applied to the evacuation aggregation (whose ~10 s rebuild window is stated in its own item description): a drop to 0 is re-read after 15 s before it is shown as "none". Status: the guard is tested with an injected network that answers non-zero, then empty, then non-zero; the live window itself was not caught on the day, so the suite asserts the guard, not the outage. (2026-10-22, live-position build) Seen once on a live vehicle-position layer (San Diego MTS MTS_AVL/0, a transit fleet): 0 rows on two polls 30 s apart, between reads of 41–68 vehicles. Not reproduced, so not explained — but two empty reads were not enough to call the fleet empty. Show such an answer as the feed's answer ("answered with 0 units"), never as "no units out".

A rolling-window feed's count falls with no event ending. [data] (2026-10-21, watch wall research) USGS's "past day" earthquake summary went 172 → 168 → 166 → 162 over two hours while no new California event arrived — rows age out of the 24-hour window. A KPI that reports the change in that count, or a rule that treats a drop as something resolving, is reading the window, not the world. Guard: count events by their own time against your own window (since a handover, since a date), never by the feed's length; a falling length is never a change.

A feed that has not answered yet is not a zero — a "quiet" headline needs every counted feed. [app] (2026-10-21, watch wall build) The wall's first live paint said "Quiet since 06:00" while its NWS alert feed still read "grey: no data yet" — the seven-day /alerts history paginates and takes seconds longer than the other feeds, and the quiet rule only checked that the incident feed had answered. Every other count was a real zero; the warnings count was an absent one, rendered as 0 and folded into an all-clear. The offline suite could not see it, because every fixture answers at once. Guard: an all-clear is a conjunction over every feed it counts — compute the list of feeds still pending, refuse the quiet headline while it is non-empty, and say on screen which feed is still being read ("not counted as zero"). Test it with one feed failing while the rest are empty.

A perimeter layer that keeps every mapped version counts versions, not fires. [data] (2026-10-21, incident situation map build) The FIRIS intel perimeter layer returned 493 rows, 60 of them Active — every perimeter ever mapped for an incident stays in the layer beside its successors. A count of rows is a count of mapping flights; drawn as-is, the map stacks a fire's old outlines under its current one. Guard: dedupe to the newest row per (incident_name, source) before counting or drawing, and assert in the suite that no key appears twice after the dedupe. Keep the history rather than discarding it: the versions are what make an honest growth replay possible, each frame dated by its own row. (2026-10-21, impact zone build) The dedupe key needs an upper-cased incident_name: one fire is spelled Timber by one source and TIMBER by another. And returnDistinctValues on that host folds case (7 distinct names) where reading the rows gives 8 spellings — so a distinct-values probe hides the very split the dedupe must handle. Print each perimeter's own poly_DateCurrent and source too: the "Active" version adopted as a zone was 21.9 days old, while the interagency layer held a newer outline of the same fire.

Socrata's default page is 1,000 rows, and it carries no flag, no total and no Link header. [data] (2026-09-07, property hazard exposure) An unbounded read of a 13,277-row register returns exactly 1,000 rows and looks complete. Unlike an ArcGIS service there is no exceededTransferLimit to check and no count beside the page, so nothing in the response distinguishes "this is all of it" from "this is the first 4 %". The platform hosts thousands of municipal datasets, so this is not one register's quirk. Guard: send an explicit $limit above the count you got from $select=count(1), then assert the returned length against that count and fail loudly — a short read is a stop, not a warning, because every figure downstream is wrong by an unknown amount and nothing on screen would say so.

OpenFEMA silently returns 1,000 rows when a query has no $top, and metadata.count reads 0 unless $count=true. [data] (2026-10-22, data compact build) - Symptom: FEMA's DisasterDeclarationsSummaries v2 for California, all years, came back as 1,000 rows with metadata.top 1000 and metadata.count 0. Nothing said 691 rows were missing; $count=true reports 1,691. - Cause: OpenFEMA's default page is 1,000 and the total is computed only when asked for. - Guard: always send $top and $count=true, compare the rows to metadata.count, and print "capped at n of N — not complete" when they differ. The live suite asserts the trap is still there.

An ungrouped statistic over an empty selection returns one row of nulls, not an empty list. [data] (2026-10-21, impact zone build) A min/max of IMAGE_DATE over a zone with no footprints (an offshore box) came back as one feature whose attributes are all null, while the grouped count over the same zone returned features: []. A reader that takes "one row" as "found something" prints an imagery range of null → null. Guard: a statistic row whose values are all null is none; assert it in the live suite against a known-empty geometry.

A statewide group-by on a 135-million-row layer is a coin toss; the same query scoped to a zone answers in a second. [data] (2026-10-21, impact zone build) groupByFieldsForStatistics=OCC_CLS,PRIM_OCC over every California footprint (STATE_FIPS='06', 10.9 million rows of a national layer) failed with a generic 400 after 56 s in the recipe's probe, answered in 38.6 s on a re-run, then in 0.2 s from the server's cache. Scoped to a county it answers in ~1.3 s; scoped to a zone polygon, in under a second. Guard: never group a national layer statewide from a browser; scope by geometry or a small attribute filter, and treat a fast answer as an accident of the cache, not a contract.

3 · Field semantics — does this field mean what it is called?

A nullable class field can be null on MORE ROWS THAN ANY REPORTED CLASS, and a renderer without a labelled default is then a map of the minority. [data] [app] (2026-09-07, facilities & space utilisation) Utilization on the federal inventory is null on 7,015 of 13,001 California buildings — 54.0 %, against 5,637 Utilized, 290 Unutilized and 59 Underutilized. The largest utilisation class in California is the absence of one. A three-value uniqueValue renderer draws the 46 % and leaves the majority to a default symbol that, unlabelled, reads as "other" or as nothing at all. Guard: whenever a symbolised field is nullable, the renderer needs a defaultSymbol with a label that says NOT REPORTED — never "Other" — and the null class must appear in the legend, in the split, and in every count with its own denominator. Generalise the house rule: a geography with no data renders NOT COVERED and never zero now has a sibling — a silence renders NOT REPORTED and never zero. The second half is the one that bites inside a covered geography.

"Publishes a value" has two honest readings, and a source can encode a silence AS A ZERO. [data] (2026-09-07, facilities & space utilisation) Owned_and_Otherwise_Managed_Annual_Operations_Cost is non-null on 7,088 California buildings and greater than zero on 6,597: 491 standing buildings publish an annual operating cost of exactly $0.00. Every headline in the recipe — the tri-lane count, the unused-area total, the per-county denominators — reconciles only on the > 0 population, while the recipe's own prose quoted the non-null one, so the same document described the lane with one number and computed it with another. A $0 operating cost for a standing building is not a measurement; it is a non-report written as a number, and averaging it in drags a cost-per-square-foot toward zero. Guard: for any money or measure column, count IS NOT NULL and > 0 and compare them before quoting either. Where they differ, say so on screen and report the zero-publishing population as its own state, folded into neither side. This is the mirror of the published-zero-means-not-counted entry: there the zero was a suppression, here it is a silence, and in both the fix is to refuse to let it become arithmetic.

Two official registers from the SAME publisher publish two different values for the same feature on the same day. [data] (2026-09-07, facilities & space utilisation) The federal inventory and the federal owned-and-leased register both publish a floor area per building. 613 California buildings join across them — by identifier and, independently, by coordinate, returning the same 613, which is how the join was asserted rather than assumed. Of the 612 pairs with two positive areas, 483 agree EXACTLY and 65 differ by more than 5 %, the worst by 99 %: one downtown address is published as 120,149 rentable square feet in one register and 42,471 in the other. This is not an error to correct. It is ANSI/BOMA Z65.1 (rentable area, measured for leasing) against ASTM E1836 / IFMA (measured for facility management) in live data: both correct, both published, and not the same number. Guard: reconcile before you join; report the disagreement per feature rather than picking a side; and never average two measurement standards. Note the shape of the evidence — 78.9 % agreeing exactly is what makes the 10.6 % that do not worth flagging. A disagreement rate near 100 % means you joined the wrong things.

A ratio is not bounded by 1, and the source will not tell you before you have drawn the gauge. [data] (2026-09-07, facilities & space utilisation) On the federal owned-and-leased register, BLD_VACANT_RSF exceeds BUILDING_RSF on 14 of the 65 California rows that report any vacancy. Nine overrun by 2 % or less, which is two measurement standards applied to one floor; five do not, and one San Francisco building publishes 120,577 vacant square feet in a building of 49,410 — 244 %. A 270-degree arc cannot draw 244 % honestly: it pins, and a pinned gauge reads as "full" rather than as "impossible". Guard: probe a ratio's actual range before choosing a gauge, a percentage or a progress bar, and where the source cannot guarantee the bound, report the two measured quantities and let the reader see that one is larger than the other.

Identity fields that are empty today, on a service that refreshes weekly, are not a safeguard. [data] [app] (2026-09-07, facilities & space utilisation) The federal owned-and-leased building layer carries nine named-person and personal-email fields in its schema — two points of contact and their addresses, a second contact pair, two senators and a representative — and all nine are populated on 0 of the 628 California rows today. An inspection of today's values would conclude there is nothing to protect. The service is refreshed weekly. Guard: enforce an allow-list of requested fields, never a deny-list and never an inspection, and assert by name that each forbidden field is absent from the compiled spec, from every popup, column and export header. A field that is empty this week is a field that is populated next week, and by then the leak is in a PDF somebody has emailed.

A join key can identify a FILING rather than a thing, and the symptom is a distribution with a suspiciously tidy ceiling. [data] @@STAMP@@ A regional agency republishes the same statutory return as three services joined on a GUID (apr_uuid). All 9,046 entitlement-to-permit joins came back with a reporting-year difference of exactly zero — because the GUID identifies a project-year filing, not a project. A project whose permit landed in a later cycle has an entitlement row in one service and a permit row in another under a different GUID, and nothing joins them. The consequence is fatal for a timing read and invisible without this check: the frame cannot express an interval longer than one reporting cycle, so its maximum measurable lag is 355 days and its over-two-years share is 0.0 % by construction — which reads as a fast, well-run region and is an artefact of survivorship. Guard: when a join yields a suspiciously bounded distribution, group the joined pairs by the difference in their period column. A single-valued result means the key is period-scoped and the whole population is survivors. The right response is to bar that frame from producing the figure at all, not to caveat it.

A date field named for one stage can hold the date of a different one. [data] @@STAMP@@ The same republish carries issue_date on its entitlements service, and it is not the entitlement date — it is the date of the furthest stage the project reached, equal to ent_approve_dt1 on the 12,627 of 21,880 rows still at entitlement and equal to the permit date on the rest. A naive issue_date − ent_approve_dt1 therefore returns zero on 58 % of the table and drags any median it feeds toward nothing. The metadata says only "issue date". Guard: where a service carries both a stage-specific column and a generic date column, prove which stage the generic one belongs to by comparing it against the stage column on rows in each stage separately. Equality on exactly the rows in one stage is the tell.

A missing value can be an empty string in a column that also accepts nulls, and IS NOT NULL will then return the whole table. [data] @@STAMP@@ ENT_APPROVE_DT IS NOT NULL returned 464,059 — every row in the table — while ENT_APPROVE_DT <> '' returned 78,381. Both are valid SQL and only one is a measurement; the wrong one overstates the measurable population by 5.9× and does it silently, because a count of every row looks like a healthy answer rather than a failed predicate. Guard: test both forms of the emptiness predicate on every String column before building a population on it, and record the pair in the recipe. If they differ, the column uses empty strings and every subsequent WHERE must say <> ''.

A String column holding numbers can differ by exactly ONE row between a lexicographic and a numeric comparison, which is the size of error that passes review. [data] @@STAMP@@ ENT_APPROVE_DT > '44000' returned 32,874; CAST(ENT_APPROVE_DT AS INTEGER) > 44000 returned 32,875. The single differing row held a six-digit value where every other row held five, and lexicographic order put '335719' below '44000' because '3' < '4' — while numerically it is eight times larger. orderBy DESC on the same column did not surface it either, because the true maximum is not at the top of a lexicographic sort. This library already records a case where the same trap understated a maximum by 117×; the small-delta version is more dangerous, because a one-row difference reads as a rounding artefact rather than a bug. Guard: cast, always, and check the value-length distribution of any String column you compare — a column that is 45,744 five-digit values and one six-digit value is exactly the shape that hides this.

A name join between two public agency layers can be 460 of 465, and every one of the five failures can be a real, active entity — one differing only by a diacritic. [data] @@STAMP@@ Filing jurisdictions named ANGELS CAMP, CARMEL, CATHEDRAL, SAINT HELENA and LA CANADA FLINTRIDGE do not match the boundary layer's Angels, Carmel-by-the-Sea, Cathedral City, St. Helena and La Cañada Flintridge. The last differs only by a diacritic, so whether the join reports 460 or 461 matches depends on whether the fold normalises Unicode — and a build that folds diacritics will find four failures where a build that does not finds five, with neither number wrong. A naive join renders five cities that filed as blank ground, indistinguishable from cities that filed nothing. Guard: assert the resolved count against the expected count and stop the build on a mismatch. A fuzzy fallback that silently succeeds is worse than a hard failure, because nothing on screen would show it fired. Note also that the boundary layer's names are mixed case while the return's are upper — case folding is required before the aliases are even reachable.

An esriFieldTypeDateOnly column serialises as an ISO date STRING under f=json, not as epoch milliseconds. [data] @@STAMP@@ ent_approve_dt1, bp_issue_dt1, co_issue_dt1 and report_last_updated are all typed esriFieldTypeDateOnly on the regional services, and they come back as "2021-05-11". Arithmetic written for the ordinary esriFieldTypeDate — which does return epoch milliseconds — throws unsupported operand on the subtraction rather than returning a wrong number, which is the merciful outcome and only because subtracting two strings is an error rather than a coercion. In a language that coerces, it would be a silent NaN in a duration. Guard: read the field type from the layer document, not just the name, and branch on DateOnly versus Date. Assert the parsed value round-trips to the string the service sent.

A published aggregate's DOCUMENTED field name may not be the field name it serves. [data] (2026-09-07, portfolio & asset register) The BLS QCEW annual CSV layout names annual_avg_estabs_count; the API returns annual_avg_estabs. A build that trusts the published layout gets a KeyError at best and a silent zero at worst — and a silent zero in an establishment count propagates into every share computed from it. Guard: a field name may not enter a design until a response has shown it, and that includes a bulk file's own published layout. Assert both directions — that the documented name is absent and the served one present — so the day the layout starts matching its documentation you are told.

A suppression flag can zero ONE column and leave another intact, and the zero looks like a measurement. [data] (2026-09-07, portfolio & asset register) Every one of QCEW's 221 suppressed rows reports employment as 0 while still publishing an establishment count, so a mean, a share or a mix computed without reading disclosure_code is quietly wrong — and wrong exactly where an industry has few enough employers to be identifiable, which at county grain is a large share of the interesting cells. At six-digit industry detail the suppressed share is 14.4 %. Same class as the -999 and -666666666 sentinels already catalogued, but harder to spot because the sentinel is a plausible value. Guard: read the disclosure flag, exclude the cell, and render the suppression — never impute across it, and never let a suppressed zero into a mean, a share or a mix. Pick the aggregation level whose suppression you can live with: here the NAICS sector level is the one level with zero suppressed rows, which is why the mix is drawn there rather than deeper.

The SAME published table can support two defensible readings that differ by 2.1x on the largest class. [data] (2026-09-07, portfolio & asset register) QCEW establishment counts for Los Angeles County are dominated by NAICS 62, which is 284,456 of 565,948 private establishments — 50.3 % — at 2.9 employees each, because it counts individual practitioners and home-health registrations rather than occupied commercial floorspace. An establishment-weighted use-class mix therefore reads 54.8 % specialist and an employment-weighted mix of the same table reads 26.4 %. Neither is wrong. Guard: where a published table supports more than one weighting, pick one, print which you picked, and print the alternative beside it. A figure that does not say which reading it is, is the wrong one.

A published break-right column can be populated on 99.99 % of rows and mean nothing on a fifth of them, because the date is set EQUAL to the expiry it is supposed to precede. [data] (2026-09-07, portfolio & asset register) GSA's Current Lease Inventory carries Termination Right Date on 7,367 of 7,368 leases, which reads as "almost every federal lease carries a break right." It does not. On 1,299 rows the date is exactly the lease expiration date — a right to terminate on the last day of the term, which is not a right to terminate at all — and on 2,726 more it has already passed. Only 3,342 are live. The recipe's own §4 had counted a break right as "a termination date inside the horizon", and on that reading the five-year roll-off splits 43.4 % optional / 56.6 % contractual. Excluding the equality rows, 767 of those 1,559 "options" evaporate and the honest split is 26.5 % / 73.5 % — fewer than one in four of a real estate's five-year roll-off is a decision the occupier owns, not fewer than half. Nothing errors, nothing is null, and every count is reproducible; the column is simply not a sentinel you can spot by type or by null-rate. Same class as the -999 and -666666666 entries in this section, but harder, because the sentinel here is a valid date and it is the relationship between two columns that is the tell. Guard: where a column is defined relative to another (a break before an expiry, a review before a renewal, a start before an end), assert the relationship on ingest and count the degenerate cases — equal-to, after, and already-past — before any share is computed off it.
