Epochs are UTC seconds, as strings, so new Date(value) is wrong twice (string, and seconds not milliseconds). And end.endLatitude / end.endLongitude are populated, so a closure is a segment: drawing only the begin point is a choice the app should state, not a limit of the data. Guard: fetch only the districts the area of interest touches; filter to in-place on the three code flags and show planned work as a separate, labelled count; parse Number(s) * 1000; keep every request CORS-simple (the §5 rule).

Two shelter registers cannot be summed, and the federal one keys on lower-case objectid. [data] (2026-10-21, incident situation map build) FEMA's ESF-6 Open Shelters layer reports objectIdField: "objectid" — lower-case (the §1 derive the OID by type, never by name rule); its Cloudflare edge sends the CORS header as lower-case access-control-allow-origin, which a case-sensitive grep Access-Control-Allow-Origin in a probe misses and records as "no CORS" (use grep -i, as the §5 probe does); and several text fields hold a single space " " meaning blank (the single-space entry above). California's CHHS Open Shelters layer is rebuilt daily around 07:00 PT and its count means "open as of the previous day". The two overlap without a shared key, so their sum double-counts and mixes two dates. Guard: show the registers side by side, each with its own as-of line ("FEMA: live · CHHS: open as of yesterday"); never add them; trim and blank-test text fields before display; bind the OID per layer from its own metadata.

Building footprints can pre-date the fire that burned them, and a "structures exposed" count then counts a town that is gone. [data] (2026-10-21, impact zone build) In a drawn zone over Paradise, 9,074 of 9,075 FEMA USA Structures footprints were imaged on or before 2018-09-10 and lie inside the CAMP perimeter (alarm 2018-11-08). Every count reads as current; nothing says the buildings burned two months after the imagery. Guard: print the footprints' IMAGE_DATE range on every count, and flag any historic perimeter whose ALARM_DATE is later than the newest IMAGE_DATE in the zone — a test on YEAR_ alone returns 0 for a fire in the imagery's own year (it missed CAMP). The historic-perimeter release is annual, so the current season must come from the live perimeter layers.

An occupancy class that is not the class its name says. [data] (2026-10-21, impact zone build) USA Structures OCC_CLS='Residential' contains PRIM_OCC Temporary Lodging, Nursing Home and Institutional Dormitory, and Residential × Unclassified is 2,241 of 9,075 footprints (25 %) in Paradise and absent from a Monterey zone. Mapping on OCC_CLS alone puts a hotel among "single residences". Guard: crosswalk on the (OCC_CLS, PRIM_OCC) pair, give the residential-type-unknown rows their own line rather than splitting them, keep Unclassified visible, and make the live suite fail on any pair the table does not map (a statewide re-run found 'Veterinary/Pet ', with a trailing space).

An edit stamp that moves without the data moving. [data] (2026-10-21, impact zone build) The interagency current-perimeters layer advanced dataLastEditDate three times in 25 minutes while California's rows and their newest poly_DateCurrent did not change — the layer is national, and another state's edit moves the stamp. A freshness dot driven by the stamp reads "updated a minute ago" over a day-old perimeter. Guard: time of record from the row's own date field (poly_DateCurrent), within your scope; the service stamp is at most a liveness hint. (See also the feed is up but the number is old.)

A stated rebuild schedule is not a stamp — classify each layer's stamp as refresh or on change by two reads. [data] (2026-10-22, data compact build) - Symptom: Cal OES's evacuation aggregation states "fully updated every 5 minutes", yet its edit stamp sat on 2026-10-19 14:51Z across reads on 21 and 22 October. A freshness rule built from the statement calls it two days late. CHHS's daily open-shelter layer, by contrast, writes its stamp at every 07:00 PT rebuild even with 0 rows — delivery is provable and empty is a state. - Cause: an aggregator that rewrites only on change cannot be told apart, from outside, from one that ran and found nothing new. (A stated cadence can also be missed: NIFC's WFIGS item does state "refreshed from IRWIN every 5 minutes" further down its description than a two-hit regex reads.) The interagency incident-locations stamp (WFIGS) ticked ~10 min by day on the 21st but sat still 06:05 → 06:22Z overnight on the 22nd — a "refresh" stamp is not guaranteed to move either. - Guard: take two reads at least one claimed interval apart (the live suite waits 16 min) and assert movement only on feeds measured to refresh (here the Caltrans LCS file and the outage query); show an on-change layer as in force · cadence not verifiable, never stale; report an unreliable ticker as an observation, not a pass.

A record that never publishes an end — fire-management declarations carry no incidentEndDate. [data] (2026-10-22, data compact build) - Symptom: every California FM declaration since 2025 (9 of 9 on 2026-10-21) has incidentEndDate null, while the EM and DR rows carry one. An app that waits for the end to compute a stand-down never stands down. - Guard: treat "no end" as a designed state — take the date by hand, label it MANUAL, derive every window from it, and say how many listed declarations of that type carry an end ("none of the 9 FM declarations listed carries one"). It is a property of the rows, not of the type: in a 24-month window, FM-5545-CA and FM-5548-CA (Nov–Dec 2024) do carry an end — so derive the sentence from the list, never write "FM declarations carry no end" as a rule.

A cumulative layer counts history as current. [data] (2026-10-22, data compact build) CAL FIRE's DINS master holds every structure inspected since 2013, so "inspections in the declared county" read 1,282 for Calaveras with no inspection this year (newest incident 2025-11-23). Guard: filter on the event's own start date (INCIDENTSTARTDATE >= DATE '<declaration incidentBeginDate>') and print the older count apart ("1,282 older ones not counted"); the live suite asserts the cumulative count exceeds the filtered one.

A standard's element names, sketched from memory, are not the standard's. [app] (2026-10-22, data compact build) The recipe's EDXL-RM RequestInformation sketch used SenderID and DateTimeSent — EDXL-DE envelope names that do not occur in the EDXL-RM 1.0 (errata) text — and omitted OriginatingMessageID, which §3.8.3 says MUST be present. RM's names are SentDateTime and ContactInformation (rm:ContactRole Sender). Guard: keep the emitted element list in code (EDXL_RM_ELEMENTS), have the live suite fetch the OASIS text and find every name in it, and have the offline suite fail on any element not in the list.

A "publicly owned utilities" layer mixes wires utilities with CCAs and joint powers authorities, and one row is typed as investor-owned. [data] (2026-10-21, lifeline board build) - Symptom: California's non-investor-owned electric service territory layer (Cal OES, CEC-derived, edited 2024-08-28) was used to ask which counties are served only by the utilities that report to the outage feed. Treating every row as a distribution utility produced the wrong set. - Cause: the layer holds municipal and irrigation-district utilities alongside community choice aggregators (Type = 'CCA', which buy power but own no wires) and joint powers authorities. One row inside it is typed IOU. Acronym fields are "", " " or null. - Guard: use an explicit reporting list matched on names, not on the Type field. Exclude CCAs as not distribution. Treat everything else as outside the feed. Make the rule conservative on purpose: a wrong exclusion should only withhold a favourable answer, never create one. Print the layer's edit date beside every result that depends on it.

4 · Geometry and measurement

ONE bad coordinate poisons a service's own published extent, and any viewpoint computed from it opens on the ocean. [data] [app] (2026-09-07, facilities & space utilisation) The federal inventory's California building extent is published as xmin -124.24 … xmax -3.697 — across the Atlantic — because one row of 13,001 carries longitude -3.696511. LAX TERMINAL 4, reported by Homeland Security with a County_Name of LOS ANGELES, is plotted in Madrid, and the error is in the source rather than in a projection: the geometry agrees exactly with the row's own Latitude/Longitude columns, so every consistency check passes. A viewpoint computed from returnExtentOnly centres the map at longitude -64. Guard: never compute an initial viewpoint from a service extent. Clip to a stated envelope, and then — this is the half that is easy to get wrong — keep the row in every count and every total, rendering it as coordinate out of range rather than dropping it. A silently dropped row makes the counts stop reconciling, which is a worse bug than a building in the wrong country.

A percentile's COHORT RULE can move the published figure more than the data does. [data] @@STAMP@@ On the same 20,157 rows carrying three stage dates, requiring only that the certificate follow the entitlement gave n = 19,826, p90 = 1,874 days; requiring the full monotonic order — entitlement ≤ permit ≤ certificate — gave n = 17,997, p90 = 2,118 days. A 244-day difference from a choice, not from a measurement, and neither rule is wrong: the first keeps projects whose permit is mis-dated but whose certificate is not, the second discards them. Guard: any published percentile must carry its cohort rule and the count each rule excludes, on screen, or the reader cannot check it. Print the alternative rule's figure beside it, exactly as the already-catalogued one-table-two-readings entry requires for weightings.

A wrong date epoch shifts absolute dates and leaves every INTERVAL untouched — so an epoch test that expects "every figure to move" hides a real bug. [app] @@STAMP@@ The stage dates in this return are Strings holding 1900-epoch spreadsheet serials, and the recipe's own verification step asked the builder to change the epoch to 1900-01-01 and confirm that "every figure moves by two days". It does not. Every lag in the application is a subtraction of two serials, so a constant offset cancels exactly; only an absolute date on screen could move, and this app prints none. A build that trusted the stated check would have found it "failing" and gone looking for a defect that was not there — or worse, "fixed" the arithmetic until the check passed. Guard: state what an epoch actually controls before testing it. Assert the two properties separately: that a shifted epoch moves a rendered date by the offset, and that it leaves a rendered interval identical. Also worth recording: the serial-to-date convention that puts Excel serial 61 at 1900-03-01 (past the phantom 1900-02-29) is 1899-12-30 + serial, and two of the recipe's own worked examples were off by one and by twelve days against it.

A centroid-in-polygon test and an areal intersection give opposite readings of the same site. [app] (2026-09-02, land bank & acquisition screening) Measured against a real regulatory zone: a 225.46-acre footprint straddling a delineated Alquist-Priolo earthquake fault zone has 49.62 acres (22.0 %) inside and 175.84 acres outside — while the centroid test returns True, implying the whole site is constrained.

footprint      =   225.46 acres
inside AP zone =    49.62 acres
outside        =   175.84 acres
share inside   =    22.0 %
centroid-in-zone verdict = True

The centroid says call a consultant; the fraction says the building goes there. Every product that answers hazard questions at an address point produces the first reading.

Guard: where a decision depends on HOW MUCH of a site is affected, intersect and report the fraction — and reproject to an equal-area CRS first. An area taken in degrees is a function of latitude and is not an area. This build reprojects to EPSG:3310 (California Albers) before any area is taken, and the whole computation is dependency-free: an ellipsoidal Albers forward transform plus an exact polygon-intersection area by signed triangle fans, which handles holes and non-convex rings without a general clipping library. One trap inside it: Sutherland–Hodgman keeps the half-plane to the LEFT of each directed edge, so a clockwise clip triangle keeps the complement and silently returns nothing. Normalise the clip orientation, or every intersection quietly measures zero.

Shape__Length on a Web-Mercator layer is not ground distance. [data] It is Mercator metres, inflated by 1/cos(latitude). A network measured 12,042,671 m (7,483 mi) against a true geodesic 6,093.1 mi — a 21 % "discrepancy" that is pure projection. Never report mileage from Shape__Length on a 102100 layer.

Everything must be EPSG:4326. Reproject on the way in (-t_srs EPSG:4326).

Generalised geometry is for drawing only. [data] Drawing tolerance (~0.0005° ≈ 55 m) is correct for paint. Never measure on it.

The geocoder's default output SR is Web Mercator. [data] Omit outSR and you get x: -13162802 — metres. Fed to a 4326 map it lands in the Gulf of Guinea. The response declares its own spatialReference, so this is silent only if you don't read it.

A computed ring does not close itself — floating point will not do it for you. [app] Sweeping i = 0 … steps and taking sin(2π) yields -2.4e-16, not 0, so the last vertex misses the first by an epsilon and the polygon is technically open. Some consumers accept it, some reject it, and the failure is invisible on screen. Guard: generate steps vertices and push a copy of the first as the last. Assert the two are identical, not merely close. (nearby)

For CLASSIFICATION, do not parent holes at all — use even-odd across every ring. [app] (2026-08-20, asset-level exposure scoring) The two entries above fix an Esri→GeoJSON converter, and they are right for drawing. For answering is this point inside? there is a simpler and stronger reading. Measured on California's statutory 200-year floodplain (2 features, 1,247 rings = 183 exterior + 1,064 holes) against 2,000 sample points, one ring array gives three answers:

Reading Points inside
union every ring as its own polygon 45
parent each hole to the most recent exterior 42
even-odd across all rings, orientation ignored 41 — equals the server, exactly

Even-odd needs no orientation test and no containment test, so neither can be got wrong: a point inside a hole crosses its exterior once and the hole once, which is even, which is outside. The failure the middle row hides is precise — hole 821 belongs to exterior 795, but exterior 818 sits between them in the array, so most-recent parenting hangs it on the wrong polygon and it never subtracts. Guard: classify with even-odd over the whole ring array; parent by containment only for the GeoJSON you hand to a renderer. And assert by consequence: classify the same sample locally and server-side with esriSpatialRelIntersects, and fail the build on a one-feature disagreement. That gate caught exactly one asset in 2,000 — 106 m from the nearest edge, so not even a boundary case.

A GeoJSON ring repeats its first vertex as its last. [app] Averaging it twice drags every centroid toward that vertex — on a square, [4,4] instead of [5,5].

Snapping band edges to vertices manufactures states that must never be manufactured. [app] Two abutting unevaluated polygons each lost their shared edge, drawing a hairline "evaluated, and not zoned" sliver — painting an authority looked and found nothing onto ground nobody has looked at. Guard: refine edges by bisection; reproduce an independent geodesic clip in the suite.

A bare except in a measurement script is data corruption that reports success. [app] A probe silently swallowed 5 of 13 polygons that failed validity, publishing 53 of 91 miles when the truth was 91.05 of 91.05 — 100 %. The figure reached the recipe, the design proposal and the catalogue before the app disagreed with it.

A multipart feature has no single linear axis. [app] A third of one network is discontinuous, so any milepost ruler drawn straight across it is fiction. Guard: break the axis at the gap, label the gap in real units, and never interpolate across it.

Overlay throws "side location conflict" on invalid geometry. [data] GEOS/Turf refuse self-intersecting rings. make_valid before every intersection — and see §4's rule on bare excepts: swallowing the throw is worse than the crash.

A long geometry exceeds the shell's ARG_MAX. [data] A 1,352-vertex polyline cannot go in a query string. POST it as a form body.

Esri ring orientation is the whole of an Esri→GeoJSON converter, and inverting it is silent. [app] (2026-08-17, climate-risk regulatory reporting) In Esri JSON an outer ring is clockwise and a hole is counter-clockwise; with the standard shoelace formula clockwise is negative, so area > 0 is the hole, not the outer ring. Getting that backwards does not throw and looks perfectly correct on a single-ring polygon — it turns every subsequent part of a multipart feature into a hole cut out of the first part. The features still draw, the feature count still reconciles against returnCountOnly, and only a point-in-polygon measurement against a second source shows it: on CAL FIRE's FHSZ surfaces it put 1,000+ of 9,129 census-tract points "outside" a hazard surface that covers them, in the flattering direction. Guard: assert orientation by consequence, not by reading the code — for every polygon with holes, check the hole's first vertex is inside its outer ring, and fail if more than a handful are not.

…and a hole belongs to the ring that CONTAINS it, not to the one that came last. [app] (2026-08-17, climate-risk regulatory reporting) Esri emits the rings of a multipart polygon in no guaranteed order, so "attach each hole to the most recent outer ring" punches a void through a part the hole has nothing to do with. After the orientation fix above, 175 of 9,658 holes were still mis-assigned, one of them into an 814-ring Very High polygon. Guard: assign each hole to the smallest outer ring that contains it, and keep an orphan as its own part rather than dropping geometry. The containment check in the previous entry catches both mistakes.

Generalise to draw, but measure the drawing copy and you have moved the answer. [app] (2026-08-17, climate-risk regulatory reporting) A coverage measurement run against the display copies of two seismic layers instead of the full-resolution ones moved every class boundary by the generalisation tolerance. Re-run at full resolution the same code reproduced an independently-published measurement to the dollar. Guard: name the full-resolution file and the drawing copy differently (x.geojson / x.display.geojson), and have the measurement script say in its header which it reads.

A population-weighted centre is not a centroid, and a BOM is not nothing. [app] (2026-08-18, branch & ATM network operations) Two ways to get a coverage figure that is plausible and wrong. First: the published distance conventions measure from a tract's population-weighted centre, which the census publishes as its own file; substituting the geometric centroid moves the measuring point into empty land and silently changes who counts as outside the band, with no error anywhere. Second: that file is UTF-8 with a BOM (EF BB BF), so a naive CSV reader names the first column with a leading U+FEFF — the printed header looks perfect and every join on it returns nothing. Guard: read it as utf-8-sig, use the weighted centre, and assert the published state total before computing anything (California 2020: 39,538,223 across 9,129 tracts, and the file reconciles with diff 0). An assertion that reconciles to a published total is the cheapest proof a pipeline is right.

A three-way standard with no reproducible three-way classifier moves the answer by 3×. [app] (2026-08-18, branch & ATM network operations) The same arithmetic, the same 5,384 points and the same 9,129 tracts, under two honest readings of one published convention: a tract-level urban/mixed/rural flag gives 317 tracts / 1,149,470 people beyond the distance band; a county-level metropolitan/micropolitan classification gives 926 tracts / 3,568,904. Neither is a mistake — the convention names urban / suburban / rural and no published classifier produces exactly those three. Guard: pick one classifier, print it on screen and in every export, and never put a control in the app that changes it — an app whose own headline depends on a standard must not let its user move the standard.

The GeoJSON crs member is back, and it lies by omission. [data] (2026-08-23, climate scenario explorer) RFC 7946 removed the crs member, so most readers ignore it — and one API returns EPSG:4326 from /locagrid/ and EPSG:3857 from /states/ and /counties/, declaring each only in that member. A pipeline that loads both and assumes one CRS treats metres as degrees; the failure is silent, and the symptom is a mask that returns zero features, which looks exactly like a filter bug. Guard: when a GeoJSON response carries a crs member, read it; when it does not, assert the first coordinate is plausibly degrees. Then assert the expected feature count after masking — a mask that returns 0 must fail loudly, not render an empty map.

A published cell layer is not necessarily a faithful footprint of the raster it describes. [data] (2026-08-23, climate scenario explorer) The publisher's own grid layer holds 26,517 polygons against 26,518 non-nodata raster cells: at one latitude two raster cells have no polygon and one polygon has no cell, offset by half a cell. The polygons key on a centre-coordinate string with no row/column index, and the numeric ids are sparse. Guard: rebuild the cell boxes from the raster's own affine (origin, pixel size, width, height) rather than joining to the published footprint. The affected row here was outside the study area, which is precisely why looking at it would never have caught it.

Stepping single model-years makes a climate signal non-monotonic, and the app then reports a re-convergence that did not happen. [app] (2026-08-23, climate scenario explorer) On single annual rasters the share of cells differing between two pathways ran 7.00 % (2030) → 6.26 % → 8.96 % → 4.65 % (2045) → 6.82 %, because one year carries interannual variability, not a climate. A horizon stepper built on those years tells a risk officer the pathways converge in the 2040s. On 30-year centred climatological windows the same measurement rises monotonically at every step (3.45 % → 30.93 %). Guard: step windows, not years; record n_years per window and print the truncation where the series ends short (here 2085 is a 29-year window, because the data stops at 2099).

Before comparing two model scenarios, count the members on each side. [data] (2026-08-23, climate scenario explorer) One published downscaling carries 33 / 62 / 34 model-members across three SSPs, with a different model missing from each and one model contributing ten members to one pathway and none to another. An unqualified "SSP2-4.5 vs SSP3-7.0" ensemble mean over them compares who is in the ensemble as much as the forcing. Guard: fix a common model-and-member set across both sides, print it, and treat "the ensembles are not comparable as published" as a design requirement rather than a caveat.

A count of features that changed class is a function of the breaks as much as of the data, and it can FALL while the underlying difference rises. [app] (2026-08-23, climate scenario explorer) With a fixed break set, the moved-cell share ran 30.55 % (2040) → 24.97 % (2045) → 24.63 % → 31.44 % while the continuous gap rose monotonically — because both sides crossed a shared break together. Guard: publish the break set beside every change count, render the continuous difference next to it, and never let the count travel into an export alone. A movement count without its breaks is not a reproducible number.

A difference that is smaller than the disagreement inside either thing being compared is not a finding — and reporting it as one is the defect. [app] (2026-08-23, climate scenario explorer) Across 10,667 cells at twelve horizons, the gap between two emissions pathways never exceeded the spread between the 32 models inside a single pathway: 5.4 % of that spread at the near horizon, 48.4 % at the far one, and 0.00 % of cells exceeding it anywhere. An app that had shown class-movement counts without the spread would have published model noise as a scenario result. Guard: wherever a comparison has a measurable internal spread, make the ratio difference / spread a first-class figure on the page and in the export — and let it, not the raw count, carry the verdict.

esriSpatialRelContains and esriSpatialRelWithin are expressed from the INPUT geometry's side. [data] (2026-08-23, property peril report) To ask "which polygons contain this parcel" the relation is esriSpatialRelWithin. esriSpatialRelContains returned an empty set for every parcel, on both an ArcGIS Online hosted layer and an ArcGIS Server MapServer. Nothing errored. In an app that distinguishes wholly inside a zone from the boundary crosses this parcel, that silently inverted the more consequential of the two on every single reading. Guard: verify the relation against a feature you already know contains your geometry, before trusting either name.

One spatial query can return 46 MB. [data] (2026-08-23, property peril report) A 5 km envelope over Sacramento against a statewide dam-failure inundation surface returned 45,973,504 bytes and timed out at 120 s; geometryPrecision=6 only brought it to 37 MB. The polygons are few but enormous, so a feature count is no guard at all. Guard: put a deadline on the geometry fetch and have a fallback that never downloads geometry — ask the service for the verdict (spatialRel=Intersects, returnGeometry=false) and let it measure distance for you with buffered distance + units + returnCountOnly probes. Each probe is a few bytes however large the polygons are, and the distance is computed by the service in its own projection rather than in degree space.

Bisect a radius in parallel: the predicate is monotone. [app] (2026-08-23, property peril report) "Is there a feature within R?" is monotone in R, so a whole ladder of radii can be fired together and the answer read off the first true — one round trip instead of nine. Two further parallel rounds of seven evenly spaced probes narrow the bracket 64x. What comes back is a bracket, not a point value, and the reading must say so and print a tolerance. Bound the whole thing with a budget: a fallback that never returns is worse than one that reports it could not measure.

A failed measurement must never render as a finding of absence. [app] (2026-08-23, property peril report) When the fallback measurement failed, the row printed "no differing boundary within 5 km" — which reads as a result. An error and an answer must never be indistinguishable on screen. Guard: carry the failure as its own state ({failed: true}) all the way to the render, and word it as "could not be measured — the service did not answer".

Full-resolution geometry can be two orders of magnitude larger than the same features drawn. [data] (2026-08-24, shortage-area explorer) 215 California HPSA perimeter polygons are 23.35 MB at native resolution and 0.19 MB at maxAllowableOffset=0.005 (≈550 m) — 120×, with all 215 features intact. Over a hospital or agency network the raw payload makes first paint unusable, and nothing in the service metadata warns you: maxRecordCount counts records, not vertices, so a layer with a small record count can still be enormous. Guard: the house default full resolution to measure, generalised to draw is load-bearing, not advisory. Request a drawing tolerance whenever the app does not measure, assert the generalised payload's size in the live suite, and state the tolerance on screen — a reader who does not know the boundaries were simplified may take an edge literally.

A national bounding box that spans the antimeridian fits the WHOLE WORLD. [data] (2026-08-24, housing & homelessness services) The US Continuum of Care footprint includes American Samoa at −170° and the Northern Marianas at +145°, so its true bounding box is −179.9 → +179.9. fitBounds on it gives a view of the entire planet with no region legible on it — while every non-visual assertion (388 in scope, 387 drawn, the correct totals, the correct cap message) stays green. One screenshot made it obvious and nothing else could have. Guard: for any national footprint with offshore territories, fit the contiguous extent and say on screen which in-scope features lie outside the opening view. Applies to the US, France, Spain, Portugal, the Netherlands, New Zealand, Chile and Ecuador at minimum — never derive an opening viewport from min/max longitude without asking whether the set crosses ±180°.

NAD83 geographic (4269) on an ArcGIS Online feature service. [data] (2026-08-24, housing & homelessness services) extent.spatialReference.wkid is 4269 — neither 4326 nor 3857, the two values a reader checks for. Layers land offset from every sibling, and Shape__Area / Shape__Length are in the source CRS and mean nothing measured. Guard: read the wkid rather than assuming one of the two usual values, request outSR=4326 unconditionally on every query, and never measure a Shape__* field.

returnCentroid=true works on layers that do not advertise it — and honours the layer's SR, not yours. [data] (2026-08-25, public health preparedness) A tract layer reported supportsReturnGeometryCentroid = None (and supportsPagination = None), yet returnCentroid=true was honoured on every one of 9,109 rows. Two traps, in opposite directions: a build that trusts the capability flag hand-computes centroids it never needed to; and a build that trusts the parameter but forgets outSR gets the centroid back in the layer's own Web Mercator — roughly (-13179351, 4050474) for every row — which, fed to a nearest-site assignment, puts every tract in the state at the same distance from the same site and produces a plausible, uniform, entirely wrong answer. Guard: try the parameter regardless of the flag, and always set outSR=4326; then assert the returned x lies inside the expected lon range before using a single one of them.

A ZIP-code cell frame costs four things, and all four are measurable before you draw it. [data] (2026-09-03, transaction evidence) Binding a postal code as an analysis cell, against the Census ZIP Code Tabulation Areas: 60 situs strings resolved to 55 codes (five codes carried two place names each, so a place-keyed cell would have double-counted); one code had no tabulation area at all, so a GEOID IN (…) list of 55 returned 54 and the missing cell would have vanished rather than shown; seven of the 54 straddled the county line — esriSpatialRelIntersects returned 54 against esriSpatialRelContains 47 — so an unclipped cell colours ground the data never covered; and a postal code is a mail-delivery construct, not an area, which a reader will take for a market boundary unless told.

Guard: run the IN list against the frame and the frame against the data's own coverage boundary before committing to the geography. Clip every cell to the coverage boundary, render the un-drawable cell as a labelled state that still counts toward every total, and say on screen what the substitute geography costs.

A polygon clipper that weaves each intersection into the lists it is still testing against corrupts the very edges it is walking — and reports success. [app] (2026-09-03, transaction evidence) Seven of 54 ZIP Code Tabulation Areas straddle the county line and must be clipped to it, because the transfer file stops at the boundary and an unclipped cell colours ground the data never measured. A textbook Greiner–Hormann implementation found intersections and spliced them into the subject and clip linked lists inside the same loop, so every later edge test read an endpoint that was now an intersection node rather than the original vertex. The result: one cell returned 100 % unclipped, one at negative area, three empty — and the function returned ok for all seven, because the algorithm's own success condition (an even crossing count, a closed trace) was still satisfied. Cause: phase 1 of the algorithm is defined over the ORIGINAL edges; mutating the lists during it is not an optimisation, it is a different algorithm. Guard, in two parts. Collect every intersection first as plain data — {subjectEdge, t, clipEdge, u, point} — and build both lists afterwards from that. Then verify the output geometrically instead of trusting the return value: every vertex of a clipped ring must lie inside the clip polygon or within epsilon of its boundary, the clipped area must be strictly between zero and the original, and a polygon the clip does not touch must come back byte-identical. Measured here, the worst stray across all seven cells was 7.1 x 10⁻¹⁵ degrees. Detect degenerate intersections (a parameter within 1e-9 of a vertex — two layers built on a shared TIGER edge base produce them) and nudge the subject by a fraction of a millimetre and retry, rather than emitting a shape whose wrongness nothing downstream can see.

A drawn ring and its stated radius are two different boundaries, and a feature can sit between them. [app] (2026-09-03, tenant mix & catchment) A "6.0 mi straight-line buffer" was drawn as an inscribed 64-segment polygon and then used for three jobs at once: painting the ring, selecting the peer set inside it, and intersecting tracts to apportion households. An inscribed polygon is smaller than its circle by a sagitta of r x (1 - cos(pi/n)) — 11.63 m at n = 64 on a 6-mile radius — and two of 1,919 derived centres fell in that gap, 6.4 m and 8.4 m inside the true circle and outside the drawn one. Selecting by distance gave 64 peers; selecting by the drawn polygon gave 63. The recipe being built had itself recorded 64 peers (selected by radius) beside an apportionment measured against the drawn polygon, so its own two numbers disagreed by exactly those two centres and neither figure was wrong on its own terms.

Guard: have one catchment object, and draw it finely enough that the drawn edge and the stated definition are the same boundary. At n = 256 the sagitta is 0.73 m, below the coordinate precision of the register itself; the drawn area moves from 292.45 km2 to 292.89 km2 against a true 292.92 km2. Then assert the sagitta directly in the suite — not the equal-area radius, which is smaller and understates the gap. The general rule: if a polygon is both the picture and the predicate, its resolution is a correctness parameter, not a drawing one.

DBSCAN can return a cluster smaller than min_samples, and that is correct behaviour, not a bug. [app] (2026-09-03, tenant mix & catchment) Clustering 47,394 points at eps 150 m with min_samples = 5 produced 19 clusters of 3 or 4 members out of 1,919. The reasoning that says the minimum must be 5 — a core point plus its four neighbours — omits border-point stealing: a core point's neighbours may already belong to an earlier cluster, and they are not reassigned, so the cluster that core point seeds can be smaller than the threshold that made it a core point. Guard: do not "correct" small clusters or raise min_samples to remove them, and do not assert a floor of min_samples on cluster size. Assert the reproduced distribution instead. Where the clusters are an analytical unit, this matters twice: those 19 centres fell below every published size band, and the honest handling is a labelled "below the published bands" class that still counts toward the denominator.

Shape.STArea() is in the storage projection's units, which need not be the units the declared extent implies. [data] (2026-09-07, valuation & taxation base map) A county parcel layer declares latestWkid: 3857 in its extent and returns Shape.STArea() in square feet on a State Plane foot projection. Read as Web Mercator square metres, every area in the application would have been wrong by a factor of about ten — and a latitude correction would then have been applied that does not belong. The test that settles it is latitude invariance: the ratio of the published area to the true ground area held constant to within 0.001 — about 10.737, near the 10.7639 square feet in a square metre — from latitude 33.83 to 34.63, where a Web Mercator reading would have varied by roughly 2 % across the same range. Guard: measure two features far apart in latitude and compare the ratio before trusting any published area column. A constant ratio proves a projected unit; a ratio that tracks 1/cos²(lat) proves Web Mercator. Never take the unit from the extent's spatial reference — it describes how the extent is expressed, not how the geometry is stored.

A published centroid column can contain Null Island. [data] (2026-09-07, valuation & taxation base map) 26 of 2.37 M parcels publish CENTER_LAT / CENTER_LON of exactly (0, 0); every other row is populated and in range, so nothing about the column's completeness or its type gives it away. An aggregate that averages those centroids without a bounds gate drags a whole group's position into the Gulf of Guinea, and the group is then drawn there rather than dropped — which is worse, because it looks like data. Guard: gate published coordinates to the layer's own extent before aggregating, and count what the gate dropped, because a dropped row is a statement. Here the gate cost exactly one assessor map page its position out of 87,634 — a number worth printing rather than a number worth hiding.

A drawing tolerance is not a measurement frame: maxAllowableOffset geometry answers a different question, and the answer looks reasonable. [app] (2026-09-07, property hazard exposure) The app fetched hazard-zone polygons with maxAllowableOffset — correct, for painting — and then ran point-in-polygon against the same features to count buildings inside the zone. It reported 9,425 of 9,429 buildings in the frame against a true statewide population of 710. A generalised polygon covers ground the real one does not, so a point outside the zone falls inside its simplified outline. Every suite was green and every figure was internally consistent, because the arithmetic was right about the wrong shape. Guard: the house rule is full resolution to measure, generalised to draw — make it structural. Have the fetch return a flag saying whether a tolerance was applied, refuse to compute against a generalised read, and withhold the count with its reason at zoom levels where full resolution is too expensive. "Not counted at this zoom, and here is why" is a correct answer; a plausible number is not.

A membership test that accepts every class a layer carries will accept the class that means "none". [app] (2026-09-07, property hazard exposure) A statutory duty attaches in the State Responsibility Area at any hazard class, but in the Local Responsibility Area only at Very High. The in-frame test accepted every polygon the map had drawn — so Moderate, High and, worst, NonWildland, which is the layer's mapped finding of no wildland hazard. The result put a statutory obligation on buildings the regulation does not reach, silently, with the count still smaller than the total in view and therefore not obviously wrong. This is the known "a fourth class the sibling layer does not have" trap committed in the arithmetic rather than in the renderer, where nobody is looking for it. Guard: write the qualifying predicate as its own named function derived from the rule, not from the layer's class list; unit-test it against every distinct value the layer publishes, including the one that means absence; and never let "what the map drew" stand in for "what the rule covers".

A point-in-polygon that tests only the outer ring counts holes as inside. [app] (2026-09-07, property hazard exposure) inRing(pt, rings[0]) on each polygon put buildings standing in unzoned enclaves inside the hazard frame. It was caught only because the in-view count reached 726 against a statewide population of 710 — an impossibility the app could state, and would not otherwise have noticed, since the error is a few per cent and every figure stays plausible. Guard: test the outer ring, then every interior ring, and reject a point that falls in any of them; handle Polygon and MultiPolygon nesting explicitly rather than indexing to a fixed depth. And derive a population bound during research so the application has something it can contradict. (2026-10-21, lifeline board build) [data] The same mistake in an area share, made by the reference tool. - Symptom: a research script measured utility-territory shares of each county in EPSG:3310 with shapely. It built every territory polygon from its rings as if each ring were an outer ring, then unioned them. The Los Angeles Department of Water & Power came out at 11.83 % of Los Angeles County. - Cause: the territory has hole rings. With the holes kept, shapely gives 11.519 %, and the app's grid-sampled share gives 11.54 %. The filled-ring figure was the one written into the spec, so the app's correct answer first looked like the defect. - Guard: build reference polygons with interior rings as holes (parent each hole to its shell, or use even-odd, as in §4). When a build check compares against a reference value, say which hole rule the reference used, and print both values for any polygon that has holes.

NWS alerts almost never carry a geometry — ask by point, and remember what disappeared. [data] (2026-10-21, incident situation map build) Of 256 California alerts from api.weather.gov over seven days, 254 had geometry: null — and so did all 6 active ones. An app that draws alert polygons draws a nearly empty map and reads as "no warnings". The zone-based alerts are located through their zones (see the geocode.SAME entry in §3), and for "is this place under an alert?" the API answers directly: /alerts/active?point=lat,lon. Separately, a cancelled alert simply vanishes from /alerts/active, so a display that only renders the current list cannot say "no longer in effect" — it just loses a row. Times arrive with mixed local offsets. Guard: query by point (or by zone/SAME) rather than relying on polygons; keep the last-seen set and label a departed alert "no longer in effect" with its last-seen time; normalise every time to UTC before comparing or sorting, and display in one stated zone.

Divided highways are drawn once per carriageway, so a summed length doubles. [data] (2026-10-21, impact zone build) Caltrans' State Highway Network carries each direction of a route as its own line (Direction NB/SB, AlignCode Right/Left): Route 1 through a buffered perimeter read 15.014 km each way, 30.028 km summed. The functional-classification layer does the same with SHS_…._P / ._S route ids. Guard: report route-km — per route, the mean of its per-direction clipped lengths — and drop the ._S side of state-highway rows from centreline totals; say on screen that the network is undated.

Buffering and self-intersection checks on a published perimeter cost seconds, and the perimeter is already invalid. [app] [data] (2026-10-21, impact zone build) A real fire perimeter (12 parts, ~10,500 vertices) took Turf ~22 s to buffer by 1 km, and turf.kinks took 15 s to report that the published outline has 6 self-intersections. Guard: simplify the core to ~1 % of the buffer distance before buffering, then union back the exact core (area change < 0.05 %; record the tolerance on the zone); refuse self-intersection only for operator-drawn or imported geometry, and take a published footprint as published.

"Intersects" a utility territory is not "served by" it: boundary slivers. [data] (2026-10-21, lifeline board build) - Symptom: a server-side esriSpatialRelIntersects test of the California electric service territories (Cal OES's CEC-derived investor-owned and publicly-owned layers, edited 2024-08-28) against each county reported the Los Angeles Department of Water & Power inside Fresno County. The overlap is a 0.016 % boundary sliver. Other counties had slivers of the same kind, at 0.002 % and 0.005 %. - Cause: two independently digitised boundary sets never share edges exactly, so intersects is true for almost every neighbour. - Guard: use an area share with a stated floor (equal-area projection or grid sampling; this build used 0.5 %), computed at build time and printed beside any claim that depends on it. Re-measure any share near the floor at a finer resolution. Solano / City of Pittsburg measured 0.498 %, within 0.002 pt of the floor, where grid noise alone flips the answer between runs. A grid-sampled share also needs a minimum sample count: a small target (a little water system) under a coarse step holds a handful of points, so re-sample it to ≥ 2,000 rather than report "no samples". The step must be fine enough to meet the check's tolerance (bbox/200 missed a 0.25-pt check by resolution alone; bbox/400 met it).

Co-location is not dependency: a cascade chained by intersection explodes. [data] (2026-10-21, lifeline board build) - Symptom: a one-customer power outage area intersects the service area of a 545,970-person water system (State Water Board service areas). The overlap is 0.005 % of the service area. Chained naively (outage → water system → hospitals inside its service area), that one customer "reaches" nine hospitals, and a population sum over the hop says half a million people are affected. - Cause: geographic co-location is one of the CISA dependency kinds, and the weakest. An intersection says nothing about which feeder serves which pump. - Guard: compute an overlap share (the share of the downstream area inside the upstream one) and tier it (e.g. large ≥ 25 % · partial 5–25 % · touches < 5 %). Chain the next hop only under large/partial; under touches, count the downstream items without listing them. Never sum a population across a hop. Label every edge inferred — validate with the operator.

A bounding box is not the county. [data] (2026-10-22, data compact build) Counting "in the declared county" with the county's envelope overstated every layer tried: HCAI facilities 22 vs 5 (polygon), DINS inspections 1,604 vs 1,282, WFIGS incidents 2 vs 1. Guard: intersect the county polygon (esriGeometryPolygon, inSR=4326, by POST — §2 a GET over ~2,048 characters) and never a box; the live suite asserts box > polygon on two layers so the guard's reason stays on record.

5 · Services and hosts

A SERVICE name can be misspelled while the LAYER inside it is not, so the natural URL 400s. [data] (2026-09-07, portfolio & asset register) CAL FIRE serves FHSALRA25_v1_All (FHSA) containing a layer that reports its own name as FHSZLRA25_v1_All (FHSZ). Building the URL from the layer name — the obvious move, and the one a catalogue entry or a colleague's note invites — returns {"error":{"code":400,"message":"Invalid URL"}}, and so does every plausible correction (FHSZLRA25, FHSZ_LRA_25). Nothing in the layer document points back at the service that carries it. Guard: enumerate the server root and read the name it publishes; never infer a service name from a layer name, and record the service path in the recipe rather than the layer's self-description.

A service can carry your category's name and be a superseded vintage. [data] (2026-09-07, portfolio & asset register) A CAL FIRE service called "FHSZ FOR REAL ESTATE INSPECTIONS" sits on the same server as the current hazard layers, looks purpose-built for a real-estate recipe, and its Local Responsibility Area half is a 2007–2011 map superseded across every local jurisdiction by the 2025 adopted maps. Guard: a name is not a vintage. Read the vintage, and where a look-alike is rejected, name it in the recipe so the next reader does not rediscover it as a find.

A keyless tile host can answer HTTP 403 to a request that carries no User-Agent, so a server-side probe reports a perfectly healthy basemap as dead. [data] (2026-09-07, portfolio & asset register) tiles.openfreemap.org served its style, its TileJSON, its glyphs and its sprite to curl and to a browser, and returned 403 Forbidden to Python's urllib, whose default agent string is Python-urllib/3.12. A browser always sends a User-Agent; a server-side fetch does not unless told to, and several federal file hosts behave the same way. The failure is indistinguishable from a gated host, an outage, or a blocked network — and it arrives at exactly the moment a §4 probe is deciding whether a basemap is bindable. Guard: send an honest User-Agent on every server-side fetch in a bake, a probe and a live suite, and name the tool in it. When a host 403s, re-probe with an agent string before recording it as unreachable. (OpenFreeMap has since been removed from the basemap catalog for other reasons; the lesson applies to any host.)

Two layers published by one agency, in one server, meant to be read together, can be in DIFFERENT spatial references. [data] (2026-09-07, portfolio & asset register) CAL FIRE's Fire Hazard Severity Zone layers report FHSZSRA_23_3 at wkid 3857 and FHSALRA25_v1_All at wkid 3310 (California Albers), on the same host, with identical field lists, and documented as the two halves of one map. A client that asks for one in outSR=4326 and takes the other's default gets two layers that do not overlay — and because both draw somewhere, the symptom is a hazard band in the wrong place rather than an error. Guard: read extent.spatialReference per layer, never per server, and put outSR=4326 on every request rather than on the requests you remembered.

An ArcGIS rejection arrives as HTTP 200 carrying an error whose code is 400 — so response.ok is true for a query that never ran. [data] (2026-09-03, parcel record coverage) Two recipe traps recorded a rejected predicate as "returns HTTP 400". What the wire actually carries is:

$ curl -s -o body.json -w "HTTP %{http_code}\n" \
    "$LAYER/query?where=Roll_LandBaseYear%20%3E%202000&returnCountOnly=true&f=json"
HTTP 200
$ cat body.json
{"error":{"code":400,"extendedCode":-2147220985,"message":"Unable to complete operation.","details":[]}}

The error code is 400; the status line is 200. Every if (!r.ok) throw wrapper — which is the idiom this repository's own injectable seam recommends — sails straight past it and hands the caller a body with no count in it, so the failure surfaces later as an undefined in arithmetic rather than as a network error. The details array is empty, so there is nothing to print either. This is sharper than the trap as recorded, not softer: a genuine HTTP 400 would at least have been caught. Guard: in any ArcGIS fetch helper, check the body as well as the status — throw when body.error is present, naming body.error.code and body.error.message — and when a recipe records an HTTP status for a failure, re-observe it with curl -w "%{http_code}" rather than inferring it from the error object inside the body.

A 4326 distance query against a projected layer is rejected as an invalid SRID — unless you send outSR. [data] (2026-10-21, incident situation map build) CAL FIRE's DINS (damage inspection) master FeatureServer is stored in wkid 102962 (California Albers). A query with a 4326 point geometry, inSR=4326, distance and units returned HTTP 200 carrying {"error":{"code":400,…"24204: The spatial reference identifier (SRID) is not valid…"}} — the shape of the entry above, so a status-only client reads it as zero damaged structures near the fire. The same query with outSR=4326 added returned {"count":133} and the features. So the rejection is not "this layer cannot do 4326 buffers"; it is a missing parameter. Guard: send outSR=4326 on every query (the §4 rule), including count-only and distance queries where no geometry is returned; treat any 200 body with an error object as a failed read, never as zero rows. A fallback that avoids server-side buffering also works: an envelope query in 4326, then a haversine distance filter on the client.

An HTTP 200 is not a success — check the content type, and check for an empty body. [data] (2026-09-02, land bank & acquisition screening) A California state host serves two ArcGIS roots and only one carries the data: gispublic.waterboards.ca.gov/portalserver/ answers JSON, while .../arcgis/ answers HTTP 200 with an Incapsula bot-wall HTML body. In a hazard or contamination screen, a client that treats a parse failure as "no features" converts a transport error into a verdict of clear on ground somebody is about to buy.

Two refinements this build measured, both of which defeat the obvious guard:

  • The wall fires per PATH, not per host. On re-probe the /arcgis/rest/services root answered clean JSON five times running — 15 folders, a real ArcGIS root — while /arcgis/rest/services/USTCUF_2025/MapServer/42 on that same root still returned the Incapsula HTML. Probing the root and concluding "this host is fine" passes, and then the layer request walks into the wall.
  • The failure mode is not stable. The same wrong path has also answered HTTP 200 with a JSON {"error":{"code":404,"message":"Service not found"}}. A guard written against the HTML shape alone goes green on the day the host returns the JSON one.

Guard: assert JSON, assert a non-empty body, AND assert the payload carries what a layer document carries (fields, or features) rather than merely parsing. Surface the failure as its own visible state — "the source did not answer" — which is neither a finding nor an all-clear. A sibling entry records the same class from the other direction: HTTP 200 with a zero-byte body, one request in seven.

An unconditional query can answer HTTP 304 with an empty body. [data] (2026-10-22, live-position build) CAL FIRE's water-drop layer answered one query with 304 Not Modified and no body (11:31Z), although the request carried no If-None-Match or If-Modified-Since; three later reads of the same URL were 200. A client that treats "not an error status" as success parses nothing and shows zero rows, and one that reads 304 as "unchanged" has nothing of its own to reuse. Guard: a 304 to a request that sent no validator, or any empty body, is a failed read — keep the last good set (as for truncated JSON below) and retry on the next cycle; never read it as zero rows.

An ArcGIS JSON response can arrive truncated: HTTP 200, the right content type, and invalid JSON. [data] (2026-10-21, lifeline board build) - Symptom: a geometry query against the State Water Board's drinking-water service-area layer (several MB of polygons) returned HTTP 200, application/json, about 9.4 MB, and a body that did not parse. It stopped mid-coordinate. The same request succeeded on retry. - Cause: the transfer was cut short. The status and headers had already been sent, so nothing in them says so. It is rare and not reproducible on demand. - Guard: treat a parse failure as a failed refresh, exactly like a timeout or a 200 carrying {error}. Keep the last good payload, degrade only the freshness indicator, and retry on the next cycle. Never read a parse failure as "no features", and never let it blank a layer that was drawn a minute ago. Test it offline by serving a body cut in half through the injected fetch.

A cadence "measured" from two reads can be a sampling artefact. [data] (2026-10-21, lifeline board build) - Symptom: two reads of Cal OES's statewide power-outage layer, at 11:09 and 11:30, saw lastEditDate jump 10:56 → 11:26, and the feed was recorded as publishing every 30 min. Reads at 13:04, 13:10, 13:12 and 13:17 saw 12:56:10 → 13:11:12 → (later) 13:26:12: every 15 min. The morning reads could not see the 11:11 publish between them. - Cause: two reads further apart than the true interval measure an upper bound, not the interval. - Guard: measure cadence from consecutive changes, polling at a fraction of the claimed interval (the live suite's cadence check waits ≥ 16 min for a 15-min feed). Print the publisher's claim, if any, beside the measurement. Derive the stale threshold from the measured cadence plus any CDN cache age; here that is 2 × 15 min + 5 min cache + slack = 40 min.

Metadata and rows come from different CDN cache fills, so the layer's edit date can lag the rows. [data] (2026-10-21, lifeline board build) - Symptom: ArcGIS Online answered both ?f=json and /query on Cal OES's outage layers with Cache-Control: public, max-age=300. At 13:12 a /query count had already fallen 30 → 28 while ?f=json still reported the previous edit (12:56:10). An app that took the time of record from the layer document labelled the new rows with the old time. - Cause: each URL is cached separately, so the two responses can come from different five-minute fills. - Guard: take the time of record from the Last-Modified header of the response that carried the rows. On this host it equals editingInfo.lastEditDate to the second. Never combine the layer document's date with a separately fetched row set. State the cache age in the freshness copy ("served through a 5-min cache"). Related: the feed is up but the number is old (§3) and an edit stamp that moves without the data moving (§3). (2026-10-22, data compact build) A form-encoded POST query carries the same Last-Modified as the GET (measured on four ArcGIS Online hosts), and the header is CORS-safelisted, so a browser can use it as the delivery stamp of a polygon count. On WFIGS it equalled editingInfo.lastEditDate (06:05:38Z), not dataLastEditDate (05:26:52Z).

A folder or service named for a register frequently does not contain it. [data] (2026-09-02, land bank & acquisition screening) A state agency's map server carries a folder literally named Geotracker holding six services — CalEnviroScreen, opportunity zones, disadvantaged communities — and not one of them is a GeoTracker site layer. The register itself is not published as a queryable service anywhere: the authority's own host 403s under two user agents, and its 47 published feature services do not include it. What is published is a Cleanup Fund claims subset of unknown proportion, on layer ids 39/40/42, where /0 returns Layer not found.

Guard: enumerate and read the fields array; never bind by title. And where the published thing is a subset of the thing you needed, print the cap beside the finding rather than presenting the subset as the register.

Several differently-NAMED sublayers can be one identical dataset, and the name promises a filter the service does not apply. [data] (2026-09-02, land bank & acquisition screening) CGS Fault_Lines/MapServer publishes sublayers /0 Historic Fault, /1 Holocene Fault, /2 Late Quaternary Fault and /3 Quaternary Fault. All four return exactly 10,021 rows with an identical field list; the age distinction lives in an AGE column the sublayers do not filter on, and the sublayers differ only by renderer. Binding /1 believing it holds Holocene traces alone silently widens the finding to every mapped trace of any age. The same service also carries /5, a scale-break group layer whose /query 400s, and /6, a duplicate of /4.

Guard: count the sublayers against each other before believing a name. Equal counts across differently-named siblings means the name is a rendering label, not a filter — carry the discriminating attribute into the finding instead.

One publisher can serve several LIVE releases of the same dataset, and the one you bind to by default is the undated one. [data] (2026-08-30, resilience disclosure pack) FEMA publishes four National Risk Index census-tract feature services from one AGOL account, all owned by FEMA_NationalRiskIndex, all modified within two days of each other (2025-12-16/18), and all answering STATEABBRV='CA' today. SUM(EAL_VALT) for California:

release                       N         EAL_VALT sum        ERQK_EALT sum
current (undated title)    9106       30446966827.95       14853244103.03
March 2023                 9106       16344919094.90       13246704324.34
November 2021              8036        7022567832.48        4570837474.12
October 2020               8036       (count(OBJECTID) fails - the OID is FID)

4.34× apart, same publisher, same field name, same state — because two things moved at once and neither is visible in the number: the model changed, and the tract register changed (the 2010 census-tract vintage giving way to the 2020 one, 8,036 → 9,106). Every one of the four has an empty copyrightText and an empty description; the release appears only in the AGOL item title, and the service an app reaches for first — National_Risk_Index_Census_Tracts — is the one whose title carries no date at all. A figure of the form "California expected annual loss is $30.4 bn" is arithmetically true against the default binding and arithmetically true at $7.0 bn against a release the same publisher serves from the same account in the same second. Guard: bind a release, never a dataset — put the release in the layer id, carry it in whatever descriptor the app saves, and print it on screen and in every export. Treat an undated service title as a defect rather than a default, and prove the pinning by repointing a second build at a dated sibling and confirming the two figures differ. This is the largest measured reproducibility gap in this file: a vintage choice moving a headline financial figure by 334 %, against the 6.08 % that the CAL FIRE FHSZ vintage moves a hazard classification.

A citation can resolve, return 200, carry the right title — and not contain the sentence it was cited for. [data] [app] (2026-08-30, resilience disclosure pack) An app quoted a requirement from IFRS S2 and cited the standard's own page. That page returns HTTP 200, 112,965 bytes, <title>IFRS - IFRS S2 Climate-related Disclosures</title>, and carries the objective and the effective date — and the words resilience and scenario appear zero times on it ("climate-related risks"=10, "scenario"=0, "resilien"=0). The quoted requirement lives on a supporting-materials page one level away, where those words appear 11 and 12 times. So the natural build step — "re-verify the quoted text against the source page on every build" — passes while verifying none of the quoted sentence, because it checks that the page is reachable rather than that the string is there. Guard: store the quoted string, the URL that carries it, and the read date as one object; assert the string is present at that URL; and make the build fail when it is absent, not only when it differs. Falsify the assertion deliberately by pointing it at the parent page and confirming it fails. A status code plus the right domain is not a verification — this is the document-side twin of the soft-404 entry above, and it is worse, because here the page genuinely is the right page.

Access-Control-Allow-Origin absent on a host that answers 200 to everything else. [data] (2026-08-24, health equity overlay) ftp.cdc.gov serves the authoritative USALEEP tract file at HTTP 200, 305,984 bytes, with a correct Last-Modified — and sends no ACAO header at all, with or without an Origin. curl works; the browser cannot. This joins the file's existing family of a status code is not an availability verdict findings. Guard: probe CORS with an Origin header on every host a browser will call, and treat an absent ACAO as a build-time-fetch requirement rather than a bug to work around at run time. Every ArcGIS host in that same app answered * on both GET and preflight, so the difference is per-host and must be measured per-host.

A service enumeration truncated with an ellipsis hides the newest services. [data] (2026-08-24, health equity overlay) A recipe recorded, from a listing abbreviated with ..., that a publisher's ArcGIS organisation carried "no service whose name contains a FINAL CalEnviroScreen 5.0 or a 2026 designation". Both existed. On a 100-plus service organisation the newest items sort to the end, which is exactly where an ellipsis lands. The finding did not change the app — both are on the wrong tract vintage — but it was recorded as an absence, and an absence is a claim. Guard: search the enumeration programmatically for the thing you are claiming is missing, print the match or the miss, and never conclude absence from a listing you truncated for readability.

A soft 404: every unknown path resolves to the 404 page and is served with HTTP 200. [data] (2026-08-23, climate scenario explorer) /terms-of-use, /about, /data, /guidance and /help/faqs on one publisher's site each returned HTTP 200, 16,563 bytes, final URL /404.html, body "Sorry, we couldn't find the page you're looking for." A link checker passes every one. It cost two things that mattered: the licence page (so redistribution rights for the derived data became an open item to be cleared, not an assumption) and the definition of a variable (so that variable ships as a visibly withheld option naming its reason rather than as a number nobody can cite). Guard: a status code is not an availability verdict — check the final URL and the body. Joins the 403-with-a-244-KB-CAPTCHA-body and the 302-to-a-key-page patterns already recorded here.

A designated source can have no anonymous machine route at all, and its disqualifying notice can sit behind a redirect. [data] (2026-08-23, climate scenario explorer) The scenario source a solution was specified on answered HTTP 200 with the same 1,847-byte single-page-application shell at every path, and every API route 404'd — there is nothing a build job or an in-perimeter deployment can fetch. Separately, the notice retracting the physical-risk basis of the named release was reachable only with -L: without it the probe records 266 bytes at HTTP 301 and never sees the text. A pipeline asking only "2xx or 3xx, and some bytes?" files both sources as healthy. Guard: follow redirects in every probe and read the body; and treat "this source cannot be obtained" as a finding to render on screen — the lane stays empty with its citation, and the app says which family it is actually running instead of borrowing the unavailable source's label.

An unsupported query parameter can be silently ignored and the ungrouped total returned at HTTP 200. [data] (2026-08-23, climate scenario explorer) Adding &variables=loan_to_value_ratio to a public aggregations endpoint returned a byte-identical response to the request without it, at 200, with "servedFrom":"cache" — there is no such breakdown published behind that endpoint. A silent 200 carrying the wrong aggregation is exactly how an unanchored number reaches a disclosure looking anchored. Guard: assert that a grouping parameter changed the response before believing it grouped anything; where it did not, carry the absence into the data model (here: a register with no LTV and no valuation, only the published mean) rather than assuming a distribution.

A RENAMED ArcGIS Online service answers 499 Token Required, not 404. [data] (2026-08-24, shortage-area explorer) A previously-published FeatureServer path began returning {"error":{"code":499,"message":"Token Required","messageCode":"GWM_0003"}} on its root, its layer document and its query alike. It reads exactly like an expired credential or a layer that went private — and it was neither: the service had been renamed, while the layer inside it kept its original name. The host was answering normally throughout, and …/rest/services?f=json listed the new service name immediately. An hour can disappear into an authentication bug that does not exist. Guard: on a 499, crawl …/rest/services?f=json before concluding anything about credentials; search the listing for the old layer name, which usually survives the rename. (2026-10-22, live-position build) A view withdrawn by unsharing answers the same way. CAL FIRE's public aircraft-tracking view (three sparse rows in July) now answers HTTP 200 + {"error":{"code":499}}, and — unlike a rename — it is absent from the org's anonymous …/rest/services?f=json listing. Guard, extended: a 499 plus a vanished directory entry means withdrawn, not renamed and not expired credential; render the lane gated with its citation (as for a token-gated layer below) rather than substituting a neighbouring feed.

A token-gated layer shows its 499 only when asked for JSON — without ?f=json it answers an HTML page. [data] (2026-10-21, watch wall build) The statewide "current" PSPS layer answers {"error":{"code":499,"message":"Token Required"}} at HTTP 200 when the request carries f=json; the same URL without it returns the ArcGIS Online HTML sign-in/landing page, also at 200. A liveness probe that fetches the bare layer URL and checks the status (or "did it return something") reports the gated lane as up, and a parser that tries the body as JSON first and falls back to text reports "unreadable", not "gated". Guard: always probe ArcGIS layers with ?f=json, read the body for an error object before trusting a 200, and treat any HTML body as a failed read — then render the lane grey with its citation rather than substituting a neighbouring feed.

An invalid outFields can return an empty feature array with no error object at all. [data] (2026-08-24, shortage-area explorer) Paging a federal MapServer with two mistyped field names returned {"features": [], "exceededTransferLimit": null} at HTTP 200 — no error, no message, no hint. The same where clause with returnCountOnly reported 215 rows. The failure is indistinguishable from "this state has no records", which is a conclusion a build will happily render as an empty map with a citation. (An AGOL FeatureServer, by contrast, raises 'Invalid field: X' parameter is invalid — so the behaviour is host-dependent and cannot be inherited.) Guard: read the field list from one outFields=* response per layer and assert every requested name against it before paging; and treat a zero-row page whose returnCountOnly disagrees as a bug in the request, never as a finding about the data.

Sub-totals need not reconcile to the published total, and the residual is usually not random. [data] (2026-08-23, climate scenario explorer) Statewide 433,460 / $231.676 bn; the 58 counties sum to 431,473 / $228.875 bn. The 1,987 unmatched records are 0.46 % by count but 1.21 % by amount — systematically larger. Guard: fetch both levels, compute the residual, and print what share of the published total the derived figure actually represents (here 99.54 % by count).

Group layers are not queryable. [data] geometryType: null and /query returns HTTP 400. The data lives in the point/line/polygon children.

An ImageServer's identify and getSamples do not answer the same question. [data] (2026-08-20, asset-level exposure scoring) On the same raster and the same point, CGS MS48 returns identify → "0.205429" (6 significant figures) with a location that is the snapped pixel centre in the service's own SR (102100); getSamples → "0.205428749" (9 figures), echoing your coordinate in the SR you asked in, plus locationId and resolution. Guard: pick one and record which. If a card prints a location beside a value, use getSamples — otherwise it prints a pixel centre tens of metres from the feature the reader clicked.

An ImageServer batch limit can be a TIME budget, and it fails as an HTTP error, not an ArcGIS one. [data] (2026-08-20, asset-level exposure scoring) getSamples with a multipoint: on 18 Aug 500 points succeeded in 25.3 s and 600 failed at 30.9 s; on 20 Aug the wall had moved to 400 OK at 28.5 s, 500 → HTTP 504 at 31.1 s. The constant is a 30-second gateway timeout at whatever throughput the server has that day — so a batch size tuned once will fail later, on the same service, with no change to the data. The response is an HTML 504 page, not an {"error":{…}} object, so an error-shape check misses it entirely. Guard: batch well under the wall (250 here), treat a 504 as "too many points" and retry smaller rather than "service down", and never put an interactive control over a raster sampler — budget it as a build-time bake.

A raster layer answers identify, not query. [data] An image/MapServer raster has no features to select — /query is meaningless on it. Bind it as context with an identify path, and never expect attributes to join.

Some services publish no coordinates at all. [data] Guard: mark the layer not mappable and refuse to toggle it — never a checkbox that ticks and paints nothing. Only queryable FeatureServers belong in a catalogue drawer.

A layer can be mappable and still unjoinable. [data] OpenStreetMap footprints draw fine but carry no building id, so they can never join to an inventory and must never produce a derived figure. Label such a layer context only, and keep it out of every calculation.

Free public endpoints fail often enough to need a retry and a plain-English message. [data] Overpass returns 429/504 routinely — budget two attempts and a human-readable failure, and never wire it into boot. (2026-08-12, broadband gap matrix) A reliable host needs this too, once a run is long enough. A statewide join — ~330 paged requests over ~25 minutes against an Azure-hosted ArcGIS server — died on page 102 of 132 when one request passed its 90 s deadline, discarding twenty minutes of completed paging. Guard: bounded retry with backoff on the paging helper, retrying only what is transient (timeout, network, 5xx, 429) and never an ArcGIS {error} body or a 4xx — those fail identically forever, so retrying them only turns a clear failure into a slow one. Return the retry count rather than hiding it, and keep the bound so a genuinely dead endpoint still fails fast.

Public demo endpoints have limits worth measuring. [data] Overpass returns HTTP 406 to an unidentified client (Node's default User-Agent) — fail fast, don't retry; its mirrors were worse, 504 after ~105 s on a query the main endpoint served in 5.4 s. It also 504s under load on an ordinary city-sized bbox, repeatedly, so no app may depend on it at boot. api.census.gov now requires a key — a keyless call answers 200 and redirects to an HTML page titled "Missing Key", so a naive fetch stores markup as JSON. Guard: assert each in the suite so a catalogue correction cannot rot silently, and let a register that fails degrade to a named absence.

OSRM /table's ceiling is URL LENGTH, not a coordinate count. [data] (2026-08-13, catchment & market-share) This entry previously said "above ~250 coordinates". Measured: 300 sources × 33 destinations (333 coords) → 200, 357 × 33 (390 coords) → HTTP 414, 1 × 399 (400 coords) → 414 — yet 400 coordinates with a bare sources=0 → 200. The sources=0;1;…;356 index list is most of the URL. A coordinate-count guard therefore passes every probe and fails in production the moment you ask for a real matrix. Guard: build the URL, measure it, and tile against a character budget (~6,800 worked); assert that the guard refuses an over-long request and that the server really answers 414 for the same URL sent raw.

Overpass needs OPPOSITE headers in Node and in the browser. [data] [browser] (2026-08-13, catchment & market-share) From Node you must send a User-Agent or the POST is 406. From a browser you must send no custom header at all: the OPTIONS preflight is answered 406 with no CORS headers, so any non-safelisted header kills the call — while a CORS-simple form-urlencoded POST returns Access-Control-Allow-Origin: * and works. Note also that Overpass emits that header only when the request actually carries an Origin, so a probe without one reads as a closed host. Guard: keep the User-Agent in the injectable network seam and never in the domain module, so one module can serve a Node suite and a browser; probe CORS with an Origin.

MapServer f=geojson lower-cases every field name. [core: fixed] [data] FeatureServer preserves case; MapServer does not, so a canonically-cased popupInfo yields undefined for every field. Fixed in engine/popups.ts (propValue) and engine/styleCompiler.ts (getField emits a coalesce over exact/lower/upper). Still applies to any lookup you write yourself.

A host can answer a simple GET and refuse every preflight — so make CORS-simple a GLOBAL RULE. [data] (2026-08-16, collateral & portfolio risk) One estate, three postures, measured the same minute with a full preflight (Origin + Access-Control-Request-Method + Access-Control-Request-Headers): services.arcgis.com answers * and allows the preflight 200; tigerweb.geo.census.gov answers the preflight 200 with no Access-Control-Allow-Origin at all; and services.gis.ca.gov — which carried the app's headline layers — answers the preflight 403 with or without the request-headers line, while answering a simple GET 200 with the Origin echoed. A recipe had recorded that last host as 200 on preflight; it is not, and a preflighted request there dies in the browser having passed every server-side check. Guard: do not carry a per-host workaround. Make it one rule the whole app obeys — every request is CORS-simple: GET, no custom headers, ever — and assert that rule rather than the posture of each host, because the postures change and the rule survives all three.

Probe CORS with an Origin header, or you will misread an open host as closed. [data] Many ArcGIS hosts reflect the request's Origin instead of sending Access-Control-Allow-Origin: *. A curl -I with no Origin therefore returns no CORS header at all, which looks exactly like a host that forbids the browser — and the conclusion is a proxy nobody needed. Guard: curl -s -D- -o /dev/null -H "Origin: http://localhost:PORT" "<url>?f=json" | grep -i access-control, and check the OPTIONS preflight separately. (DC open data, nearby/showcase verification)

One server does not mean one spatial reference. [data] On the same host, one layer published in NAD83 State Plane (wkid 26985) sat beside siblings in 3857. Read spatialReference per layer, never per server. (DC open data)

A WAF's 403 can be client-dependent, so do not record it as the finding. [data] (2026-08-12, broadband gap matrix) broadbandmap.fcc.gov/home answered 403 (Akamai, "Access Denied") to three consecutive curl probes with a browser UA, and 200 with a real app shell to Node's fetch — same URL, same minute. A recipe had recorded "403 to every non-browser client", and a suite asserting that flaps. Guard: assert the fact that actually disqualifies the source and does not depend on the client — here the download API's 401 to everyone. An app shell is not data; a 200 is not access.

A register can be published, mappable, and still too large to draw. [data] (2026-08-12, broadband gap matrix) A provider service-area layer returns valid geometry, but its features are statewide multipart polygons: 60 of them, already generalised to 0.005°, weigh 22.8 MB and take 65 s — so the ~379 intersecting one county would exceed 100 MB in a browser. Guard: measure the payload before binding a polygon register as a map layer, not just its row count. Bind it attributes-only where it is really a list (54 KB, 21 s for the same query), and give the layer panel a list only state with the measurement — a layer that ticks and then hangs the tab is worse than one that says why.

A host that answers curl may refuse the browser. [data] 200 with no Access-Control-Allow-Origin, and 403 to the preflight, is the class of failure that passes server-side verification and dies in production. See how-to/cors-and-proxy.md for the three-layer decision and the SSRF rules that any proxy must follow.

A static house server cannot reach a keyless feed that sends no CORS header. [core: open] (2026-10-21, incident situation map build) Two keyless, public, authoritative feeds answer HTTP 200 with no Access-Control-Allow-Origin: CAL FIRE's incidents GeoJsonList and NASA FIRMS' active-fire CSV.
