An app served from a static host cannot read either from the browser, and the template offers no documented, opt-in way to proxy them — so such a feed is either fetched at build time (stale by the time it is read, for a live layer) or left out. Current guard: leave it out and say so on screen, or bake it at build time with its fetch time printed. Fix: a documented opt-in proxy mode for the house server that forwards only an allow-listed set of hosts, GET only, under the SSRF rules in how-to/cors-and-proxy.md.

Every external call needs a deadline. [app] A dependency that neither answers nor refuses is worse than one that fails: in a real browser a geocoder left the app sitting at "geocoding…" indefinitely, with no error to show and nothing to retry — while both offline suites were green, because a stub always answers. Guard: every outbound call carries an AbortSignal.timeout (or equivalent), and a timeout is surfaced exactly like any other failure. (nearby)

Browsers forbid setting User-Agent on fetch. [browser] A politeness header added to satisfy a provider's usage policy is silently dropped in the browser and applies only from Node — so a request verified with curl or a test runner is not the request the app sends. Providers that require identification must be called from a context that can supply it, or through your own allowlisted route. (nearby)

Secured services. [app] Never send or store the password — mint a short-lived, referer-bound token via {portal}/generateToken and attach only the token.

/edit won't save on a Strata layer. [app] Editing and attachment writes need a writable, authenticated ESRI backend; @strata/auth-arcgis assertEsriBackend throws for a strata backend. Strata Serve is read-only (Strata editing is planned). Reads — query, statistics, related, attachment viewing — work on both backends.

A superseded surface can outrank the current one, and the best-named one is the worst. [data] Three live public services carried older vintages of the same statutory hazard map; the one titled "…FOR REAL ESTATE INSPECTIONS" — the exact use case — served a half that had been superseded across every local jurisdiction, and another was marked deprecated in its item title only, serving happily and anonymously under the undecorated URL with nothing in the service response saying so. Measured against the current surfaces, the choice between vintages moved $470.7 bn — 6.1 % of a state's building value across the hazard line, more than any modelling choice in the app. Guard: bind by endpoint, never by title; read the vintage out of the layer's own description and put it on screen; and when two vintages of one map are both live, name the superseded ones in the recipe so nobody re-finds them.

A publication date is not where a service says its dates are — and the machine-readable one is wrong. [data] (2026-08-17, climate-risk regulatory reporting) Across seven layers an app needed to date: three print a real vintage in their own description ("map dated September 29, 2023"; "Census Tracts; January 1, 2022 vintage"); one prints it only two hops away, in prose inside an AGOL item's HTML description on a different host; three print none at all, at any level. Worse, the field that looks machine-readable is not the one you want: editingInfo.lastEditDate is an edit timestamp and disagrees with the published map date by up to two years — one layer's map is dated 2023-09-29 with a lastEditDate of 2025-06-27, another's map is dated 2025-03-24 with a lastEditDate of 2026-02-02. An app that prints lastEditDate as a vintage produces a provenance line that is confidently wrong. Guard: read the vintage out of the layer's own description; where it lives only in an item's prose, pin the string in the app's own source registry with its read date and re-verify it on every build rather than scraping it at runtime; and never print editingInfo as a publication date.

A whole peril can be undatable, and that is a finding about the product, not a gap to paper over. [data] (2026-08-17, climate-risk regulatory reporting) No California flood surface reachable from inside a perimeter publishes an effective date: not the layer (dateless description, empty copyrightText), not the AGOL item (empty description, empty accessInformation, created == modified), and not the feature — VERSION_ID is a DFIRM database-spec version (19 distinct values across 70,856 polygons) and SOURCE_CIT is a study key (1,214 distinct, 1,119 of them literally NP). Neither is a date. The authority that would settle it is on a host that refuses every path (below). Guard: where a figure's defensibility depends on a date, make "does this source publish a retrievable publication date?" an explicit probe of its own, run it before designing, and let a lane that fails it render empty with its citation rather than being dated from an item's creation timestamp.

A host can block every path at once, including the static download. [data] hazards.fema.gov reset the TLS handshake (curl exit 35, ~0.3 s, TCP connects then Recv failure) on 8 of 8 attempts across a session and again on re-probe — and the block covered the REST root, the Map Service Center and every static .zip, so "just download it instead" was not an escape. Sibling hosts on the same domain (www.fema.gov, gis.fema.gov) answered normally. Guard: probe the host, not the endpoint, before concluding a service is dead; and check whether the agency publishes the same layer from its own account on a host that answers — FEMA's National Risk Index does, with Extract enabled, which made the AGOL replica the only viable bulk path.

A bot challenge that fails as an intermittent HTTP 403 defeats a one-shot smoke test. [data] (2026-08-18, branch & ATM network operations) A public statistical file behind Cloudflare, measured in one minute: HEAD with a browser user-agent → 200 application/zip; GET with the same user-agent at 11:45 → HTTP 403 with a 244,647-byte HTML CAPTCHA page served in place of the ZIP; the same GET with a Referer and the browser Accept/Sec-Fetch headers → 200, 95,033,841 B; a ranged GET with the user-agent alone at 11:46 → 206. A browser user-agent is not a fix, it is a coin flip, and the failure carries a 200-shaped body under a 403 status. Guard: never map 403 → file missing; assert the content type and the magic bytes, retry, and keep a checksummed copy inside the perimeter. Pairs with the rule that one probe is not enough to declare an endpoint dead — here one probe is not enough to declare it alive either.

A recorded WAF verdict that does not reproduce is a re-verification, not a repeal. [data] (2026-08-20, branch & ATM network operations) The FFIEC flat file was recorded as sitting behind an intermittent Cloudflare challenge that answered 403 at one minute and 206 at the next for the same URL and User-Agent. Re-probed on the build date with the full browser header set (UA + Referer + Accept + Sec-Fetch-*), it returned 200 on the first attempt, 95,033,841 B with PK magic. Guard: keep the workaround and keep the guard — assert the content type and the magic bytes, never that the status was 200 — but never hard-code the 403 in an assertion, or the suite goes red for a service that got better. Record the re-verification date beside the original finding.

A documented request cap can be the wrong constraint entirely. [data] (2026-08-23, access to care) OSRM's demo /table endpoint is widely documented — and was recorded in this recipe's own §4 — as capped at 100 coordinates. Probed: 100, 150, 200, 256 and 400 all returned code: "Ok"; 500 returned HTTP 414 Request-URI Too Large and 1,000 dropped the connection. The binding constraint is the URL length (~8 KB at the reverse proxy), not a coordinate count. An app that batches to the documented number issues four times the requests it needs against a community instance under fair use — the opposite of the courtesy the cap was assumed to encode. Guard: probe the cap by bisection rather than inheriting it, batch by bytes with margin, and assert the failure mode (the 414) so the day the proxy is retuned you find out.

Sibling layers of one FeatureServer need not share a schema. [data] (2026-08-23, access to care) CDC PLACES layer 0 (PlacePoints) names the settlement PlaceName; layer 1 (PlaceBoundaries) names it NAME/NAMELSAD and carries no PlaceName at all. Binding layer 0's field name to layer 1 returns 'outFields' parameter is invalid. The same service's HPSA siblings differ further: primary care is 43 fields of designations filtered on PriStNM, while mental and dental are 65 fields of components filtered on StAbbr. Guard: probe every layer id you bind, not the service; "it is the same FeatureServer" is not a schema guarantee.

An enriched layer may key on none of the names you expect. [data] (2026-08-23, access to care) Cal OES's Esri-enriched tract layer carries 164 fields and joins on ID — an 11-digit tract GEOID — with no FIPS, GEOID or TRACTCE column anywhere. The obvious binding joined zero rows, and because the app renders a missing value as "no estimate" the failure looked like an honest data gap rather than a bug. Guard: assert the join rate, not just that the query returned 200 — a join that matches nothing and a source that covers nothing are indistinguishable downstream.

A mistyped field and a sick host return the identical error, so a retry cannot tell them apart. [data] (2026-08-24, shortage-area explorer) gisportal.hrsa.gov answers {"error":{"code":400,"message":"Failed to execute query.","details":[]}} both when outFields names a column that does not exist and when the host is transiently unwell. The same query failed on several consecutive attempts and then succeeded, unchanged, minutes later. The empty details array carries nothing to distinguish the two, so a backoff loop retries a permanent mistake forever and a field-name bug reads as an outage. This is a sharper form of the neighbouring invalid outFields returns an empty array with no error trap — worse, because it looks like a real fault rather than like no data. Guard: make the check local — read the field list from one ?f=json metadata call and assert every requested name against it before paging, then retry only what survives that check. And probe more than once before calling a host dead (see Three rules).

HTTP 202 with a zero-byte body, as a bot challenge. [data] (2026-08-24, housing & homelessness services) huduser.gov answers a default curl/fetch User-Agent with 202 Accepted, Content-Length: 0 and x-amzn-waf-action: challenge. The status line reads as success, the download "succeeds", and the failure surfaces two steps later as a parsing error on an empty file. A sibling federal host (hudexchange.info) refuses with 404 at every path including the site root. Guard: send a browser User-Agent to federal document hosts, and assert Content-Length, not the status line — > 1_000_000 on a workbook is the check that actually fails when the WAF wins.

A data file with NO CORS header is not a lane — it is a build input. [data] (2026-08-24, housing & homelessness services) The same host sends no Access-Control-Allow-Origin at all, on any method. A design that assumed it could fetch the publisher's spreadsheet in the browser would have been rewritten after the first paint. Guard: probe CORS with an Origin header before the design depends on the source, and decide build-time versus runtime there. Note the asymmetry that makes this cheap to get wrong: the same organisation's ArcGIS host answers Access-Control-Allow-Origin: * on both GET and the OPTIONS preflight, so one lane is browser-direct and the other can never be.

returnDistinctValues=true that returns an empty body — or takes a hundred seconds. [data] (2026-08-24, housing & homelessness services) On one AGOL layer this parameter returned a zero-byte body on two of three attempts during research; on a later run it returned valid JSON after more than 100 seconds. json.load then raises at character 0 and the bug is chased in the parser. Guard: sniff the first character for {/[ before parsing, carry a deadline as well (a body sniff alone waits for ever on the slow variant), retry once with backoff, surface source unavailable — retrying on screen, and prefer groupByFieldsForStatistics — which returned the same five classes in under a second, every time.

A plain-text error body under HTTP 200 surfaces as a parser bug in your code. [data] (2026-08-25, public health preparedness) Under load an ArcGIS Online organisation answered outStatistics calls with the bare string The service is unavailable. — HTTP-level success, no JSON error envelope. response.json() then raises at character 0, so the failure presents as "Unexpected token T in JSON at position 0": a parser fault in the app rather than a service fault upstream. The same request answered {"count":0} three seconds later. Guard: every fetch helper sniffs the first non-space character for an opening brace or bracket before parsing, retries with a widening backoff, and reports "source unavailable — retrying" quoting the body it refused. Fail fast on a 4xx and on an ArcGIS error envelope — those are well-formed answers that say no, and retrying them only repeats the same wrong request.

One dead CDN node reads as a CORS error, not as an outage. [data] [browser] (2026-08-25, public health preparedness) Two of CARTO's three basemap subdomains (a. and c.) answered HTTP 502 for every request across a sustained period while b. served normally. Because a 502 HTML error page carries no Access-Control-Allow-Origin header, Chrome reported it in the console as "has been blocked by CORS policy: No 'Access-Control-Allow-Origin' header is present" — so a plain availability fault presents as a configuration or policy bug, and sends you to look at headers, origins and the server instead of at the status code. It cost a red browser suite and five cascading assertion failures before the cause was understood. Guard: when a CORS error names a host you do not control, curl the exact URL and read the status code before believing the diagnosis. And do not pin one subdomain: tile hosts publishing a/b/c should be handed to MapLibre as a tiles array, so one node's outage degrades the backdrop instead of blanking it. Keep the ESRI Web Map JSON templateUrl single-hosted — it is the spec artifact — and fan out on the way to the renderer. Joins the existing absent ACAO on a 200 family, from the opposite direction: there the header was missing on a healthy response, here it is missing because the response is not healthy at all.

Two vintages of one dataset, and only the stale one is machine-readable. [data] (2026-08-25, public health preparedness) A statutory ratings dataset existed twice: an ArcGIS republication last edited 2021-12-29, and the authoritative CSV behind it, CC-BY, refreshed 2026-08-12 — whose host returned HTTP 403 to page, CKAN package_show API and download alike, from three different user agents. The gap is four and a half years, and the readable copy is the stale one. This shape is not specific to one publisher and will recur. Guard: bind the readable copy, print its vintage on screen, assert the lastEditDate in the suite so the day it moves you find out, and document the browser-download-behind-the-perimeter path as the customer's refresh step. Assert no column name from the unreadable copy appears anywhere in the build — if no response has shown a field, it does not exist yet.

A national feed can be complete for most states and entirely empty for one. [data] (2026-08-25, public health preparedness) A CDC syndromic feed published 648,179 rows nationally. For California, 11,774 of 11,977 rows read Data Unavailable, and every row carrying a value was the statewide roll-up — zero county-level values. Texas published 49,735 county-level values on the same feed on the same day, which is what proves this is a publication posture, not a dataset limitation. Rendering the state's 58 empty counties on a map would have produced a blank surface a reader takes for no pressure — a disclosure defect, not a styling one. Guard: a coverage check is per reporting unit, never per dataset. Compare the unit you are binding against a peer unit in the same feed, and prove a blank map is absence before drawing it.

The identifier that gates an API is not a credential - and it is still an outbound call. [data] (2026-08-26, humanitarian response map) An endpoint refuses every request without an app_identifier (403 {"error":"Invalid app identifier"}), so it looks authenticated, and a build stores it like a secret. Cause: it is a base64 application:email pair used for usage analytics, and the server does not validate it - an address invented for the probe, never registered, returned real data. Guard: treat it as a usage label. Never store it as a credential and never present it to a customer as access control - and note separately that the endpoint is still an outbound call to a third party carrying the customer's query, which is what fails a perimeter review even though nothing is key-gated. That is a reason to take a dated snapshot at build time, not a reason to hide the identifier better.

The query-parameter form of an API identifier is CORS-simple; the documented header form preflights into a wall. [data] (2026-08-26, humanitarian response map - a second occurrence of the CORS-simple rule in this section) curl works and the browser gets a 403 on a request that never reached the endpoint. Cause: passing the identifier as the documented X-HDX-HAPI-APP-IDENTIFIER header makes the request non-simple, so the browser preflights - and the preflight carries no identifier and is rejected 403 before CORS is ever considered. The query-parameter form answers Access-Control-Allow-Origin: * with no preflight at all. Guard: the rule already stated here - every request is CORS-simple: GET, no custom headers, ever - and its sharper corollary: any API whose auth lives in a custom header and whose 403 fires before CORS will preflight into a wall, however correct the credential is.

Access-Control-Allow-Origin set to the publisher's own origin, behind a 302. [data] (2026-08-26, humanitarian response map) A download URL works in a terminal and in a browser address bar and fails from application code. Cause: the response 302-redirects and its ACAO names only the publisher's own host, so a cross-origin fetch never gets the bytes. Guard: a 200 in curl is not evidence of a browser-reachable source - probe with an Origin header - and treat such a lane as a file fetched once at build time, which is usually what a perimeter rule wanted anyway.

A well-known service host that no longer resolves at all. [data] (2026-08-26, humanitarian response map) The ArcGIS host named in most published guidance for a standard boundary set returns curl: (6) Could not resolve host. Cause: the distribution moved to files; the guidance did not. Guard: probe the host before designing a live-service delivery route, and probe more than once - the same estate's dataset web pages returned 403 to a machine fetch during the study and 200 to its API the next day, which is bot control varying over time and not an endpoint property. A DNS failure repeated across a session, though, is a dead host and not an intermittent one.

A blocked host's block can change form between builds while staying a block — record the conclusion, not the body. [data] (2026-08-26, facility capacity & catchment) A recipe recorded three state hosts returning HTTP 403 with a 17-byte error code: 1009 body — Cloudflare's access denied by country/region, which no user agent, referer or header defeats. Re-probed five weeks later the same hosts return 403 with an 8 KB Cloudflare challenge page, and a fourth returns a 179-byte nginx 403. The mechanism changed; the conclusion did not. Guard: a recipe's §4 should record the host refuses a scripted request as the finding and pin the status, not the body — and the suite should assert the refusal, so the day it lifts you find out. The correct response to any of these is the same: stop retrying, rebind onto another source, and say so on screen. Distinguishing a country block from a bot challenge matters only for deciding whether to try a user agent once; after that, both are walls.

A trap goes STALE, and an un-asserted workaround becomes a lie. [data] [app] (2026-08-26, facility capacity & catchment) A recipe recorded that services.gis.ca.gov "answers the GET and fails the CORS preflight with 403", so the layer had to be ingested offline. On re-probe the preflight answers 200. The workaround was harmless here (the frame is ingested at build time anyway) but the record was wrong, and a later recipe reading it would have designed around a constraint that no longer exists. Guard: assert every recorded trap inverted in the live suite — the day a service fixes itself the suite goes red, and a red suite is how a workaround gets retired instead of inherited.

A federal open-data catalogue's API can disappear entirely; the publisher's own data.json is the route that survives. [data] (2026-08-26, facility capacity & catchment) catalog.data.gov/api/3/action/* returns HTTP 404 on every action and catalog.data.gov/dataset?q= 301s to the site root. The route that worked was https://data.cms.gov/data.json — 3 MB, 159 datasets, with each dataset's distribution array carrying the API and CSV URLs and the modified / temporal / accrualPeriodicity fields a recipe needs. Guard: prefer the publisher's own data.json to any mirror or aggregator, and treat a catalogue's availability as a fact to probe rather than assume.

A load-balanced service can report two different vintages for byte-identical geometry. [data] (2026-08-30, ATM cash & service routing) tigerweb.geo.census.gov/.../tigerWMS_Current/MapServer/93 returned "Metropolitan Statistical Areas; January 1, 2026 vintage" on one probe and "...January 1, 2025 vintage" on 54 others within the same hour — while the GEOID 41740 geometry was byte-identical across 24 probes (1,284 vertices, same SHA-256). The description string is served per node; the data is not. Two costs: a live suite that pins the vintage string goes red intermittently for no reason, and a caption pinned from a single read is intermittently wrong about the data actually shipped. Guard: record the vintage the download was actually served alongside the file it describes, print that, and assert the shape of the claim (an MSA delineation with a dated vintage) plus the geometry (vertex count against the frozen copy) rather than the string itself. Generalises: a metadata field can be a property of the node that answered, not of the dataset.

Keyless is not the same as licensed — and the basemap gallery now says which is which. [data] [core: fixed] (2026-10-08, the standard map controls rollout) Every basemap the house set offered loaded without a key, and that was taken to mean free to use. It does not. CARTO's basemap terms require an API key for licensed use — the GL styles load keyless today, but without a key an app is outside the terms and can be watermarked at any time, exactly like the raster CDN below. Esri's public tiled MapServers are free only for public, non-commercial apps that credit Esri; a business or government app needs an ArcGIS subscription. The OpenStreetMap France hosts (HOT, CyclOSM) are community servers. Only OSM, OpenTopoMap, VersaTiles and USGS are commercial-ok — and the volunteer-run ones still have usage policies. OpenFreeMap was removed and is on the deny-list (rejected by the user; an ofm-* / openfreemap-* id still renders, as its alias). Fix in core: there is now one basemap catalog, @strata/map-controls/basemaps — 33 keyless entries (OpenStreetMap · Esri · USGS · CARTO), each carrying license (commercial-ok | non-commercial), a plain licenseNote and the provider's licenseUrl, shown in the gallery. CARTO stays in More basemaps, tagged non-commercial "requires a CARTO key for licensed use"; it is not in the universal Recommended 8 (VersaTiles' vt-gray took that slot), and no legacy alias points at CARTO — retired carto-*-gl / openfreemap-* / ofm-* ids land on VersaTiles or OpenStreetMap. The default is OpenStreetMap (osm), the theme pair osm / osm-dark. @strata/core-map's OPEN_BASEMAPS / VECTOR_BASEMAPS / RASTER_BASEMAPS are a deprecated shim over the catalog. Guard: read the licence tag before shipping a business app — keyless answers can it load, the tag answers may it ship. The behavioural keyless lesson below still stands and is now enforced twice: offline host/deny-list guards in pnpm test, and the live two-tile + style-delegation check (pnpm --filter @strata/map-controls verify:basemaps) that CI runs weekly. User-facing summary: Map controls. (2026-10-21, lifeline board build) [core: open] Reopened in part: the gallery does not render the tag. - Symptom: in a vanilla app vendoring @strata/map-controls 1.0.4, the basemap gallery cards show a thumbnail and a name only. There is no commercial-ok / non-commercial mark, although the catalog tags 21 of its 33 entries non-commercial. An operator can pick an Esri or CARTO basemap with nothing on screen saying it may not ship in a business app. - Cause: the catalog carries license / licenseNote / licenseUrl, but the gallery code never reads them. The "shown in the gallery" above is true of the data, not the UI. A spec that requires the tag to be visible cannot be met by an app that vendors the controls and may not edit them. - Current guard: keep the default and the theme pair commercial-ok (vt-gray / vt-gray-dark, osm / osm-dark). Say in the recipe and the README that the tag is not shown. - Fix: render the licence tag (and licenseNote as its tooltip) on each gallery card in @strata/map-controls, and assert it in the shared conformance suite. (2026-10-22, live-position build) Still absent in the vendored 1.0.4 gallery; a recipe that asks for visible non-commercial tags again had to record it as a library gap.

A keyless vector light/dark pair still exists, and it is the substitute the raster set no longer has. [data] (2026-08-30, ATM cash & service routing) Re-confirming the CARTO watermark entry below on this date and hitting it independently, the useful part is the way out. OpenFreeMap is keyless end to end — the style JSON, the vector tiles, the natural-earth raster underlay, the glyphs and the sprite are all on tiles.openfreemap.org with no key anywhere — and it publishes both halves of a theme pair: .../styles/dark and .../styles/positron, plus liberty and bright. tiles.versatiles.org (.../assets/styles/eclipse/style.json, colorful) is the same. This supersedes the earlier note that "there is no keyless dark raster basemap left in the set; the honest substitute is the same keyless tiles dimmed" — dimming a light basemap is no longer necessary, because a real keyless dark style is available. The cost is that a GL style means setStyle, which drops every source and layer (see §7). Verified on this date: tiles.openfreemap.org/styles/{dark,positron,liberty,bright}, tiles.versatiles.org/assets/styles/{eclipse,colorful}/style.json, tile.openstreetmap.org and tile.opentopomap.org — three different tiles returning three different hashes at plausible byte lengths, which is the behavioural check the entry below asks for.

SUPERSEDED 2026-10-08. OpenFreeMap is no longer offered and is on the catalog's deny-list (see the licence entry above). The keyless vector pair in the catalog is now VersaTiles (vt-gray / vt-gray-dark, vt-colorful / vt-colorful-dark, vt-muted / vt-muted-dark); the default theme pair is raster osm / osm-dark — the same OpenStreetMap tiles, inverted for dark.

Both "keyless" default basemaps now answer HTTP 200 with a placeholder -- one watermarked, one blocked -- and a URL-pattern guard cannot see either. [data] [app] (2026-08-26, hospital network planning) Two independent regressions in the house basemap set, found only by looking at a screenshot: basemaps.cartocdn.com/rastertiles/dark_all and .../light_all return HTTP 200, image/png, ~6.4 KB -- the real map with "API KEY REQUIRED . carto.com/basemaps/apikey" composited diagonally across every tile; and tile.openstreetmap.org returns HTTP 200, image/png carrying "418 -- Access blocked. App is not following the tile usage policy of OpenStreetMap's volunteer-run servers". Both pass a /(openstreetmap|cartocdn|opentopomap)/ URL check, both pass a !/[?&]key=/ check, and both pass an img.complete && img.naturalWidth > 1 thumbnail check. This reaches past one app: OPEN_BASEMAPS / defaultBaseMap() in @strata/core-map, the BasemapPanel default and the house set named in CLAUDE.md all still name CARTO as keyless. Verified keyless and clean on this date: maps.wikimedia.org/osm-intl, tile.openstreetmap.de, tile.opentopomap.org. Guard: assert behaviour, not the URL -- fetch two different tiles and require that they differ (a fixed placeholder is byte-identical for every z/x/y), require a plausible byte length and image/*, keep an explicit deny-list of hosts known to be gated, and look at one tile with your own eyes before shipping, because a watermark composited onto real map data defeats every automated check in that list. There is no keyless dark raster basemap left in the set; the honest substitute is the same keyless tiles dimmed with MapLibre's own raster-brightness-max / raster-saturation / raster-contrast, and labelled on screen as dimmed.

Re-confirmed 2026-08-30 (resilience disclosure pack), with two changes to the substitute list. CARTO still watermarks: a.basemaps.cartocdn.com/light_all/6/10/24.png returns HTTP 200, 9,827 bytes, image/png with the watermark composited over real map data. maps.wikimedia.org/osm-intl has since gone to HTTP 403 (2,278 bytes, text/html) and is no longer a substitute — a reminder that a verified-clean list is itself perishable. Still clean on this date, checked with the two-tile guard below: tile.openstreetmap.org, tile.openstreetmap.de, a.tile.opentopomap.org. Note that tile.openstreetmap.org serves the 418 Access blocked image to a bare curl User-Agent and a real tile to a browser UA with a Referer — so probe it the way the app will call it, or you will disqualify a working host. Note also that tile.openstreetmap.de renders German exonyms ("Kalifornien"), which silently breaks an English-only product's language boundary; a basemap is a text surface too.

The guard, written out, because a URL check cannot express it. Fetch two different z/x/y and require the responses to differ — a gated host returns one byte-identical placeholder for every tile:

js export function tilesLookReal(bytesA, bytesB, contentType) { if (!/^image\//.test(contentType || "")) return false; // 403 HTML dressed as a tile if (!bytesA || !bytesB) return false; if (bytesA < 500 || bytesB < 500) return false; // a placeholder is small return bytesA !== bytesB; // …and identical for every tile }

Run it in the live suite over every basemap the app offers, and keep the visual check as well: this guard catches the 418 block and a byte-identical watermark, but a watermark composited over real map data still differs tile to tile, so only a human looking at one tile catches that one. Both defects reached a finished build here with every non-visual assertion green; the screenshot is what found them.

RESOLVED IN THE CORE, 2026-09-01. [core: fixed] The three watermarked CARTO raster presets are gone from @strata/core-map; OPEN_BASEMAPS is now VECTOR_BASEMAPS then RASTER_BASEMAPS, and defaultBaseMap() returns OpenFreeMap Positron — a keyless GL style, keyless end to end (style JSON, vector tiles, natural-earth underlay, glyphs and sprite). It used to return raster tile.openstreetmap.org, so the other half of this entry was shipping as the house default too. The raster gallery keeps OSM and OpenTopoMap: offered, never defaulted to.

The guard in tests/basemaps.test.ts no longer greps for key=. It asserts the shape that cannot lie — every preset resolves to exactly one keyless https URL and carries exactly one of style/templateUrl, the default is a vector style, and a deny-list names every host already caught serving a placeholder so a preset can never quietly return. The positive proof stays behavioural and lives in the live suites, because a unit test cannot see a watermark: two different tiles must differ in bytes, and a style must parse with every host it delegates to keyless too.

Two things the fix surfaced that this entry had not predicted. A vector row has no tile to preview — BasemapPanel/MapChrome painted an empty box for every vector option, which is the same wall of grey boxes this trap is about, arriving through the new door. Both now read the style's own background, water and road colours out of the style document. And the OpenFreeMap attribution is longer than CARTO's, which at phone width ran under a corner readout in one app: a licence term covered by chrome, the very defect logged two entries above. The chrome moved.

SUPERSEDED 2026-10-08. defaultBaseMap() now returns OpenStreetMap (osm) from the one catalog in @strata/map-controls; OpenFreeMap is gone (deny-listed). tile.openstreetmap.org is safe as the default from a browser: it serves real tiles to a browser request carrying a Referer, and the 418 placeholder to clients outside its usage policy (a bare curl) — so probe it the way the app calls it. Its licence note says a busy or commercial app should use its own tile server; setUrlRewrite() is the one hook for pointing it at one. The guards moved with the catalog (packages/map-controls/tests/ basemaps.test.ts, verify:basemaps).

The eleven docs that named CARTO as the keyless house set — CLAUDE.md, app-design.md, building-apps.md, find-and-verify-data.md, map-templates.md, human-language.md, getting-started.md, docs/README.md, COMPONENT-MANIFEST.md, /create-map, the strata-map and strata-symbology skills — and the WebMaps/ starters were updated with it.

A traffic-camera image that loads is not a working camera — and neither its header nor its caption dates it. [data] [browser] (2026-10-21, incident situation map build) Caltrans CCTV stills answer HTTP 200 image/jpeg with a fresh Last-Modified while the picture is a "Temporarily Unavailable" card: in a District 7 sample of 80 in-service cameras, 45 were cards. Two things make the obvious checks worthless. A card can carry a stale burned-in date in its footer ("Friday, September 04, 2026 09:18:01 PDT", read on 21 Oct), and one card's Last-Modified was ~1,126 h old while others were minutes old — so neither the header nor the caption is evidence the image is live. And cameras answering HTTP 500 send no Access-Control-Allow-Origin, so the browser reports a CORS error for what is an outage (the dead-CDN-node entry above). This is the keyless-placeholder family above, arriving through a data feed: a 200 image that is not the thing asked for. Guard: decide from the pixels — the white fraction of a card is > 0.6, against 0.02–0.13 for real road images in the same sample — and count cards, 500s and CORS failures together as "not showing a live picture"; show the reader the camera's own status, never a card as if it were a view.

A dataset can be open-licensed, refreshed the same day, and still unfetchable -- and the mirror will not save you. [data] (2026-08-26, hospital network planning) HCAI's Seismic Ratings and Collapse Probabilities of California Hospitals (SPC and NPC per building, plus a HAZUS collapse probability) is CC-BY and was refreshed on the morning of the probe. Every route to it returns 403: hcai.ca.gov answers a plain nginx 403 from its edge, and data.chhs.ca.gov / data.ca.gov answer 403 with a 17-byte error code: 1009 body -- a client-geography rule -- on the dataset page, the CKAN package_show API, datastore_search and the direct resource download, identically to curl and to a full desktop Chrome user agent. A state mirror (lab.data.ca.gov) answers 200 with the package metadata and no column names and no rows, and has no CKAN API. Re-probed on this date and still 403. Guard: a geography block is not a bot block -- no header defeats it, so do not spend a pass on user agents. Record the block, bind the readable substitute, state on screen exactly what the substitute lacks, assert no column name from the unreachable file, and name it as the customer's own refresh path -- an organisation inside the admitted geography can download it in a browser. Keep the 403 as a live assertion: the day it flips is the day the lane can be upgraded, and that is news you want.

Keyless is not unmetered — and the only request shape a browser can make may cost several times the quota of the shape curl uses. [data] (2026-09-03, tenant mix & catchment) The BLS Consumer Expenditure API v2 needs no key, and a probe found it healthy: a batch POST of eight series answered HTTP 200 with Access-Control-Allow-Origin: *. Both halves of that were misleading.

  • A browser cannot make that call at all. The batch form is a POST with Content-Type: application/json, which is not a CORS simple request, so a browser sends an OPTIONS preflight first — and the preflight answers HTTP 405 with no CORS headers of any kind, while the POST it is asking about answers 200 with ACAO: *. curl, which sends no preflight, sees a perfectly healthy endpoint. The failure appears only in a real browser, as blocked by CORS policy: Response to preflight request doesn't pass access control check.
  • The shape that avoids the preflight is metered. The per-series GET is a simple request and works from a browser — at one request per series. Eight series is eight requests per page load against an unregistered daily threshold, and a few page loads of testing exhausted it. Once spent, the API answers HTTP 200 with "status":"REQUEST_NOT_PROCESSED" and empty results, so response.ok is true, the status code is clean, and every series silently renders empty.

Guard: probe the preflight (OPTIONS with Access-Control-Request-Method and Access-Control-Request-Headers), not only the request — a curl probe of a POST proves nothing about a browser. Count how many requests a page load costs before choosing a request shape. Read the body's own status field, never the HTTP status, on any API that reports application errors inside 200. And prefer the programme's flat file where one exists: download.bls.gov/pub/time.series/cx/cx.data.1.AllData carries the same values with no key and no quota (120.8 MB — stream it and keep the rows you need), which makes the lane a build-time bake and removes both traps at once. A quota is invisible to a probe that runs once; it is discovered by exhausting it.

An ArcGIS cached MapServer serves its tiles row-before-column, and a layer-level minScale means it will not draw at all above that scale. [data] (2026-09-07, valuation & taxation base map) Two facts about one service, and both change a design. The tile path is /tile/{level}/{row}/{col} — row before column — the reverse of the {z}/{x}/{y} order a raster template is rewritten into, so a template built by pattern-matching the URL fetches transposed tiles that are all healthy 200s carrying real PNG bytes. And singleFusedMapCache: true together with a layer-level minScale of 18,057 means the publisher will not draw that layer above roughly 1:18,000 however you ask for it — not a rendering preference to override but a fact to design around, here by carrying a second, coarser surface above that scale. Guard: read singleFusedMapCache, tileInfo and every layer's minScale/maxScale from the MapServer document before promising a picture at any zoom; and when binding a cached endpoint, fetch two tiles that should differ and confirm that they do.

A recipe's rejected-sources table is a claim with a date on it, and it decays as fast as the bound ones. [data] (2026-09-07, valuation & taxation base map) Re-probing a verified §4 four days after it was written, two of the services recorded there as verified-live had gone: one county parcel service now answers HTTP 404 "Service ... not found", and another answers HTTP 499 "Token Required" — so it is not merely unavailable, it is no longer keyless, and the sentence explaining why it had been rejected is now the second reason rather than the first. Neither was bound, so neither cost this build anything, and that is exactly why it is worth recording: nobody re-probes the sources they did not use, and the rejected table is where the next builder goes to avoid repeating a search. Guard: re-probe the rejected sources on every verification pass, not only the bound ones, and record the date and the response beside each rejection. A "considered and rejected" row without a fresh date is a rumour about a service — and it is the kind of rumour that sends someone to spend a day on an endpoint that has since been gated.

An agency's web estate can present three different failure modes in one day, one of which flips between consecutive reads — so a recorded 403 may be a burst response, not a policy. [data] (2026-09-07, property hazard exposure) A research pass recorded CAL FIRE's and the Board of Forestry's entire web estate returning 403 Access Denied to every non-browser client, including with a full desktop User-Agent, while the same agency's ArcGIS services answered 200. Re-probed weeks later: bof.fire.ca.gov gave two UND_ERR_CONNECT_TIMEOUTs and then HTTP 200 within the same minute, on four different paths; osfm.fire.ca.gov did not connect at all under any User-Agent; readyforwildfire.org refused a request with no User-Agent and answered 200 with one. One estate, three mechanisms — a WAF rate limiter, a dead host, and a User-Agent filter — and only the middle one is stable. And the cited deep link had become a soft 404: HTTP 200 carrying a "Page not found" page, which no status check sees. Guard: retry before recording any host as blocked, record which failure you saw rather than "403", re-probe on every build, and assert on the body of a page you cite, not its status. The design conclusion is usually the same either way — transcribe the text and link out rather than fetching at run time — but "this host is unreliable" is a stronger reason to do that than "this host refuses machines", and only one of them is true.

A public, keyless, freely downloadable document can still be unlicensed for your product, and no automated check can see it. [data] (2026-09-07, property hazard exposure) A research report funded by two public agencies, served over plain HTTPS with no key, no paywall and no robots restriction, carries terms permitting reproduction on three conditions — the third being that "the Materials are only for public policy and educational purposes." Nothing in the HTTP response, the licence field of any catalogue, or the file itself expresses that. It is invisible to a CORS probe, a status check and a keyless-behaviour test alike, and it is a live constraint on a commercial deployment. Guard: read the publisher's terms page as a research step, not a formality — and note that the footer link may be the only correct path to it (/terms-of-use/ and /terms/ both 404'd where /about/terms/ served). Then design around it rather than arguing with it: ship the facts (a unit price is a fact) and not the document's expression, print the required attribution beside every figure, and mount the source swappably so a deployment can substitute a licensed one — with the degradation path (source absent ⇒ that column renders empty with its citation) actually tested. Pairs with answering an anonymous request is not a licence, from the opposite direction.

A regular expression over a PDF table drops whole rows whose optional cell is empty — silent omission, not just the fragments you were watching for. [data] (2026-09-07, property hazard exposure) A known trap said a report's cost appendix could not be scraped because measure names wrap across lines, so a regex returns fragments like included and chimney, etc.) as measure names with real prices attached. True, and incomplete: the same regex required a unit cell, and two priced rows whose unit is blank in the source vanished entirely — an omission that leaves a plausible, self-consistent table with no sign anything is missing. The count was reported as 50; it is 53. Extract positionally instead: keep each drawn string's x/y from the content stream, group by baseline, read the column anchors out of the header row, and assign every cell — including tick marks in a scenario grid — to the column it is actually drawn in. That also recovers wrapped names correctly. Two encodings to expect: subset fonts emit f-ligatures and prime marks as control bytes (0x1a–0x1f, 0x94), so baffle extracts as ba<>e and soffit as so<>t; and a body font may be offset from ASCII (here by 29, so The extracts as 7 K H). Guard: assert the row count, and assert the shape of every name — a minimum length, an upper-case or digit first character, no leading bracket.

A PDF stream walker that accepts a bare \r after stream desynchronises the whole file. [app] (2026-09-07, property hazard exposure) /stream\r?\n/g found 218 markers and inflated 150 streams. The apparently more permissive /stream(\r\n|\r|\n)/g found 219 — one spurious early match — and because the loop advances lastIndex from each match's endstream, every subsequent boundary was wrong: 150 inflatable streams became 1, with no error, just an empty extraction. Guard: require the newline, and sanity-check the inflate success rate against the count of /Filter /FlateDecode occurrences in the file before trusting anything you extracted.

A "CORS error" in the browser that is really a bot challenge — and it comes and goes with how often you run your own suite. A county ArcGIS service that had answered all day began failing in headless Chrome with "No 'Access-Control-Allow-Origin' header is present", and only for the heavier query the app makes at zoom 13 and above. Two checks said the obvious diagnosis was wrong: the same host did return Access-Control-Allow-Origin for a light query, and the exact failing URL fetched from curl returned HTTP 200 — with an HTML bot-protection interstitial in the body, carrying an injected <script src="/pull-purger-…">, and no CORS headers on that response. The browser sees a cross-origin response with no headers and reports the only thing it can, which points at the wrong cause entirely. It cleared on its own once the request rate dropped, so it was rate-triggered protection reacting to a browser suite run eight times in twenty minutes, not a policy change.

Three things follow. (a) This is the HTTP 200 with a placeholder body family from §5 wearing a different hat — assert the shape of the body, never the status. (b) Before believing a CORS diagnosis, fetch the exact failing URL server-side; if it returns 200 with HTML, the CORS message is a symptom and the cause is upstream. (c) Your own test loop can be the trigger. A browser suite that re-runs a live service a dozen times in an afternoon looks like a scraper, and the resulting red is about your rate, not your code — pause, re-run once, and see whether it clears before you go changing anything.

A dataset whose URL changes every school year. [data] (2026-10-21, impact zone build) The California Department of Education publishes its public-school points as SchoolSites2526 — next year's is a new service, and the older candidates still answering (2015, 2018 and 2022 subsets) carry administrator e-mails on every row. On 2026-10-21 the calendar school year was 2026-27 and SchoolSites2627 did not exist ({"error":{"code":400,"message":"Invalid URL"}}). Guard: assert in the live suite that the bound service's Year is the newest the publisher lists and at most one year behind the calendar, so the suite goes red the day next year's service appears; never request the administrator fields.

6 · Publishing to Strata Serve

Points don't render. The published layer's objectIdField must be OBJECTID. object_id_field only picks the SOURCE column to cast; a string id (GERS, ministry number) can't be the OID — omit object_id_field so it is synthesised. The snapshot query uses orderByFields=<OID>; a wrong OID returns 0 features and points silently don't draw (polygons and lines use the tiled path and are unaffected). This is the publishing half of the §1 rule — it does not license assuming OBJECTID on a service you did not publish.

Custom markers don't show, and marker shape never carries meaning. Don't use esriSMSPath. Use esriSMSCircle, and vary colour and size per layer. Square / Diamond / Triangle are accepted and then rendered as circles — styleCompiler.ts maps every esriSMS symbol to a MapLibre circle layer and emits marker style '…' approximated as circle as a warning. (2026-08-18, branch & ATM network operations) A uniqueValue renderer that distinguished two device classes by square-versus-circle drew two identical circle sets while the legend implied a difference the map did not show. Guard: encode categorical difference in colour, radius and legend text; treat that compiler warning as an error in any build whose meaning depends on shape.

Overlapping polygons hide each other. Use a low fill alpha (~40/255).

Changes don't take effect. The Serve server has no hot reload — restart after any config, datasource or metadata change (/restart or .claude/scripts/restart_server.sh).

Vector tiles are huge. tile_fields controls which attributes are baked into vector tiles — keep it to a handful. It is not the popup field list (FeatureServer /query always returns all fields).

A stray field appears. pandas-written GeoParquet can carry __index_level_0__ — drop it on convert (SELECT * EXCLUDE(__index_level_0__)).

7 · Rendering and the map

An ungated styledata retry loop exhausts itself and reports a failure over a perfectly drawn map. [app] (2026-09-07, facilities & space utilisation) The retry that correctly fixes the setStyle reinstall above was hooked to styledata with no gate. That event also fires while tiles load, so the reinstall ran continuously from first paint, burned through its own 60-attempt ceiling, and wrote "the basemap changed but the estate could not be redrawn" into the status line — over a map carrying 13,001 points. The fix for one defect produced a second one that was more alarming than the first, because it accused a working app. Guard: gate a restyle recovery on a flag set only by the code that calls setStyle, clear it on success, refuse to re-apply a style already in force, and make the give-up branch check whether the sources are actually missing before it says so. This is the status-line-that-reports-intention trap wearing a retry loop: report a state, never an attempt.

One dropped basemap request put a permanent "the map failed" on an unattended wall. [app] (2026-10-22, watch wall build) The wall routed every MapLibre error event to its failure notice. A single net::ERR_CONNECTION_RESET on the keyless basemap host's sprite at first load wrote "the map failed: Failed to fetch" onto the notice bar of a display meant to run a whole shift. Nothing cleared it, and the map under it drew normally (a later fetch of the same sprite returned 200 three times out of three). It surfaced as two console failures in the browser suite, 30 minutes before the soak it was attached to. Guard: split map errors by source. An error on one of the app's own sources is a real failure and stays up. A basemap tile, sprite or glyph failure is a third-party blip: show a dated note that clears itself, log it as a warning, and never let it overwrite a real failure. In the browser suite, count connection-level failures at known basemap hosts separately and print them, but keep any HTTP status from those hosts failing, because a 403 is what a gated basemap looks like (§5). Same family as the styledata entry above: report a state, not an event.

A MapLibre canvas cannot be pixel-probed from the page, so the five-identical-thumbnails guard has to capture the composited frame. [browser] (2026-09-07, facilities & space utilisation) This file already records that a failed basemap thumbnail leaves five identical grey boxes with every non-visual test green, so the obvious guard is to read each preview canvas back with drawImage and compare. It cannot work. MapLibre runs without preserveDrawingBuffer, so the drawing buffer is gone by the time any script reads it: getImageData over every preview returned exactly one colour for all five, on a screen where three of them were visibly different maps. A guard that reports the same answer for a working page and a broken one is worse than no guard. Guard: capture the composited frame through Page.captureScreenshot with a clip box per thumbnail and hash the PNGs — five identical boxes are then five identical hashes, and the check is decisive. The same applies to any assertion about what a WebGL map actually painted.

A preview zoomed too far in is as blank as one not zoomed at all. [app] (2026-09-07, facilities & space utilisation) The recorded advice is to zoom a basemap thumbnail in past the parent map, because at the parent's zoom a 46 px box holds nothing but each style's background paint. Overshooting has the same result for the opposite reason: at the parent centre plus five zoom levels, a statewide California view lands in the empty Central Valley and every style is a flat rectangle again. Guard: +3 is the useful band — far enough in that coastline, water and road colour are in frame, which is the thing that actually differs between two light styles, and not so far that the frame contains no features at all.

The largest class on the map can be its least visible mark, and the app then contradicts its own reading. [app] (2026-09-07, facilities & space utilisation) The design's whole argument is that 54 % of the estate publishes no utilisation class, and the card said so: the grey band was the widest band on the bar. The map showed a green state. The silent class was drawn as a size-6 hollow ring, which at statewide zoom occupies about three pixels — and a thin ring at three pixels antialiases away to nothing, while a filled circle of the same size reads strongly. Every non-visual assertion was green; only a screenshot showed it. Guard: a hollow or outlined mark needs materially more size and stroke than a filled one to carry the same weight, and the class that is most numerous should be sized at least as large as the reported ones, not smallest by default. Assert the claim visually: this app's browser suite now measures the four bands and requires the grey one to be the widest at first paint — a rendered check for a statement the card makes in words.

Marks rasterised once, from one palette, disagree with a themed legend. [app] (2026-09-07, facilities & space utilisation) MapLibre's circle layer draws only circles, so honouring an ESRI renderer's triangle and square means an icon per class — and those icons were generated once, at boot, from the light palette. In dark mode the legend swatches switched to the measured dark hues while the map still carried the light ones, so the two surfaces disagreed about what a class looked like. Guard: resolve a data colour through one function that takes the mode, use it for both the swatch and the raster, and re-rasterise on every mode change — including the case where the reader has pinned a basemap, which skips the setStyle that would otherwise have rebuilt the images as a side effect. Relying on the restyle to rebuild them is the bug: the restyle is not guaranteed to happen.

Reinstalling sources from styledata runs too early, and setStyle then leaves a correctly themed page over an EMPTY map. [app] (2026-09-07, facilities & space utilisation) A second occurrence of the family already recorded here for load/isStyleLoaded, in the one place it is most likely to ship: the theme toggle. setStyle drops every source, layer and image; the app reinstalled them from styledata, which fires while the new style is still loading, so installLayers returned early on its own isStyleLoaded() guard and put nothing back. The canvas was the right size, the tokens were right, the reading was right, and there was nothing on the map. Guard: never treat styledata as "the style is ready". Reinstall through a small retry that re-checks isStyleLoaded() (and listen to style.load as well), and — for the harness — wait for a positive feature count, not for "a source exists": immediately after a restyle the old source is briefly still there, so an existence check returns instantly and asserts against a map that is about to be emptied.

A capped, scrollable popup paints a white slab below itself. [browser] (2026-09-07, facilities & space utilisation) Giving .maplibregl-popup-content a max-height with overflow-y: auto produced a clipped scroll container whose backing store painted as a white rectangle the width of the popup and roughly the height of its overflow, below the popup, over the map. The DOM was correct — the content box measured exactly its max-height and nothing was positioned there — so no geometric assertion could see it; only a screenshot did. Guard: do not make a popup a scroll container. MapLibre re-anchors a tall popup to keep it in view, which is the mechanism that already solves this; a height cap solves a problem the library has already solved and introduces a paint artefact instead.

A solid-colour scroller over the map punches a blank box through it under software compositing. [browser] — library surfaces [core: fixed] in @strata/map-controls 1.0.4 (2026-10-14, four vendored map-controls apps) An opaque panel with a plain colour fill and overflow:auto|scroll that sits over the MapLibre canvas — the map-controls drawer, a legend, a floating card, a popup capped with max-height — is painted into Chrome's scrolling-contents layer and used as an occluder. Under software compositing (GPU off or blocklisted, RDP, many VMs, headless --disable-gpu) Chrome mis-places the occluded rect, typically 150–250 px from the panel (often after the page scrolled and came back), so the map is not drawn there and a blank page-background box shows through it — nowhere near the panel that causes it. Real users on those machines see it; it is not only a harness artefact (the white-slab entry above and the --disable-gpu "unpainted rectangle" entries in §9 are very likely the same mechanism). The DOM is correct and elementFromPoint returns the canvas. Fix: give every opaque scroller over the map a one-colour image fill — background: linear-gradient(C, C) (e.g. linear-gradient(var(--panel),var(--panel))). An image fill is never an occluder and paints identically. Do not also set an opaque background-color under it — an opaque colour can make the box count as opaque again. Since map-controls 1.0.4 the drawer and the themed popup card (popupStyles) ship that fill; the app's own legend, cards and capped popups are the app's job. A background-color-only override of those two now paints under the gradient and does not show — use the tokens or the background shorthand; read the colour from backgroundImage, not backgroundColor. Detect: compare a full-page screenshot against the map's own pixels (gl.readPixels, or drawImage into a 2D canvas) at several points across the map — a blank in the screenshot where the GL buffer is painted is the box. A 1×1 clipped screenshot at a probe point hides it; so does SwiftShader in-process (--use-angle=swiftshader --in-process-gpu), which may not reproduce it at all — run the probe with --disable-gpu on purpose, open the drawer / popup / legend, scroll the page away and back, and shoot the whole page. Then keep the normal screenshot harness off --disable-gpu as §9 says.

map.on("load") and isStyleLoaded() can BOTH stay false forever while the basemap paints perfectly — and every operational layer is then never installed, under a status line reporting a full count. [app] @@STAMP@@ The app installed its GeoJSON sources and layers from map.on("load"), with a styledata handler gated on map.isStyleLoaded() as the re-install path after a style swap. In headless Chrome against an OpenFreeMap style — whose shaded-relief raster source keeps requesting tiles — load never fired and isStyleLoaded() never became true, so getStyle().layers held the basemap's 47 layers and none of ours. The basemap rendered normally. The canvas was the right size. The console was clean. All 115 browser assertions passed, because every one of them checked application state — visibleClasses() .length === 8 is true whether or not a single polygon exists. Only a screenshot showed an empty map. Guard, three parts. Gate hydration on the style object existing (getStyle()?.layers?.length > 0), not on a load event that may never arrive, and retry on load, styledata and idle. Assert queryRenderedFeatures({layers:[...]}).length > 0 in the browser suite, never app state. And make the offline MapLibre stub report isStyleLoaded() false on purpose, so the regression cannot come back.

A display rule in your own stylesheet outranks the browser's [hidden]{display:none}, so setting hidden does nothing and a modal stays over the whole app. [browser] @@STAMP@@ #splash{position:fixed;inset:0;display:grid} beats the user-agent stylesheet's [hidden]{display:none} on specificity, so el.hidden = true left the splash overlay covering the entire board. Every element underneath was present, measurable, clickable through .click() and assertable — the suite drove the whole signature loop through it and reported green. Guard: ship [hidden]{{display:none!important} in the app's own reset, and assert getComputedStyle(el).display === "none" after dismissing anything dismissible, plus document.elementFromPoint over a region that matters to confirm what the reader is actually looking at. el.hidden is not a visibility assertion.

MapLibre sizes its canvas once, at construction, and a flex shell has not settled by then. [app] @@STAMP@@ The map was constructed while its flex container was still resolving, so the canvas shipped 630 px tall inside a 495 px box and stayed that way — MapLibre does not watch its container. The DOM width was right, the arithmetic was right, and both non-visual suites were green. Guard: attach a ResizeObserver to the map's container and call map.resize() from it, rather than only after a known gesture; a resize listener on the window is not enough, because a sibling panel changing width never resizes the window. Assert in the browser suite that the canvas rectangle matches its container's, not merely that it is non-zero.

A column-flex splitter inside a scrolling body computes to HEIGHT ZERO, and every band that follows it paints on top of the map. [browser] (2026-09-07, portfolio & asset register) The desktop shell is body → main → #split(flex) → [#stage, #rail], with the horizon bar, the filter row and the table as siblings of the splitter. The phone breakpoint did the usual thing — #split{flex-direction:column}, body{overflow:auto} — and #split then measured 390 x 0 while #stage (464 px) and #rail (2,593 px) overflowed it. Because the splitter had no height, the three bands after it all began at the stage's own top, so the table header and the horizon bar rendered over the map. Measured, at 390 x 844:

split      top 298  h    0        <- collapsed
stage      top 298  h  464
horizon    top 298  h  182        <- on top of the map
filters    top 298  h  105        <- on top of the map
tablewrap  top 298  h  320        <- on top of the map

Every box is individually correct, the CSS greps in the offline suite passed, and the page is unusable. Guard: at the phone breakpoint take the shell out of flex sizing — body{display: block}, main{display:flex; flex:none} — and give the splitter display:contents so its children become direct flex items that can be ordered against the bands outside it. Then assert the GEOMETRY in the browser suite: no band may start above the map's bottom edge. A CSS grep cannot see this, and it is the second time in this library that a phone layout has been green in two harnesses and broken on screen.

And in the same breakpoint: one white-space:nowrap title widened the LAYOUT VIEWPORT past the device. [browser] (2026-09-07, portfolio & asset register) #topbar h1{white-space:nowrap} is right on a laptop and is a 458 px element on a 390 px phone. Chrome widened the layout viewport to 470 px to contain it, so the whole page rendered inside a band with a dead margin beside it — which reads as a styling accident rather than as overflow, because nothing is clipped and nothing scrolls horizontally in an obvious way. A rail table with white-space:nowrap on its cells did the same thing at 793 px, and was merely clipped by the rail's overflow-x:hidden, so the source and vintage of every number on the page was unreadable on a phone with no sign that anything was missing. Guard: at the phone breakpoint let the title and any PROSE table wrap (white-space:normal; overflow-wrap:anywhere), keep nowrap only for the data table that scrolls in its own box, and assert document.documentElement.scrollWidth <= window.innerWidth and window.innerWidth === <device width> on a real emulated viewport.

A moveend handler that refetches context layers closes a loop through MapLibre's OWN ResizeObserver, and the application renders perfectly while its main thread is never clear. [browser] (2026-09-07, portfolio & asset register) The cycle: moveend → refetch the context layers → setData on five GeoJSON sources → MapLibre recomputes its own chrome, because the attribution control's width changes with the sources in the style → MapLibre's internal ResizeObserver on the map container fires → resize() → moveend. Measured on a camera that was not moving at all: the stage stayed 1020x404 while the refresh counter climbed 3 → 14 → 30 → 46 → 61 in fifteen seconds, about six full refreshes a second, each one re-fetching a live county service.

The symptom is the nastiest kind. Nothing crashes. The map renders, the counts are right, every screenshot is correct, and both non-visual suites are green. The main thread is simply never clear, so isStyleLoaded() is almost never true and a scripted Runtime.evaluate answers only intermittently — the browser suite reported "the application boots and settles" red for three consecutive runs while every assertion after it passed, which reads exactly like a dead application and is not one. Detaching the app's own ResizeObserver changed nothing, because the observer in the cycle belongs to MapLibre.

Chrome does say so — ResizeObserver loop completed with undelivered notifications — and this suite's clean-console guard was filtering that line out as benign boilerplate. It was the only report of the defect. A filter that excuses a warning excuses the defect behind it.

Guard: three things, and the first is the one that matters. (1) Never let a viewport handler refetch unconditionally — key the refresh on the rounded bbox, the zoom and the layer set, and return immediately when the key is unchanged; debounce on top. That breaks the cycle where it is yours, rather than chasing a library's internals. (2) In your own ResizeObserver, compare the contentRect against the last one and coalesce to a frame before calling map.resize(). (3) Do not filter ResizeObserver loop out of a console assertion. And when a readiness helper times out, make it report which condition it is still waiting on — a boolean that says only "not ready" sends the next reader hunting in the wrong place.

A keyless vector-tile host can answer HTTP 200, with the CORRECT content type, and ZERO BYTES — and the map renders as blank ground with nothing in the console. [data] (2026-09-07, portfolio & asset register) This extends the existing "keyless is a behaviour, not a URL" rule with a failure mode none of its checks catch. OpenFreeMap's Positron style names its vector source as a TileJSON endpoint (.../planet), not as a tile template, and the real template inside carries a build stamp: .../planet/20260830_080001_pt/{z}/{x}/{y}.pbf. The obvious guess — take the source URL and append {z}/{x}/{y}.pbf — returns 200 · application/vnd.mapbox-vector-tile · 0 B. A host check passes. A status check passes. A content-type check passes. A !key= check passes. And the two-tile differ test passes as well if it is run against the guessed path, because two zero-byte bodies compare equal only when something actually compares them. The stamp changes whenever the planet is rebuilt, so a hard-coded template also rots on a schedule nobody controls. Measured on the resolved path: 188,679 B and 235,658 B, different hashes, real map. Guard: resolve the TileJSON at load and read tiles[0] from it; never construct a tile path from a style's source URL; run the two-tile differ test against the resolved path and require both bodies over a size floor; and look at one tile before shipping.

A basemap preview pointed at an endpoint that does not exist leaves every row an identical box, and every non-visual assertion passes. [app] (2026-09-03, parcel record coverage) The basemap drawer built a thumbnail URL by pattern — https://<style host>/styles/<name>/{z}/{x}/{y}.png — on the assumption that a vector-style host also serves matching raster tiles. It does not. Chrome blocked every request (ERR_BLOCKED_BY_ORB), all five rows showed the same empty box, and the offline suite reported the drawer perfect because the markup was perfect. This is distinct from the lazy-loading entry in this section: there the tiles were merely late, here the endpoint never existed. The fix was to stop guessing at a picture of a style and render the style — a small maplibregl.Map per row, mounted when the drawer opens and destroyed when it closes. Guard: never synthesise a tile URL from a style URL by pattern. And assert the preview from a clipped screenshot rather than from the canvas: a screenshot is what the reader gets, it does not depend on the page setting preserveDrawingBuffer, and a flat box compresses to a few hundred bytes while a rendered map does not — so "all five differ and none is tiny" is a check that actually bites.

Second occurrence, 2026-09-07, portfolio & asset register — and with a sharper edge. The same mistake was made in a new shape: rather than guessing a raster path on the style's own host, the drawer built a tile.openstreetmap.org URL for the ground in view and showed it in an <img>. That host is one the same application explicitly refuses as a basemap, in a comment two hundred lines above, because it answers HTTP 200 with an "Access blocked" image — so the app ruled the host out for the map and then leaned on it for the picture of the map. It was also an external call to a host outside the three services the app binds, which a deployment behind a customer's perimeter cannot make. Headless Chrome returned naturalWidth: 0 on all six rows: identical grey boxes, with every non-visual assertion green. The rule is not "do not guess a path on the style's host" — it is RENDER THE STYLE, mounted when the drawer opens and destroyed when it closes. And the assertion is on pixels: each preview must paint more than a handful of colours, and the set of them must not all be identical.

A preview rendered at the parent map's zoom shows one flat block of background colour, which is exactly the swatch it was meant to replace. [app] (2026-09-03, parcel record coverage) With the previews rendering correctly, a 76-pixel box at a state-wide zoom held nothing but each style's background paint. Two light styles were then indistinguishable, which defeats the whole reason a preview exists rather than a colour chip. Guard: zoom a style preview in past the main map, not out. Coastline, water and road colour are what differ between styles; background alone is not.

styledata fires ONCE after setStyle, while isStyleLoaded() is still false, and never fires again — so a handler gated on isStyleLoaded() loses every operational layer permanently. [browser] (2026-09-02, site selection - the swing slate) setStyle drops every source and layer, so an app must re-add its own. The documented hook is styledata, and the obvious guard is if (map.isStyleLoaded()) { hydrate(); }, because addSource throws while a style is loading. Measured in headless Chrome across a light-dark swap, polled every few hundred ms for nine seconds: styledata fired exactly once, at isStyleLoaded() === false; the style reported loaded about a second later; styledata never fired again. The guard therefore skipped the only event it would ever receive, and the app's operational layers were gone for the rest of the session. Both non-visual suites reported it green — the sources were correct in application state and the canvas was the right size. Guard: hydrate on styledata and on idle, which cannot fire before the style is usable, with an idempotent re-add that returns immediately once the sources are back, so the repeated idle costs nothing. Assert the recovery by polling for the layers to come back, never by sleeping a fixed interval — a fixed sleep turns a permanent loss into a flaky test.

A symbol layer needs a text-font the CURRENT basemap's glyph host serves, and the name differs between providers. [browser] (2026-09-02, site selection - the swing slate) A symbol layer with text-field and no text-font gets MapLibre's default ["Open Sans Regular", "Arial Unicode MS Regular"]. OpenFreeMap's glyph endpoint does not serve it: the request 404s, the labels silently do not draw, and only a console line says so. Hard-coding the fix does not work either — OpenFreeMap publishes Noto Sans Regular while Versatiles publishes noto_sans_regular, so one stack breaks half a keyless gallery. Guard: read the font out of the loaded style's own layers (the first layout["text-font"] it declares is by definition one its glyph host serves), re-read it after every basemap change, and assert both that the font is one the style declares and that queryRenderedFeatures on the symbol layer returns features. This is the label sibling of the existing glyphs: undefined entry: there the whole style is invalidated, here only your own labels vanish.

MapLibre's AttributionControl renders EXPANDED on load even with compact: true, and prints itself across the map. [browser] (2026-09-02, site selection - the swing slate) Mounted bottom-right with a multi-agency customAttribution, the control's text ran the full width of the map — underneath the bottom-left legend and over the coordinate readout, clipping both. This is the map-corner collision already in this file, arriving from a control the app did not lay out. Guard: for anything longer than a short credit, do not mount the control: render your own single line in the corner stack (short credit visible, full source list and the no-warranty note in its title), and keep the full attribution in a provenance surface and in every export. Assert that the corners do not overlap — legend against readout against attribution — as geometry, because a collision is invisible to every DOM assertion. (2026-10-21, incident situation map build) The mechanism, and a lighter guard where the control must stay: the compact control starts with class maplibregl-compact-show on its container, and only the user's first drag removes it; with a long multi-agency credit it spread across the whole bottom of the map, covering the scale bar, legend rows and the readouts. On the map's load event, remove maplibregl-compact-show from .maplibregl-ctrl-attrib — exactly what a drag does, and MapLibre does not re-add it — so the control opens collapsed to its ⓘ button; keep the full credit in every export.

Each page must reframe the map on its own subject, or it inherits the previous page's view. [app] (2026-09-02, site selection - the swing slate) A three-page app moved one map element between per-page slots and called resize(). The dossier — a page about one candidate — opened on wherever the previous page had last flown, a stretch of map holding no candidate, no trade-area band and no competitor, with every non-visual assertion green. Guard: compute the frame per page and fit it whenever the page changes, then assert per page that the page's own subject is inside getBounds() and that its own layers return features from queryRenderedFeatures. Note that queryRenderedFeatures reports nothing while a camera animation is still in flight, so wait for !isMoving() && areTilesLoaded() && loaded() before judging — the same assertion is otherwise flaky for a reason that has nothing to do with the defect. (2026-10-22, live-position build) The same is true one call later: queryRenderedFeatures immediately after a setFilter reads the previous frame — wait for idle before counting.

MapLibre rejects a data-driven line-dasharray, and it tells you through the map's own error event, not the console. [browser] (2026-09-02, land bank & acquisition screening) A single outline layer styled with

"line-dasharray": ["case", ["==", ["get", "verdict"], "unknowable"], ["literal", [4,3]], ["literal", [1,0]]]

produces layers.constraints-outline.paint.line-dasharray: data expressions not supported. MapLibre discards the whole paint property and draws every outline solid. Nothing throws, nothing reaches console.error, and every DOM and arithmetic assertion stays green — while the dashed-versus-solid distinction, which was the entire signal separating a statement about data from data, silently never rendered. It was found by reading the app's own status line in a screenshot.

Guard: split into two layers with static dasharrays and a feature filter on each. And accumulate map.on("error") into app state so a harness can assert the list is empty — a defect reported only through a framework's own event channel is invisible to a console check.

MapLibre's popup is a white bubble regardless of your theme. [browser] (2026-09-02, land bank & acquisition screening) .maplibregl-popup-content ships an unconditional white background. An app whose dark-mode body colour is near-white inherits into it, and the popup renders near-white on white — completely unreadable, while passing every structural assertion about its content.

Guard: style .maplibregl-popup-content, .maplibregl-popup-tip (per anchor direction) and .maplibregl-popup-close-button from the same tokens as the rest of the shell. Never rely on inherited colour inside third-party chrome, and look at the popup in both themes.

A repeating timer that styles a layer outlives the style that contains it. [app] (2026-09-02, land bank & acquisition screening) A "flash the selected feature" interval checked getLayer() once before starting. A theme change mid-flash calls setStyle, which drops every layer, and the next tick styles a layer that no longer exists — Cannot style non-existing layer "highlight-line". A try/catch does not suppress it, because MapLibre reports it on the map's error event rather than by throwing.

Guard: re-check the layer on EVERY tick, not once at the start, and clear the interval when it goes away. Anything that repeats across a possible setStyle has to re-establish its assumptions each time.

An invalid style key gives you a Map, a canvas, WebGL — and nothing painted, with an empty console. [app] (2026-08-24, health equity overlay) A style object carried an explicit glyphs: undefined. MapLibre rejects it in validation, and the failure is silent unless something is listening: the Map object existed, the canvas was correctly sized, getContext("webgl2") returned a working WebGL 2.0 context, and isStyleLoaded() was false while getStyle() was undefined. Both non-visual harnesses were green — the arithmetic was right, the DOM was right — and the only symptom was a white rectangle.
