[browser] [app] (2026-08-26, hospital network planning) The house rule says whatever adopts must also release, and the canonical wiring is popup.on("close", () => { if (selected) release(); }) -- correct, because a reader dismissing the popup is a release. But popup.setLngLat(...).setHTML(...).addTo(map) on a popup that is already attached makes MapLibre fire close before it re-adds, so adopting straight from one record to another selected the new record and instantly dropped it. It never showed on the click path (a card click from a released state has no popup open) -- only on the keyboard path, where every step adopts from an already-adopted state, so every keypress silently self-cancelled. Guard: set a movingPopup flag around the re-target and ignore close while it is set, so a reader-initiated close still releases and yours does not. And make the stub faithful here: a PopupStub whose addTo() quietly re-attaches hides this entirely -- ours now fires close on re-add, and the suite asserts both that the adopt survives it and that a reader-closed popup still releases.

A hidden element that still has focus swallows every subsequent key. [browser] [app] (2026-08-26, hospital network planning) Dismissing a splash by setting hidden = true on its container left document.activeElement on the dismiss button inside it. Keys still reached window -- a capture listener saw every keydown -- but the app's own handler never acted, and L / B / G / F and the arrow keys were dead for the rest of the session. Guard: document.activeElement.blur() whenever you hide the subtree that contains focus, and assert a keyboard behaviour after any overlay is dismissed rather than before, because that is the order a reader will meet it in.

A generic class name is a component boundary you did not declare. [app] (2026-08-26, hospital network planning) A splash panel used class="box". .box was already the map drawer's 14 px checkbox/radio, carrying height:14px; display:grid; place-items:center. #splash .box overrode the properties it named and inherited the rest, so the panel rendered as a 14 px strip with its own paragraphs overflowing below it and the background painting only the strip. Nothing errored and every id-based assertion passed. Guard: prefix or scope any class that describes a component rather than a utility, and assert containment geometrically -- every panel > * must have bottom <= panel.bottom -- because "the panel rendered" and "the panel contains its content" are different claims. Related: a centred flex or grid item is shrink-to-fit, so max-width alone gives it no width; the same panel had already collapsed to a one-word-per-line column until it was given width: min(640px, calc(100% - 32px)).

Moving from a raster basemap to a GL style makes every basemap switch a setStyle, and setStyle drops your layers. [app] (2026-08-30, ATM cash & service routing) Swapping the keyed CARTO raster set for keyless vector styles turned a one-line source.setTiles() into map.setStyle(url) — which removes every source and every layer the app added. Three things this build had to get right, each of which reads as green until you look: (a) re-hydrate on styledata and on idle, idempotently (getSource/getLayer first) and inside a try, because styledata fires before a remote style has finished loading and an addLayer against a half-loaded style throws; (b) repaint after re-hydrating, or the sources come back empty and the map is a bare basemap with every non-visual assertion still passing; (c) in the browser suite, poll until the remote style has settled rather than asserting after a fixed sleep — MapLibre replaces the style when it lands, dropping the layers a second time. Guard: make the MapLibre stub fire styledata, then drop the sources, then fire styledata again — a stub that keeps them cannot reproduce a switch that forgets to re-hydrate. Assert the sources, the layers and a non-zero rendered feature count after the swap, in that order. (2026-10-22, live-position build) Make the idempotency check getSource(id) / getLayer(id) per compiled id — not map.getStyle(), which serialises the whole basemap style (hundreds of layers) and, called from an idle handler, pays that serialisation on every frame.

A full-bleed map flies records underneath its own floating panels. [app] (2026-08-30, ATM cash & service routing) In a hero-map silhouette the docked panel and the floating cards sit over the canvas, so flyTo/fitBounds centre on the full canvas and put the record behind a panel. The canvas matches its container, the record is selected, the popup opens — and the user sees none of it. Guard: compute a padding from the chrome actually on screen (measure the panel, the card column, the legend and any bottom card) and pass it to every flyTo and fitBounds; recompute it as the panel is dragged; and call fit() once after boot so the app opens on its signature rather than merely containing it. Assert padding.left >= panelWidth after a resize. Related to but distinct from the earlier fitBounds-pads-the-viewport-not-the-app entry: this is a panel that overlaps the map rather than one that shrinks it.

Two floating surfaces will contend for the same corner, and only a screenshot says which won. [app] (2026-08-30, ATM cash & service routing) The house defaults put the legend bottom-left; this app's linked series card wanted the same corner, and a coordinate readout ended up under a card column that grew to the full height. Each surface was individually correct and inside the viewport, so every positional assertion passed. Guard: assert pairwise non-overlap across every floating surface as one rectangle predicate, not just that each is on screen, and include the control cluster and the readout in the set. Then look at the screenshot anyway.

An inline width written by a drag outranks BOTH the collapse rule and the responsive breakpoint. [browser] [app] (2026-09-03, transaction evidence) A resizable rail sets style.width on every pointer move, which is correct. Two rules then stopped working, and only in the order a real reader hits them. Collapsing the rail (#rail[data-collapsed="1"]{flex-basis:0}) did nothing at all once it had been dragged, because an inline declaration beats any stylesheet rule of any specificity. And at the phone breakpoint, where the rail stacks above the map and a horizontal width is meaningless, the 444 px it had been dragged to survived into a 420 px viewport and pushed the whole page sideways. Both passed every DOM assertion: the collapse attribute flipped, the media query matched, the canvas matched its container. Cause: a dragged size is session state, and the stylesheet is the spec; nothing reconciles them. Guard: park the dragged width when you collapse and give it back when you expand, so the reader's choice survives the round trip; and in the small-breakpoint block override it outright — width:auto!important — because below that width the drag axis does not exist. Assert both: that a collapse after a drag really reaches zero, and that at phone width scrollWidth <= clientWidth, naming the widest element in the failure message so the next occurrence is one line of output rather than an investigation.

A coverage state authored at low alpha reads as EMPTY GROUND, which is the one thing it exists to prevent. [app] (2026-09-03, transaction evidence) This app covers one county of California's 58 and renders the other 57 NOT COVERED — never as zero, never as blank — using the house convention that a dashed outline says "a statement about data" where a solid fill says "data". Authored at fill alpha 26/255 with a 1.0 px dash, the layer was invisible on a light basemap: 57 counties looked exactly like the ocean. Every assertion passed — the source held 57 features, the dash array was [3,2], the fill colour was the authored one. Cause: an alpha chosen so a wash cannot be mistaken for a measurement can also be an alpha at which nothing is communicated, and no DOM assertion distinguishes the two. Guard: raise the fill until it reads while keeping it below the deepest data class (46/255 here against a deepest band of 110/255) and let the dash carry the semantics, since the dash is what separates the two meanings rather than the alpha. Then assert it in pixels: map.project() a coordinate inside a not-covered polygon, decode the screenshot, and require the sampled luminance to be measurably below bare ground. A coverage layer nobody can see is a coverage claim nobody made.

Three declarative wires that read as obvious and do not work. [core: open] (2026-09-07, valuation & taxation base map) Read out of the shipped dispatchers rather than guessed, and all three would have shipped silently in a connections table that reads as complete:

  • extentChange → panTo between two maps does not sync extents. extentChange carries a bbox and no oids, and panTo re-emits it as a featureSelect. The receiving map sees an empty oids, which the shipped contract defines as a release — so it clears its selection, closes its popup and stops flying. The authored wire actively breaks the far pane.
  • featureSelect → filter clears a filter rather than scoping it. whereFromTrigger has no featureSelect branch and falls through to undefined; the dispatcher then calls setDefinition(layer, undefined), which removes the definition expression.
  • buttonClick has no shipped widget emitter. It is a declared trigger type with its emitters listed as pending, so any connection wired to it never fires.

Guard: verify that every from in a connections table is a shipped emitter and every action a shipped dispatcher before authoring it — a connection that never fires is worse than no connection, because the design reads as finished. Camera lockstep between two maps needs a small map-tool widget reaching both through the MapRegistry; a selection-derived filter needs a widget that re-emits a real filterChange. Both are app-local until the core ships them, and both need a named day-1 fallback so the application is whole either way.

A state-to-colour lookup keyed on strings that are not keys of the palette throws only when the rare row appears — and takes the whole pipeline with it. [app] (2026-09-07, valuation & taxation base map) The map from four record states to their hues used the keys notroll and atfloor. The palette's keys are notOnRoll and atFloor. PALETTE[key][theme] therefore threw Cannot read properties of undefined the moment one gated parcel entered the viewport, and the app's own try/catch around the scope pipeline turned that into "could not read the parcels in view" — no surface, no readout, and nothing in the console a reader would ever see. County-wide the trigger population is 60,785 off-roll rows, 1,181 lots at or under 100 square feet and 124 sentinels: not a corner case. A live suite of 415 assertions, a browser suite of 144 and a screenshot pass had all reported green, because every viewport any of them happened to open on contained only clean parcels. Guard, two parts. Resolve such a lookup through a helper with an explicit fallback, so a missing key is a wrong colour and never an exception — the states are the point of the surface, and losing the surface to protect a hue is the wrong trade. And make the harness fly to a named row of each rare state rather than trusting the viewport it happens to open on: a fixture carrying one row of every state offline, and a navigation to a specific record by id in the browser.

Second occurrence — the pointer path and the keyboard path of the same resize grip disagreed about direction, and a suite that drove both still missed it. [app] (2026-09-07, valuation & taxation base map; first recorded 2026-09-07, development pipeline tracker) Recorded again because the mechanism is identical and it was rediscovered independently in the same week, which says the guard belongs in the harness template rather than in one build's notes. A shared apply(start, dx) resolves which edge a panel's grip faces; the keydown handler applied that sign again before calling it, so the right-hand rail's ArrowLeft narrowed the panel that dragging its grip left widened. What differs from the first recording is what let it through: this build's suite did drive both the pointer and the keyboard path — but only on the left rail, where the double negation cancels and both paths are correct. Guard: drive both gestures on every rail, not on a representative one, and assert the direction rather than that the size changed. A sign that appears in two places is a sign that will be applied twice: resolve it once, inside the helper, and pass that helper a raw pointer delta.

Seven per-style basemap previews were one raster tile repeated, from a host the application's own text calls a placeholder — and not one of them loaded. [app] [browser] (2026-09-07, valuation & taxation base map) The basemap drawer's rows each carried an <img> whose src was built from the current view's tile coordinates: the same URL on all seven rows, in none of the seven styles, pointing at the very raster host this application's own comments name as answering HTTP 200 with an "Access blocked" image. In a browser the number that actually rendered was zero. Three faults in one element, and every non-visual assertion was green — the row count was seven, the markup was right, the arithmetic was right, and the drawer cleared the control cluster. The whole reason the house default asks for a live tile per row is that a colour swatch cannot tell Positron from Voyager — and neither can seven copies of one picture. Guard: assert a preview as a picture — a canvas per row, sized on screen, its bytes read back and compared against its siblings, so "seven previews" and "seven different previews" are separate assertions. Three mechanical notes, each of which cost a run: toDataURL on a GL canvas returns an empty buffer unless the map was created with preserveDrawingBuffer, which is how this very assertion first reported "1 distinct of 7" on seven previews that really were seven different styles; a GL map paints its style's background colour before its tiles arrive, so poll each preview's own loaded() && areTilesLoaded() rather than sleeping a fixed delay; and mounting one live GL map per row is not free. That last one is measured rather than asserted: two probes back to back on a quiet machine, and the same tier-B jump completes in 2.2 s without the previews while the page stops answering CDP entirely with seven of them mounted. Nine simultaneous WebGL contexts — two main maps and seven previews — is more than a heavy page can carry.

What shipped in the end was neither shape, and it is the cheaper answer. Each row's swatch is drawn from that style's own declared ground, land-cover and water colours, read once out of the style JSON through the application's network seam, warmed at boot so opening the drawer never races the page, and rendered as a small inline SVG with no GL context at all. It tells Positron from Dark from Liberty from Colorful — which is the job the house default actually asks of a preview — and it says on screen that it is the style's own colours rather than a picture of the reader's ground. One trap inside it: a style's colour may be a data-driven expression, which is an array and not a string, and passing that through to a fill renders the literal text [object Object]. Take flat colours only and skip the rest.

⚠ A correction to an earlier reading of this same episode, kept because the wrong answer is instructive. The repeated browser-suite aborts around this drawer were not caused by the previews, and not by the screenshots either. A clean run of the shipped design, with screenshots, passes 175/175 with no retries and no missed captures, and a capture measures 0.0–0.1 s with the evaluate behind it at 0.0 s. The aborts came from two Chrome instances driving the same 22 MB application at once. The full account is in §9, a harness that spawns browsers is a load generator.

A GL → GL basemap swap with MapLibre's default style diff requests glyphs from the OLD style's host -- labels 404 and style.load may never fire. [core: fixed] [browser]

Fixed in @strata/map-controls 1.0.2 (2026-10-13) — do not apply the old workaround. Every basemap change is now map.setStyle(style, { diff: false }) — a full swap that rebuilds the style and always fires style.load — once per map (the primary and each of maps). Delete app-side wrappers that patched setStyle to force { diff: false } (or re-setGlyphs after a swap).

(2026-10, map-controls migration) map.setStyle(url) defaults to diff: true: MapLibre tries to patch the old style into the new one. Between two GL styles from different hosts (VersaTiles ↔ CARTO, or a raster style carrying DEFAULT_GLYPHS ↔ a GL style with its own font server) the diff kept the previous glyphs URL, so the new style's label layers asked the wrong host for their fontstacks and 404'd — the basemap drew, its labels did not, and the console showed only a run of glyph 404s. In some swaps the diff succeeded "in place" and style.load never fired, so an app that re-added its layers on style.load lost them. Guard: for a basemap change always setStyle(style, { diff: false }) and re-add app layers on styledata (the package's onRestyle), not on style.load alone; assert in the browser suite that a label layer has rendered symbols after a GL → GL swap.

An unset *-opacity (setPaintProperty(id, prop, undefined)) left a MapLibre 4.7.1 LINE layer drawing nothing. [core: fixed] [browser]

Fixed in @strata/map-controls 1.0.2 (2026-10-13) — do not apply the old workaround. applyLayerState() never writes undefined any more: at 0 % transparency it writes the authored value back (a number, or the authored expression), and an expression opacity is faded as ["*", expr, k] — or, for a zoom interpolate / step, by multiplying its output stops (["zoom"] may only be the input of a top-level interpolate / step, so it cannot be wrapped). Remove the 0.999 "never quite opaque" opacity workaround from the descriptors.

(2026-10, map-controls migration) The layer list used to "reset" a fully opaque sublayer by unsetting the paint property — 1 is MapLibre's default for every *-opacity, so it looked like a no-op. On a line layer in MapLibre 4.7.1 it was not: after setPaintProperty(id, "line-opacity", undefined) the layer kept its features (queryRenderedFeatures found them) and drew no pixels until any other paint change. Apps worked around it by authoring 0.999 so the module never reached 1. Separately, an authored expression opacity (["interpolate", …, ["zoom"], …], ["get", "alpha"]) was multiplied as if it were a number, producing NaN. Guard: never unset a paint property to restore a default — write the value; and treat an authored paint value as possibly an expression everywhere you scale it.

An open drawer hid the map's popup; in dark mode the popup was white-on-white (or bare text with no maplibre-gl.css). [core: fixed] [browser]

Fixed in @strata/map-controls 1.0.3 (2026-10-13) — do not apply the old workaround. The package injects one popup stylesheet: a MapLibre popup inside the container stacks at --smc-popup-z (default calc(var(--smc-z,40) + 2) = 42), above the drawer (40); the cluster and the drawer grip sit at 43, so neither a control nor the grip is ever under a popup. With popupStyles (default on) the card is themed from the --smc-* tokens in light and dark. Delete app popup z-index overrides (calc(var(--smc-z,40) + 2)) and app dark-popup CSS. App modals go at z-index ≥ 50.

(2026-10, thirty vendored apps) The drawer and the cluster shared --smc-z (40); a MapLibre popup has no z-index of its own, so a popup that opened beside a wide drawer slid under it (seen in about eight apps). The vanilla package also had nothing like React's ensurePopupStyles: maplibre-gl.css paints the card white and sets no color, so in a dark app the card inherited near-white text — or, on a page without maplibre-gl.css, the popup was unstyled text at the top-left of the map. Guard: a popup is UI and needs a stacking tier above the panels; check document.elementFromPoint over a popup that overlaps an open drawer in the browser suite. Do not give the map element its own z-index — that makes a stacking context that traps the popup below the drawer. A fixed color on .maplibregl-popup-content written for light mode outranks the themed card and makes dark popups unreadable again.

One Esc closed the app's modal AND the map's popup together. [core: fixed] [app]

Fixed in @strata/map-controls 1.0.3 (2026-10-13). escape() closes one layer per press: the app's closePopup() first — true stops there — then MapLibre popups on the next press, then the tool, then the drawer. closePopups() still closes all. A MapLibre popup may be registered as closePopup; one opened with closeButton: false should be (the package can only remove its element without telling MapLibre). An app modal that must outrank the chrome is checked before controls.escape(). The full-screen Esc guard (escapeGuardMs, 300 ms) is time-based: any Esc in that window is swallowed.

(2026-10, thirty vendored apps) 1.0.2 closed the app's popup and every MapLibre popup in one call, so a reader who pressed Esc to dismiss a confirmation card also lost the feature popup they were reading. MapLibre 4.7.1 also has no map._popups, so the package finds popups in the map container's DOM and presses their close button. Guard: in an offline suite, stub a popup the 4.7.1 way — a .maplibregl-popup with a .maplibregl-popup-close-button inside map.getContainer() — not only on map._popups.

A GL basemap's style, sources and tile hosts are not where a synchronous check looks. [browser] [core: fixed in part]

1.0.3: BASEMAP_HOSTS now admits every host / path the catalog's own GL styles delegate to (VersaTiles assets/sprites/ + tiles/osm/, CARTO's tiles.basemaps.cartocdn.com and tiles-{a-d}.basemaps.cartocdn.com/vectortiles/), checked offline against a recorded copy of each style and live by verify:basemaps; styleDelegateUrls(style) lists what a style delegates to. A GL style that fails to load falls back once to osm / osm-dark (basemapFallback, basemapTimeoutMs, onBasemapFallback). Delete app allow-list extensions and app GL fallbacks.

(2026-10, thirty vendored apps) Three traps around a vt-* basemap. (1) After a swap, map.getStyle() and the sources an app adds are not there synchronously — the style is fetched and parsed first; read it after styledata / style.load (the package's onRestyle), never on the line after setBasemap(). (2) In headless Chrome a vector style can take more than 6 s to paint; a fixed 3 s sleep before a screenshot shows an empty ground — wait for idle with a generous timeout. (3) A GL basemap's tile, glyph and sprite requests run in MapLibre's worker, so they never appear in the page's resource timing; a host check that reads the page's network log sees only the style JSON and passes anything. Guard: read the active style's URLs (styleDelegateUrls(map.getStyle()) plus each source's TileJSON tiles) and test them against BASEMAP_HOSTS.

An app that called refreshLayers() on every idle lost clicks in the layer list. [core: fixed] [app]

Fixed in @strata/map-controls 1.0.3. refreshLayers() re-renders only when the list's HTML changes, so a call that changes nothing leaves the buttons (and a slider drag) under the pointer. Still call it when data changes, not on every styledata / idle.

(2026-10, thirty vendored apps) Each re-render replaced the row buttons; a click whose pointerdown landed on the old button and pointerup on the new one never fired. Guard: re-render a list on a data change, not on a map event; and drawerWidth() is 0 without layout (jsdom) — assert widths in a browser, or after setDrawerWidth(). (2026-10-22, live-position build) The same failure from an app's own one-second tick. A card that rebuilt its innerHTML every second to update fix ages swapped the button under the pointer between mousedown and click, and dropped focus from a <select> the reader was using. Guard: a clock tick patches text in place (per-node textContent, or a small morph); rebuild markup only on a structural change, and assert in the browser that a click and an open select survive a tick.

An app's click delegate catches the vendored map controls' own buttons. [app] (2026-10-22, live-position build) The vendored @strata/map-controls cluster marks its buttons data-act — the same attribute the app's single document-level click delegate dispatched on. Pressing the cluster's Legend button reached the app's legend row case with no dimension: it hid "undefined:undefined", every legend row read 0 of N, and MapLibre was handed a filter with an undefined value (one console error per unit layer) — the map emptied. Guard: the delegate returns early for any target inside the vendored chrome (closest('[class^="smc-"]'), or the controls' container), or the app namespaces its own attribute; assert in the browser suite that pressing each cluster button leaves the app's layers drawn, and negative-test the guard (remove it, watch the assertion fail).

A submit button that is also a click action fires the action twice. [app] (2026-10-22, live-position build) A Connect button was both a case in the app's click delegate and the form's submit button, so one press ran the click case and then the form's submit handler — two reads and two self-re-arming poll chains. Guard: an action belongs to exactly one event: let submit own anything inside a <form> (it covers Enter too), make the click case a no-op for it, and assert in the browser that one press produces exactly one request. Same hazard as §9 a keydown dispatched on both document and window.

A quick theme switch during a style load surfaces MapLibre's AbortError as an uncaught rejection. [browser] (2026-10-22, live-position build) Flipping light ↔ dark while the previous basemap style was still loading left MapLibre's superseded style request rejecting with AbortError — an Uncaught (in promise) in the console that fails a clean-console assertion although nothing is wrong. Guard: in the app's unhandledrejection handler ignore reason.name === "AbortError" (a superseded request, not an error) and let every other rejection through; test with two theme flips inside one style load.

8 · Colour, theme and contrast

Measure the theme; do not argue about it. [app] #f59e0b as text on a white panel is 2.15:1 — under WCAG AA's 4.5 — and it marked the app's biggest finding. Split into a fill token and #b45309 (5.02:1) for text. In another build an amber read 3.77:1 light against 7.12:1 dark and looked like proof the app should be dark — until every other state measured 5.57–8.34:1 and the amber turned out to be the outlier, not the theme. #b06f00 → #8a5700 put it at 5.60:1 and the decision returned to fitness for purpose.

Data colours are identical in both themes. [app] A dark-mode override painted light text onto a light amber fill and made a whole navigation band unreadable. Only the halo changes.

Mix surface tints against the panel, not transparent. [app] On a dark surface a tint mixed against transparent resolves toward the tint itself and produces mud.

Fill opacity must follow the basemap. [app] Alpha tuned for pale CARTO Positron is invisible on OpenTopoMap — the basemap a user picks precisely to see terrain context.

An underlay outline in the class hue is tuned for one ground, and it inverts on the other. [app] (2026-08-17, collateral & portfolio risk) A hazard underlay drew its fill at alpha 40 and its outline in the same hue at alpha 170. On white the fill reads as a pale wash and the outline as a firm edge; on a near-black ground the fill all but disappears while the outline glows, so the layer flips from a wash under the data to a bright cage over it. Worse where the polygons are many: the layer in question was 2,806 CGS quadrangles, and at statewide zoom its edges laid a lattice across the whole state in the same hue as the point symbols beneath it. Every assertion stayed green — a lattice that hides the payload is not a wrong number, and only the dark screenshot showed it. Two guards. Draw an underlay outline only where the edge is a real boundary: a quadrangle, tile or survey-grid layer asserts a line that means nothing, so it draws fill only. And derive the ESRI drawingInfo outline and the renderer's own line-opacity from one helper — as two literals they agree by coincidence, and drift the first time either is tuned.

Semantic roles must not conflate two different facts. [app] "An authority answered" and "an authority answered, and the answer is a hazard" cannot share ink. Painting evidenced in the danger role made a plain factual value draw as a full-width blood-red bar. No assertion could catch it — the ink was internally consistent and semantically inverted.

A network-wide verdict is a false statement about a specific asset. [app] Stamping "unverifiable" on every line because 28.3 % of network mileage is unparseable libels the lines that file a plain value. Decide per feature and print the reason on the row.

A caveat that outlives a toast belongs in a persistent notice, not a status line. [app] A vintage field-rename warning written to a status line was overwritten by load progress and never reached anyone. (2026-08-16, collateral & portfolio risk) The same shape, one step subtler: boot() caught a failing coverage-probe fetch, wrote "not available" to the status line, and then finished by writing its own success message to that same line. The caveat was true for about 200 ms, after which the app read as healthy with a silently missing lane. Guard: a lasting condition goes to the notice bar through a dedicated addNotice() that survives every filter, pan and later status write; the status line carries transient progress only. Assert the caveat in the notice, never in the status line — an assertion that reads the status line will pass on a caveat nobody can see.

A contrast checker that parses a computed colour string is wrong the moment a colour is computed. [browser] (2026-08-20, financial inclusion & coverage) Chrome resolves color-mix(in srgb, var(--warning) 12%, var(--panel)) to color(srgb 0.906 0.886 0.855) — three floats. A checker that reads the first three numbers out of getComputedStyle().backgroundColor as 0–255 channels turned a pale warm notice bar into near-black and reported 1.17:1 on a surface that measures ~14:1; dark mode "passed" by the same error, in reverse. Guard: let the browser do the conversion — paint the colour into a 1×1 canvas over a known base and read the pixel — probe alpha by compositing over black and over white and comparing, rather than parsing a fourth number that may not be there, and composite the whole ancestor stack rather than stopping at the first non-transparent one. A contrast check that is itself wrong is worse than none, because it is believed. The same parsing assumption breaks on oklch(), lab() and rgb(from …), all of which a modern engine may hand back verbatim.

A hand-built popup inherits half a theme just as the shipped one used to. [app] [browser] (2026-08-20, branch & ATM network operations) MapLibre's own CSS paints .maplibregl-popup-content white and sets no color, so an app that styles the popup's text and not its card gets a label grey measuring 3.11:1 on white — in BOTH modes, which is the tell: a ratio identical across a theme switch means the element is not taking the theme at all. On the React path ensurePopupStyles() handles this; a house-pattern app owes the same work. Guard: take the card, the text, the edge and the tip from the theme in one breath — the tip is four per-anchor border colours — and measure the popup in the browser suite, where a first document.querySelector('td') will find it before any page table.

A theme swap must not discard an explicit basemap choice. [app] Re-pair the basemap only while the user has not chosen one; an unknown basemap id falls back rather than blanking the map.

A harness that polls the status line can break out on the app's own initial text. [app] (2026-08-23, property peril report) The browser driver waited until the status line stopped saying loading, and the app's static markup said Ready. — so it broke out immediately and asserted against a page whose module had not yet executed. window.__app was undefined, with no exception and no console error to show for it. Guard: have the app raise an explicit readiness flag at the end of boot() and poll that; never infer readiness from prose that a static page can already be showing.

Order the boot so the fit has something to fit to. [app] (2026-08-23, property peril report) The locator was recentred before the reading was computed, so the app's own measure line did not exist yet and the map opened at a fixed close zoom with the far end of a 2 km measurement off the edge of the screen. Guard: re-run the framing step once the reading exists, and assert in the browser harness that the fitted bounds actually contain both ends of whatever the app's signature draws.

Four states told apart only by hue lose the two that matter. [app] (2026-08-26, humanitarian response map) A board carrying covered, uncovered, not reported and no estimate reads as two states for a reader who cannot separate the hues - and the two it loses are the honesty states, so "nobody reported this" becomes indistinguishable from "nobody is here", which was the one failure that product could not survive. Cause: colour used as the sole channel for a categorical meaning whose categories are not ordered. Guard: give every non-obvious state a second channel - a hatch in the map fill (esriSFSDiagonalCross, esriSFSBackwardDiagonal) and a spelled-out word in the popup, the table and every export - and measure all four against the panel in both modes, treating the measured number as the gate rather than the intention.

A shell rule for button[aria-pressed="true"] repaints a legend row's text to the panel colour — white on white. [browser] [app] (2026-08-26, facility capacity & catchment) Legend rows are buttons carrying aria-pressed so the "hidden / shown" state reaches assistive tech. The shell's own button[aria-pressed="true"] { background: var(--accent); color: var(--panel) } then applied to them. An id-scoped rule overrode the background but not the colour, so every legend label rendered in the panel colour on the panel — invisible. The counts survived, because they carry --muted explicitly, so the legend looked plausible in every DOM assertion and every count was correct. Only the screenshot showed a legend with five swatches, five counts and no words. Guard: when a control doubles as a toggle, re-declare both background and colour in the component's own scope, and have the browser suite assert getComputedStyle(...).color against the ground it sits on rather than merely that the label exists.

The house 40/255 fill alpha exists for OVERLAPPING polygons; on a layer that cannot overlap it just makes the data invisible. [app] (2026-08-26, facility capacity & catchment) A catchment layer is one row per chosen facility × origin ZIP, and ZIP-area polygons tile the plane — exactly one cell covers any point, ever. At 40/255 over a light basemap the whole catchment was a faint haze: the app's own signature, unreadable, with every non-visual assertion green. Raised to 110/255, uniformly across all classes so the lightness ordering and the greyscale reading are unchanged. Guard: the alpha is a default with a reason; check whether the reason applies to your layer before inheriting it, and state the deviation in the recipe and assert it in the suite (all classes share one alpha) rather than letting it drift per class.

Data hues measured against a PANEL can vanish against a dark BASEMAP, and a swatch ring cannot save them. [app] [browser] (2026-08-26, facility capacity & catchment) A five-class ramp measured 11.79 and 5.84 against white and 1.57 and 3.17 against the dark app ground — recorded, and accepted, because the house remedy for a low-contrast fill is a 1 px ring on the swatch. On the map there is no ring: over CARTO Dark Matter the two darkest classes disappeared entirely and dark mode lost the catchment. Guard: keep the rule that a colour means one thing in both modes, and make it true by compositing the class fills over a light plate (a fill layer beneath them at ~0.22 of the light text colour, switched on with the theme). Put the same plate behind the legend swatch so the key and the map agree. Assert the plate's opacity in both modes in the browser suite — the failure is invisible to every other harness.

One accent hex cannot clear 3:1 over BOTH members of a light/dark basemap pair. [app] (2026-08-26, hospital network planning) The house rule is that data colours stay identical in both themes, so a mark means the same thing in either. That rule holds for fills -- but the selection halo is meaningful non-text under WCAG 1.4.11 and has to clear 3.0:1 against the ground it sits on, and that ground swaps with the theme. Measured: #5aa9ff scores 7.70:1 over a dark basemap and 2.35:1 over a light one -- a fail no panel-based contrast table catches, because the halo is never on a panel. The theme spec itself had already implied the answer by quoting the halo as two numbers. Guard: split the rule. Data fills keep one hex in both modes and carry a dark stroke for their boundary, which reads on both grounds; selection, focus and hover affordances that sit over the basemap take a per-mode hex, and the suite recomputes both ratios from the theme file. Assert the negative too -- that a single hex could not have cleared both -- so the pair is not "simplified" away later.

A label drawn over a bar has a ground that is another token, not a surface — so a token-against-surface contrast table cannot see it. [app] (2026-09-03, tenant mix & catchment) Forty measurements of every informational token against both surfaces in both modes passed at a floor of 6.07:1, and the app still shipped dark-on-dark text: the chart's category labels were drawn inside the bars, so each label crossed two grounds — the pale track behind the bar and the saturated fill of the bar itself. No surface measurement covers that, and no non-visual assertion sees it. A phone-width screenshot made it obvious.

Guard: enumerate every ground a token is drawn on, not every surface. Where a label crosses a boundary, draw it twice: one copy on the track and a second copy inside the fill element, whose own overflow: hidden clips it exactly at the bar's end at any width. Give the clipped copy its own token (white in light mode, the app background in dark) and measure that token against each fill — four more measurements, which took this build's set from 40 to 44. In the browser suite, assert that the two copies share a left edge, that the inner one is clipped to the fill, and that their computed colours differ; a single-copy label passes all three trivially only if you forget the third.

9 · Suites and verification

An unencoded /../ is not a traversal test — the browser resolves it before the request is sent. [app] (2026-09-07, facilities & space utilisation) A browser-driven traversal probe fired fetch("/../../server.mjs") and got 200, which looks like a failed guard. It is not: the URL is resolved against the origin in the page, so the server receives /server.mjs, a file that is legitimately inside the served folder. Only percent-encoded segments (%2e%2e%2f, %2e%2e%5c) survive to reach the handler — which is exactly why the guard must decode before it normalises. Worth knowing alongside it: node:path collapses a leading .. past the root, so /%2e%2e%2f%2e%2e%2fpackage.json normalises to /package.json and 404s inside the folder rather than escaping. Guard: test with encoded payloads only, assert the refusal and that a legitimate file is still served, and clear the suite's failed-request tally afterwards — those deliberate 404s otherwise poison the clean-console assertion.

A resize helper shared by a left-docked and a right-docked panel needs a direction, or one of them shrinks when you drag it bigger. [app] (2026-09-07, facilities & space utilisation) Generalising one resizable() across the result card, the map drawer (both right-docked) and the legend (left-docked) inverted the card: the same drag that had widened it now narrowed it, and the arrow keys went with it. This file already records pointer-and-keyboard-disagree-about-direction twice; this is the sibling — panel-and-panel disagree about direction, from one helper with one hard-coded sign. Guard: take the dock edge as a parameter, apply it to the pointer delta and to the key step in the same expression, and assert in the suite that the same intent — "make this bigger" — grows every panel, whichever edge it is docked to.

A DOM stub's dataset must WRITE THROUGH to the attribute, or the harness quietly tests nothing. [app] (2026-09-07, facilities & space utilisation) The app builds its control cluster with b.dataset.ctl = key and the suite reads [data-ctl] — which in a real DOM is the same storage. The stub modelled dataset as a plain object, so the attribute stayed empty and an assertion on the six glyphs' order compared ",,,,," against the expected string. It failed loudly here; the same gap on an assertion phrased as "no row is missing its id" would have passed for the wrong reason. Guard: model dataset as a proxy that mirrors into the attribute map (and have setAttribute mirror back). The house rule stands — the harness accommodates the app, not the reverse — and the corollary is that a stub which silently drops a write is worse than one that throws.

A deliberate Page.navigate aborts every request in flight, and the clean-console guard reads that as a failed request. [app] (2026-09-07, facilities & space utilisation) A browser suite that navigates more than once — to test a deep link, or to load the page at phone width — collects two net::ERR_ABORTED entries from Network.loadingFailed that are entirely its own doing, and the "no failed network request" assertion goes red on a healthy app. Guard: clear the failure tally at each navigation rather than filtering ERR_ABORTED out by error text. Filtering by text would also hide a real abort from the app; clearing at a known, deliberate boundary does not.

A guard can match the app's own sentence FORBIDDING the thing it guards — and stripping comments does not help, because the sentence is user-facing prose the app needs. [app] (2026-09-07, development pipeline tracker) Fourth and fifth occurrences of a trap this file already records twice for proxy and once for createConnection, and the first where comment-stripping is no defence. A check that the app builds no committee-style export document grepped the shipped source for committee and matched its own empty-lane statement — "a generated project cost is the one number an investment committee would act on." A check that no geocoder is called grepped for geocode and matched the coverage table's "six statewide rows geocode into Puerto Rico", a line that exists because the app geocodes nothing and reports the publisher's six bad matches instead. Both guards were "passing" only in the sense that they were failing loudly for the wrong reason. Guard: an honesty check that scans text is testing vocabulary, not behaviour. Assert the behaviour — that the only export entry points are the two named functions and the only modal is the dismissible splash; that no URL matches a real geocoder API shape (findAddressCandidates, GeocodeServer, nominatim, photon) rather than the English word. Where a textual scan is genuinely the only option, scan for API shapes and identifiers, never for the domain noun the app is obliged to discuss.

A status line that reports the app's INTENTION rather than the map's state will confidently describe an empty map. [app] @@STAMP@@ The readout said 540 of 540 jurisdictions drawn while not one polygon was installed, because it counted the jurisdictions whose class passed the legend filter — a fact about application state that stays true when the map layer does not exist. This is the readout that building-apps.md §4 asks for precisely so that a support question becomes a glance, and it was the thing most confidently lying. Guard: derive the readout from the map (map.getLayer(id) present, features rendered), and give it an explicit MAP LAYER NOT INSTALLED state distinct from a count. Refresh it on every render, not only on the events that happen to call it, or it describes a previous state. Then assert in the browser suite that the status text does not report a count while the layer is missing.

The pointer path and the keyboard path of the same control can disagree about direction, and only a harness that drives both will notice. [app] @@STAMP@@ A resize helper resolved which edge a panel's grip faces inside apply(). The pointer handler passed a raw delta and was correct; the keyboard handler applied the same sign itself before calling apply(), so the right-hand rail's ArrowLeft narrowed the panel that dragging left widened. Both paths "worked"; they simply did opposite things. Guard: drive every affordance through both its pointer and its keyboard path in the offline suite, against the same element, and assert the direction rather than only that the size changed. Resolve a sign in exactly one place.

A spreadsheet is a service with worse manners — probe it the same way. [data] (2026-09-07, portfolio & asset register) One published inventory carried 148 completely blank trailing rows inside its row count (a 2.0 % overstatement of the population), one String among 7,367 Integers in a numeric column, and 235 rows already past the date the column is used to filter on. None of that is visible from a header, a column count or a type declaration, and a bulk file invites more trust than a service because it arrives as one artefact with a name and a date. Guard: apply the full seven-step probe to a bulk file exactly as to a service — count the real rows rather than the sheet's, check types row by row rather than from the header, look for duplicates and sentinels, and give every date predicate a lower bound as well as an upper one.

A regex over XML whose attribute group is GREEDY eats the slash of a self-closing tag, falls through to the open-tag branch, and swallows the next element whole. [app] (2026-09-07, portfolio & asset register) A zero-dependency .xlsx reader matched cells with /<c([^>]*)(?:\/>|>([\s\S]*?)<\/c>)/g. On <c r="U2" s="5"/> the greedy [^>]* consumed r="U2" s="5"/, the > branch then matched, and the lazy body ran forward to the next </c> — consuming the neighbouring cell and its value. Every empty cell therefore deleted the cell after it. The file still parsed, every row still had the right column count, the date columns were right, the square-foot total was right to the byte, and the annual-rent total came out at $5,548,683,056 against a true $5,838,396,051: a 5 % error, entirely plausible, with no error anywhere. Guard: make the attribute group lazy — [^>]*? — so the engine tries /> at the earliest position, and apply the same fix to every self-closing tag in the format (<row/>, <xf/>). Then assert a total you already know from another tool before trusting the reader; a shape check would not have caught this.

A guard can match the sentence saying the thing is absent — three times in one build. [app] (2026-09-07, portfolio & asset register) Already recorded in this file for proxy and for createConnection. It happened three more times in a single app, and the pattern is worth stating as a rule rather than as a list of victims. A money guard /rent|value|price|cost/i matched rentable_sf, an area column, and reported a clean estate as carrying money. An identity guard /owner|lessor/i matched the tenure label "1000 — no constraints, private ownership", which is a published classification code the application is required to print. And a popup guard on lessor matched the popup's own disclosure that the asset "carries no lease number and no lessor." Guard: scan field names and data, never prose. Anchor name-level guards on whole words (/^(.*_)?(rent|value|cost)(_.*)?$/), collect the bound field list and test that, and where a claim really is about text, strip the explanatory block first — or assert the behaviour instead. And negative-test every guard: each of these three was written to fail closed and each passed the thing it was meant to catch.

A read date compiled into the source is a lie with a delay, and a suite that freezes a live count goes red on a Tuesday for no reason. [app] (2026-09-07, transaction evidence build, re-run four days after it was verified) An app over a file that gains rows daily exported READ_DATE = "2026-09-03" from its domain module and printed it beside every figure — the status line, the rail, the exported note. Four days later the app fetched 57,524 rows at boot and labelled them read 2026-09-03. Nothing failed: the constant was correct on the day it was written, and the whole product rested on carrying an honest vintage. Its three suites had the mirror of the same defect. Five browser assertions were frozen to counts from that day — 1,237 parcel rows, 1,060 instruments, 239.2 %, 2,000 of 4,726 rows listed — so the suite reported five failures on an app that was behaving correctly, and the reader's next move is to stop believing the suite. The live suite, whose header already said counts must be asserted as shape, was the only one of the three that survived the file moving. Guard, both halves. A provenance date is a fact about the read, so stamp it when the read happens (S.readDate = opts.readDate ?? isoDate()) and let a suite pin it for determinism; keep the date the layer's shape was verified as a separate constant and say which is which on the face of the app. And assert a moving file relationally: ask the service for the count at run time and require the screen to agree with it, derive a percentage from the two numbers printed beside it, and match n of N against the panel's own uncapped N rather than a literal. Both are stronger checks than the frozen ones — they prove the number on screen came from the file rather than from the page.

A getBoundingClientRect check cannot see clipping by an ancestor with overflow, so "is it on screen?" passes on a layout where it is not. [app] (2026-09-03, parcel record coverage) A browser assertion guarded the rule that an app must open on a view where its own signature is visible: it compared each panel block's rect against window.innerHeight. The rect reports where an element would be; an ancestor with overflow:auto clips it without changing that rect by a pixel. So a table scrolled out of its own container reported as on-screen and the assertion passed on the very defect it existed to catch — which the screenshot then showed plainly. Guard: test visibility against the scroll container's box, not the viewport's, and take the screenshot anyway. Rects describe geometry; only pixels describe visibility.

A rule that says "always visible" cannot be delegated to scroll position. [app] (2026-09-03, parcel record coverage) A privacy banner was specified as always visible, never a disclosure behind a click. It was placed at the foot of a scrolling panel body, where at the app's own opening size it sat 910 px down an 845 px viewport — visible only after a gesture, which is what the rule forbids. Guard: a block the design calls permanent belongs outside the scrolling region — pinned to the panel, in its own flex child — and the browser suite asserts its rect is inside the viewport at the default window size, not merely that it is present in the DOM.

A privacy scanner that greps raw response text fires on the protocol's own vocabulary. [app] (2026-09-03, parcel record coverage) A suite recorded every network response and grepped it case-insensitively for twelve excluded owner-identity field names, one of which is NAME. It reported a disclosure. There was none: ArcGIS names its own schema key "name", so {"name":"APN_DASH"} appears in every query response, and one county's displayFieldName is literally SUB_NAME. A suite that cries wolf here is worse than none, because the next real hit gets waved through. Guard: scan what a response carries, not how its text reads — the keys of each feature's attributes, the fields[].name list and fieldAliases — and pair it with a negative control: a deliberate request for the excluded fields that must come back carrying them. A green privacy assertion with no negative control cannot distinguish nothing leaked from the scanner is broken.

A live suite that lets one slow service reject kills the run and reports nothing. [app] (2026-09-03, parcel record coverage) An outStatistics count over 2.4 million rows exceeded a 60-second deadline once in five runs. The rejection propagated out of an unguarded await, Node exited on the unhandled error, and ninety assertions that had already passed were never summarised — the operator saw a stack trace where a report should have been. Guard: a live harness catches its own transport errors, retries a timeout once or twice with a backoff, and converts a persistent one into a red assertion. A suite exists to report; a suite that dies has not reported.

A spec that states its own threshold twice will state it differently. [app] (2026-09-03, parcel record coverage) A design document defined a class as "= 95 % populated" in its state table and, two paragraphs later, "It is 50 %." The two are not interchangeable: at 95 % three cells measured at 90.91 % change class, and the resulting matrix contradicts the same document's own per-county table. Arithmetic settled it — only 50 % reproduces the recorded matrix. Guard: put an authored threshold in one place, in code, and make the suite prove the claim made about it. Here the claim was that the choice is inert because no measured rate falls between 13.86 % and 90.91 %, so the suite rebuilds the whole matrix at 20 %, 80 % and 95 % and asserts that only the last one differs. That is a test of the argument, not of the number.

--use-gl=swiftshader leaves part of the map viewport UNPAINTED — a false visual that looks exactly like a real defect. [browser] (2026-09-02, site selection - the swing slate) A headless screenshot showed a clean rectangle of bare container background inside the map — 16-17 % of the viewport, hard-edged, present in both themes. It survived a forced resize() and triggerRepaint(); areTilesLoaded(), loaded() and isMoving() all reported healthy; elementFromPoint returned the canvas at its full correct size; and the style declared a background layer that should have painted everywhere. It is the software rasteriser. Measured across three renderers on the same page: --use-gl=swiftshader 17.2 % bare, --use-angle=swiftshader 1.3 %, real GPU 1.3 %. Note that --disable-gpu overrides --use-angle and puts you back on the bad path. Guard: drive with --use-angle=swiftshader --enable-unsafe-swiftshader and no --disable-gpu, so that a screenshot you judge is worth judging. And because a genuine version of this symptom is invisible to every DOM assertion, decode the screenshot inside the harness and assert that the map paints its viewport: sample the map's box and require that almost none of it equals the container's own background colour. A PNG reader is about forty lines against node:zlib.

Assert the product's CLAIM, not the code's behaviour — a solver can satisfy its own predicate and still be wrong. [app] (2026-09-02, site selection - the swing slate) A sensitivity solver searched for the smallest weight change making score_b >= score_a, which is the published form of the method, and its own unit tests passed. The live suite instead asserted the claim the app prints — "applying this change reverses this pair" — and caught it at 3 of 4: two candidates sharing a nearest measurement carry identical normalised inputs, so driving that weight to its limit makes the two scores exactly equal. The pair ties; it does not reverse, and the chip promised a reversal the user would never observe. Guard: where a tool prints a promise about what will happen, the suite must apply the change and check the promise, not check that the solver satisfied its inequality. Pair it with the negative: assert that half the predicted change does not produce the effect, which is what makes the reported margin a threshold rather than a label. Use a strict > with a tolerance where a boundary case means equality, and report "no change achieves it" rather than reporting the boundary.

@strata/export composes a map, a single feature report and an atlas — but not a multi-row register. [core: open] (2026-09-02, land bank & acquisition screening) composePrintHtml takes a map image plus pre-built legend HTML; featureReportHtml handles one feature; composeAtlasHtml gives one page per feature. A "tray of findings" document — a screening memo, a disclosure sheet, an evidence list — is none of those, so it is app-local and reuses legendHtml / scalebarSvg / printLayoutCss.

A candidate for core: a composeRegisterHtml(rows, scope) primitive. Those three documents are the same shape — an unordered list of rows, each with a verdict, a source and a vintage, under a scope block — and three separate builds have now written it by hand.

A browser harness that queries a layer before it exists manufactures the very errors it is about to assert are absent. [app] (2026-09-02, land bank & acquisition screening) Once the app accumulated map.on("error") into state so the suite could assert it empty, the assertion failed — with five copies of "The layer 'counties-fill' does not exist in the map's style and cannot be queried for features." The app had not erred. The harness had: its boot-poll and its paint-settle loop both called queryRenderedFeatures on the app's layers during boot and during a setStyle, when those layers are legitimately absent.

Guard: if (!map.getLayer(id)) continue; in every polling helper. A harness that reads state has to be at least as careful as the app it is judging, or its own noise becomes the finding.

Comparing queryRenderedFeatures counts across an interaction requires the camera to be identical. [app] (2026-09-02, land bank & acquisition screening) An assertion that a legend click removes features from the map compared 237 before with 288 after, and then 8 after clearing the filter — all while the filter was working perfectly. queryRenderedFeatures reports what is on screen, and the previous section had left the map mid-flight from a fitBounds; a "wait until the count is stable" loop happily locks onto a plateau during an animation.

Guard: jumpTo a fixed camera before each reading, then wait on !isMoving() && !isZooming() && areTilesLoaded() before counting. And assert the source data changed as well as the render — the source proves the filter is a real definition change, the render proves it reached the screen.

A guard can match the hostname of the thing it is guarding. [app] (2026-08-24, health equity overlay) An assertion enforcing "no bound service is on the 2020 tract vintage" tested /CES5|SVI|2020/i against each service URL. It failed — on services5.arcgis.com, the host of the 2010 geometry spine, because the host services5.arcgis.com contains the substring ces5. This is the existing a guard can match the file it guards trap wearing a hostname. Guard: match whole path segments (/(^|[/_-])(CES5|SVI|2020)([/_-]|$)/) and test new URL(u).pathname, never the whole URL; and prefer asserting a property of the data over a pattern in a string wherever one exists.

Rewrite a "never mentions X" guard as behaviour the moment X becomes legitimate anywhere. [app] (2026-08-24, health equity overlay) A rule said a coded field "may never become a filter, a symbology class or a gate", and the suite enforced it by grepping the source for the field name. Once the field's code book became retrievable the field legitimately appeared in the record pane and in the export, and the textual guard went red on correct code. The replacement asserts the narrow role instead: flipping the flag between its two measured codes changes nothing the scope predicate selects, the compiled map filter never names it, and the renderer never keys on it — while one assertion confirms the single thing it is allowed to decide. Guard: a prohibition expressed as a string search expires; expressed as "this input cannot change that output", it does not.

A DOM stub that appends text to its parent loses every interleaved text run. [app] (2026-08-24, health equity overlay) The stack-based parser wrote character data to stack.at(-1).text, so showing all <b>50</b> tracts produced children [<b>] and a parent whose own text was discarded — textContent read "50". Every assertion about a sentence with a bold number in it — which is most of an honesty surface — was testing the wrong string. Guard: make text a child node (#text) like any other, so document order is preserved, and unit-test the parser itself on interleaved markup before trusting it to test the app.

A stub that defaults every element to hidden:false opens the app's drawers for it. [app] (2026-08-24, health equity overlay) Bare boolean attributes in the shipped markup (<div id="drawer" hidden>) were not reflected into the stub, so the drawer, the table window and the method panel all started open. The first Esc closed a drawer nobody had opened, and the Table button closed the table. Both read as app defects. Guard: reflect bare boolean attributes from the parsed markup (this.hidden = "hidden" in attrs) — the initial state in the HTML is real state, and a harness that invents a different one tests a different app.

The three-harness standard and its rationale live in guide/building-apps.md §3; the manual browser checklist is maintainers/testing/browser-smoke-test.md. The traps:

A markup-grepping suite cannot see whether the app runs. [app] A duplicate const introduced a SyntaxError, so the inline module never executed and the page rendered as a blank shell — and the offline suite found every string it expected and reported 99/99 green. Guard: node --check the shipped module before asserting anything about it, and negative-test that guard — inject a duplicate const and confirm the check fails and names the line. A guard nobody has watched fail is a guard nobody knows works.

A suite that greps markup and asserts numbers cannot see what a screen means. [app] 297 passing assertions missed four defects that one screenshot made obvious — all semantic or spatial.

A backslash escape inside a template literal never reaches the browser. [app] (2026-08-17, concentration & exposure analyzer) A CDP driver passes its expression as a JS template literal, so a lone \s in evaluate(… .replace(/\s+/g, " ") …) is consumed by the literal and the browser evaluates /s+/g — which deletes every letter s from the text under test. The assertion failed against "expo ure", which reads like an app defect and is not one; worse, the same silent mangling makes a passing assertion meaningless whenever the pattern is used to normalise rather than to match. Guard: double every backslash inside a driver template literal, and prefer sending a plain function body over building patterns and selectors by interpolation.

A trap asserted as PRESENT can stop reproducing — and that is a finding, not a failure. [app] (2026-08-17, concentration & exposure analyzer) A recipe recorded a host that answered 403 to Mozilla/5.0 and 200 to curl — a User-Agent WAF verdict, and the reason its first 58-request pull returned 0 of 58. Re-probed on the build date from a different network, all four User-Agents returned 200. The guard in the build script (send a non-browser User-Agent) costs nothing and stays; the assertion must not hard-code the 403, or the suite goes red for a service that got better. Guard: assert a trap in the form "this still behaves as recorded, or it has changed and here is how", record the re-verification date, and never quietly delete the workaround — an intermittent WAF verdict is not a repealed one.

Second instance, and this one had been recorded as a hard rejection: (2026-08-20, fraud / AML geo-dashboard) A recipe rejected federalregister.gov's own raw_text_url, body_html_url and full_text_xml_url outright, because on 18 August all three answered a byte-identical CAPTCHA page at HTTP 200 while the metadata API that handed out those URLs worked fine. Re-probed twice per URL two days later, all three serve the document. The build was unaffected — it had already taken the text from a second publisher (govinfo) at build time — which is the actual lesson: prefer a second publisher over waiting for a wall to lift, and write the suite so the day it lifts is reported as a change rather than asserted as though it still held.

"Visible" means visible to a reader — check the clipping ancestors, not just the window. [app] (2026-08-12, broadband gap matrix) A browser-suite visibility helper compared getBoundingClientRect() against the viewport only, so an element scrolled out of its own overflow:auto container measured as visible. The app opened with the matrix band 30 px too short and its amber "nobody measured" row — the entire finding — below the fold, and the assertion written specifically to catch that passed. The screenshot did not. Guard: walk every scrolling ancestor and intersect; and where a region must fit its content, size it from the content's measured height rather than an authored constant, because a constant cannot know how tall the rows render at the reader's font size.

An "offline" suite is only offline if EVERY call routes through the injectable seam. [app] One function reached for the module-level net instead of the injected one, so the offline harness silently geocoded against the live internet — it returned a real place for a synthetic fixture, which is the only reason anyone noticed. A suite that quietly depends on the network is not deterministic and its green is worth nothing. Guard: hold the seam in state (S.net), never a module-level default, and assert in the offline suite that a synthetic fixture produces synthetic answers. (nearby)

Assert behaviour, not prose — and strip comments before you scan. [app] A keyless-basemap guard grepped server.mjs for proxy and matched the file's own explanatory comment. It happened again, in a later build, to a guard checking the same file for createConnection — which appeared only in the sentence declaring that the server does not use one. Assert that the server opens no sockets, and strip comments from the source before any textual scan. Twice is a pattern: a doc entry does not prevent this, the assertion does. (pipeline, nearby)

A guard can match the DISCLAIMER that denies it. [app] (2026-08-20, fraud / AML geo-dashboard) The entry above is about scanning source; this is the same shape one level out, on rendered text, and it is worse because the sentence that matches is one you were right to write. An app forbidden from showing a customer risk score says so in its notice bar — "not a customer risk score" — and the boundary assertion scanning the rendered page matched that. Its exported case pack ends "No person, account number, card number or street address appears in this document", and the export's privacy assertion matched that. Both guards were checking exactly the right thing and both were reading the app's own denial as the offence. Guard: strip the disclaimer before the scan — remove not a/an/the X clauses from page text, and cut the known footer out of an exported document — then scan what is left; and keep the positive assertion (that the disclaimer is present) as a separate line, so removing the disclaimer cannot silently make the negative one pass.

A DOM stub that parses <script> as markup makes the app's own source part of the "rendered page". [app] (2026-08-20, fraud / AML geo-dashboard) An offline harness mounts the page's real <body> — which contains the inline module. A stack-based parser that treats <script> like any other element turns every '<div class="rc-big" id="rc-rate">' inside a JS string literal into a real node in the stub's tree, and every // comment into page text. getElementById("rc-rate") then finds an element the app never rendered, and a boundary scan of body.textContent reads the source code — so two guards failed against the comment explaining the code they were guarding. Guard: <script> and <style> are raw text: consume to the close tag without parsing, and exclude them from textContent. Then assert an element that only exists in one render state is genuinely absent in the others.

queryRenderedFeatures answers about the LAST PAINT, not about the state you just set. [app] (2026-08-20, fraud / AML geo-dashboard) setFilter, setData and a population swap all take effect on the next frame. A browser assertion that sets state and queries immediately reads the previous frame, so "the legend isolate did not filter the map", "the empty lane still draws cells" and "the cells are gone after the theme swap" were all the harness judging a stale paint — three plausible app defects, none real, each costing a diagnosis. Guard: settle on the map's own idle event (with a timeout) before every rendered-feature assertion; do not substitute a longer sleep, which is the same bug with a wider window.

A build that re-pulls a live register cannot claim byte-reproducibility. [app] (2026-08-23, fraud / AML geo-dashboard) A seeded generator is only reproducible given the same inputs, and one of its inputs was a live federal register the build re-fetched on every run — and hard-asserted: if (estate.length !== 5384) die(...). Three days after the reproducibility test passed byte-for-byte, the register republished (5,384 → 5,381 offices, index locations_20260814090007 → locations_20260821090006) and the same test failed, along with the build itself, for a service that had done nothing wrong. The seed was never the problem: the snapshot was an undeclared input pretending to be a constant. Guard: cache the raw response beside the other frozen inputs (build-cache/) and reuse it, so a rebuild is reproducible by construction and taking a newer snapshot is a deliberate act (delete the file); make the build report drift against the recorded figure and stop only on a material move; and have the suite compare the live register against what provenance records, never against a number written in the test. Then rebuild on the new snapshot when you mean to, and re-date the whole artefact — every generated figure moves with it.

A fixed sleep is not a wait — and a slow host reads exactly like a broken one. [app] (2026-08-20, fraud / AML geo-dashboard) A basemap thumbnail assertion waited 2.5 s and reported 4 of 5 loaded. The fifth was OpenTopoMap, which answers a tile in 2.7–4.5 s from this network — measured, not guessed. A 404 and a slow 200 are indistinguishable to a one-shot check, and the failure reads as a defect the app caused. Guard: poll to a deadline rather than sampling after a guessed interval, and when a host is simply slow, record the measured latency in the app's honest limits instead of tightening the test until it passes.

A retry added to the app does not cover the suite's own probes. [app] (2026-08-12, broadband gap matrix) After a long run was killed by one transient timeout, a bounded retry went into the domain's fetch helper — and the next run died at the first request with UND_ERR_CONNECT_TIMEOUT, because the suite's own raw()/j() helpers called fetch directly and bypassed it entirely. The host was fine: five probes answered 200, but its connect latency swings 0.3–3.7 s against undici's 10 s default. Guard: when you add resilience, enumerate every path that talks to the network — the domain, the suite's probes, and the CORS/preflight checks — and fix them together. A partial fix reads as a fix and fails in a new place.

The harness accommodates the app, not the reverse. [app] An id-only DOM scan broke on ordinary descendant selectors; a flat tag-only parse returned "" from textContent so a passing app read as a failure. Both were replaced with one stack-based parser used for the body and every dynamic innerHTML. The harness must also mount the page's real <body> and model window as the global object.

The MapLibre stub must be faithful where the bugs live. [app] setStyle fires styledata synchronously and then drops every source and layer; a finished render fires idle, so idle → hydrate → paint → setData → idle is a real loop.

Derive counts from state; never hardcode them. [app] A hardcoded constant meant adding a row silently moved the number on the splash screen — and a test asserting the constant was itself wrong.

Regex word boundaries are not semantic boundaries. [app] A role-tagger matched \bfass\b against CalifAssemSenDists. (2026-08-16, collateral & portfolio risk) And an unanchored one is worse: a guard asserting an app ships no scenario control searched for horizon and matched horizontal in the resize helper, reporting a control that does not exist. In the same suite a guard for EADDRINUSE scanned the server source after stripping string literals, so the identifier it was looking for had been removed by its own preprocessing and the guard could never pass. Guard: anchor with \b, and where a behaviour can be exercised, exercise it — the port check became "start two servers on one port and assert the second steps past", which cannot be fooled by either mistake.

A CDP screenshot of a WebGL canvas can be stale in a way the canvas itself is not. [browser] [app] (2026-08-17, climate-risk regulatory reporting) Headless Chrome with --disable-gpu composites a WebGL canvas into Page.captureScreenshot from a stale surface. A rectangle covering a third of the map came out blank in every screenshot while an in-page getImageData of the same rect read mean 196, min 74, max 254 — fully painted. Because the house rule is that the first paint is confirmed by a screenshot, this sent an hour-long search for a rendering bug that did not exist. Guard: read the map's own pixels from inside the page (drawImage into a 2D canvas, then getImageData) when asserting that something is painted; and for the screenshots a human will actually look at, rasterise in-process (--use-angle=swiftshader --enable-unsafe-swiftshader --in-process-gpu, dropping --disable-gpu) and force a frame plus two requestAnimationFrames before capturing. A harness that sometimes lies is worse than one that fails.

A flat canvas cell is not a blank one — open water is legitimately one colour. [app] (2026-08-17, climate-risk regulatory reporting) A pixel guard that required variation in every cell of a grid over the map failed on four cells of open Pacific, which CARTO Positron paints as a single uniform colour. Loosening it to "flat and paper-white" keeps the defect it was written for — the page showing through where nothing painted — without failing on the sea. Guard: where a visual property can be legitimately uniform, assert the combination that only a defect produces, and carry the real coverage claim on a check a palette cannot fool (project known places, require a feature rendered at each).

A fixed sleep after boot makes a browser suite flap. [app] (2026-08-17, climate-risk regulatory reporting) 629,000 vertices of published geometry take a variable time to tile. One run reported "no layer" on every operational layer and five 0×0 basemap thumbnails; a re-run of identical code passed 146/146. Guard: wait on conditions, never on a clock — style loaded, layers added, a non-zero rendered count — and where a third-party demo tile server is involved, assert the pair the app itself uses and report the rest rather than failing on somebody else's latency.

The time-player silhouette's own signature element is not in the widget registry. [core: open] (2026-08-30, ATM cash & service routing) TimeSlider ships as a control in @strata/core-map and as a plugin, and it is tested — but defaultWidgetRegistry (react/app/registry.ts) has no time-slider entry, and map.controls has no time flag either. Verified against strata/packages on this date: the registry carries date-filter and not time-slider. So a recipe assigned the time-player silhouette cannot author its dominant element in a JSON AppLayout — it must register the control app-locally via mergeRegistry, or fall back to date-filter and lose the motion, which is the one thing that silhouette is chosen for. Guard: before assigning time-player, decide which of the two you are shipping and say so in the recipe. Proposed fix: add "time-slider": TimeSlider to the registry and a map.controls.timeSlider flag — the component and its tests already exist, so this is a registry line rather than a build.

kpi.delta is a static prop that the live stat path never computes. [core: open] (2026-08-30, ATM cash & service routing) KpiCard computes value from source + stat and takes delta / deltaLabel as plain numbers passed in by the author — verified in react/widgets/KpiCard.tsx on this date. Any design whose headline is a change — proposed vs committed, this quarter vs last, before vs after — therefore either ships two cards and makes the reader subtract, or writes a widget. For the one number an app exists to publish, making the reader subtract is a real loss. Same class as the already-recorded stacked-bar.series gap: a widget with a data-shaped prop and no source path. Guard: when a headline is a delta, check the widget actually derives it before authoring it. Proposed fix: an optional deltaStat: { field, op, where } computed from the same source.

A liability guard that bans a word also bans its denial. [app] (2026-08-17, climate-risk regulatory reporting) A suite asserting the words "compliant", "approved", "certified" appear nowhere went red on the app's own disclaimer — "nothing here is reviewed, approved or filed", which is the sentence that makes the app safe to hand to anyone. Guard: assert the word never appears as a claim — check each occurrence sits inside a negation — and separately assert the denial is present. Banning the string bans the safety statement along with the danger.

A test can assert its own mistake, and pass for years. [app] (2026-08-17, climate-risk regulatory reporting) Two assertions in one suite were wrong rather than the app: one expected an "evidenced" count to move when a source was blinded that the evidenced item does not cite; another asserted a layer was rendering that the current view deliberately hides, and passed only on the runs where a deep link had not yet applied. Both looked like app defects for as long as it took to read what the app actually binds. Guard: when an assertion fails, check what the app is supposed to do before changing it — and prefer assertions that read the app's own state (which layers are active now) over ones that hard-code a name.

A noisy console is where a real error goes to hide. [app] Fix the favicon 404. A dropped third-party basemap tile is a network event, not a defect: filtering console errors to script errors plus same-origin resource failures stops a CDN hiccup flapping the suite while still failing on a file the app itself is missing.

timer → refresh is declared and never fires. [core: open] (2026-08-18, branch & ATM network operations) A {"trigger":"timer","action":"refresh"} connection is accepted by the schema and does nothing. The trigger type, TimerSource and the refresh dispatcher all ship, but <StrataApp> does not mount a TimerSource from a timer connection and does not supply the onRefresh callback.
